Near-Optimal Generalized Private Testing Thanks: 1 Institute of Science and Technology Austria (ISTA), Klosterneuburg, Austria. 1 { anamay.chaturvedi , monika.henzinger }@ist.ac.at. Thanks: 2 Rutgers University, USA. jalaj.upadhyay@rutgers.edu .
Anamay Chaturvedi 1, Monika Henzinger 1, Jalaj Upadhyay 2

Abstract
. One of the most well-studied problems in differential privacy (DP) is private threshold testing, in which there is a sequence of real values ( v t ) t ≥ 1 (v_{t})_{t\geq 1} , and the goal is to identify the first value which exceeds a given threshold τ \tau . The sparse vector technique is a privacy accounting technique that addresses this problem under the promise that the values have bounded Lipschitz sensitivity, and it is ubiquitous in the DP literature. In this paper, we introduce new algorithms, lower bounds and applications for the generalized version of this problem. The generalized private testing problem was introduced by Liu and Talwar (STOC 2019). There is a private dataset X X , and a sequence of black-box ε t \varepsilon_{t} -DP mechanisms ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} taking values in { + 1 , − 1 } \{+1,-1\} . The goal is to identify the first mechanism whose success probability p t = Pr [ ℳ t ( X ) = + 1 ] p_{t}=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X)=+1] exceeds a given acceptance threshold p ∗ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p^{*}\in(0,1) , while achieving an ε \varepsilon -DP guarantee. In other words, the Lipschitz requirement on real-valued inputs in the standard setting is replaced by a stability guarantee in terms of DP itself. The accuracy of a solution for this problem can be characterized by the gap between the acceptance threshold p ∗ p^{*} , and a corresponding rejection threshold p ¯ \bar{p} , such that with probability 1 − β 1-\beta for all t ≥ 1 t\geq 1 , if p t ≤ p ¯ p_{t}\leq\bar{p} , then the mechanism ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} is rejected, and if p t ≥ p ∗ p_{t}\geq p^{*} , it is accepted. In this paper, we introduce a new DP mechanism called the Generalized Thresholding Mechanism (GTM). For any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 and any sequence of approximate ( ε t , δ t ) (\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t}) -DP input mechanisms ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} , the GTM achieves a pure ε \varepsilon -DP guarantee. For any θ > 0 \theta>0 , γ ∈ ( 1 , 2 ] \gamma\in(1,2] , and β ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \beta\in(0,1) , it achieves a rejection threshold of max ⁡ ( p ∗ / γ ​ Λ t , 1 − γ ​ Λ t ​ ( 1 − p ∗ ) ) − δ t / ε t \max(p^{*}/\gamma\Lambda_{t},1-\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}))-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t} for Λ t = ( 5 ​ t ​ ln 3 ⁡ ( t + 2 ) ) ( 2 + θ ) ​ ε t / ε ​ ( 4 / β ) ( 3 + θ + 2 / θ ) ​ ε t / ε \Lambda_{t}=(5t\ln^{3}(t+2))^{(2+\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}(4/\beta)^{(3+\theta+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon} . With probability 1 − β 1-\beta , the number of evaluations of ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} is at most O ⁡ ( ( ln ⁡ ( t / β ) / ( γ − 1 ) 2 ) ⋅ max ⁡ ( Λ t / p ∗ , ( 1 − p ∗ ) − 1 ) ) O\!\left((\ln(t/\beta)/(\gamma-1)^{2})\cdot\max\!\left(\Lambda_{t}/p^{*},\;(1-p^{*})^{-1}\right)\right) for all t ≥ 1 t\geq 1 . We also prove lower bounds which show near-optimality of our accuracy and sample complexity guarantees. We illustrate the power of the GTM by giving a black-box reduction for private optimization from the continual observation (CO) setting to the batch setting. This gives us the first algorithms for a large class of maximization problems in the CO setting. Our mechanism permits an adaptive choice of acceptance thresholds ( p t ∗ ) t ≥ 1 (p^{*}_{t})_{t\geq 1} (replacing a uniform choice of p ∗ p^{*} with an adaptive choice of p t ∗ p^{*}_{t} ). This functionality addresses in part a challenge mentioned in prior work on using generalized private testing for hyperparameter optimization (Papernot and Steinke (ICLR 2022)).



1.
Introduction

Two fundamental problems in the study of differential privacy (DP) are
private selection
and
private testing
. The private selection problem asks the analyst to pick the best item from a given ground set based on a
score function
. The private testing problem asks the analyst to pick the first item from a given stream whose score exceeds a given target score. These problems are well understood when the function is known to have bounded Lipschitz sensitivity, and their algorithmic solutions, the
exponential mechanism

23
and the
above threshold mechanism

10
, are some of the most widely used mechanisms in the design of DP mechanisms.

A fundamental limitation to what can be achieved for private selection and testing for worst-case inputs is the requirement that the score functions have known bounded global Lipschitz sensitivity over the dataset space. In seminal work, Liu and Talwar
21
consider a generalized formulation of these problems: a sequence of
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP mechanisms,
(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{1\leq t\leq T}
, is given as input (in black-box manner), such that each mechanism’s outputs are items in the ground set along with associated scores (in selection), or a score for the mechanism itself (in testing). The analyst generates an output which is an item in the selection problem and accept/reject in the testing problem. This generalization relaxes the Lipschitz requirement on the score function to distributional stability for a randomized mechanism in the sense of DP itself. Liu and Talwar proposed mechanisms that evaluate every mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}
multiple times and output a value computed from the aggregate of evaluations, but incur privacy loss that is
invariant
in the number of evaluations. This approach offers significantly better privacy-utility trade-offs than using composition theorems
11
, especially for tasks like private hyperparameter optimization.

Mechanisms for generalized private selection have been studied and applied extensively in the literature. Following the work of Liu and Talwar
21
, Papernot and Steinke
26
, and Cohen, Lyu, Nelson, Sarlós, and Stemmer
4
gave improved mechanisms that for
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP inputs,
(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{1\leq t\leq T}
, achieved privacy loss of
(2+\theta)\varepsilon_{1}
for any fixed
\theta>0
. A lower bound argument in
(
21
, Appendix D)
indicates that under a reasonable notion of accuracy, this is the best privacy guarantee achievable.

In this paper, we study generalized private testing. Formally, we are given a private dataset
X
drawn from a dataset universe
\mathcal{X}
equipped with an arbitrary symmetric notion of adjacency, a target success probability
p^{*}\in(0,1)
, and a public sequence of black-box
\varepsilon_{t}
-DP mechanisms
(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
with range
\{-1,+1\}
such that
p_{t}=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X)=+1]
.
The goal of generalized private testing is that the mechanism should correctly halt and output the first mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}
for which
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
, while achieving an
\varepsilon
-DP guarantee. The accuracy of a mechanism for generalized private testing is described in terms of a
rejection threshold

\bar{p}<p^{*}
, such that, with high probability for all
t\geq 1
, if
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}
then the mechanism does not halt, and if for some
t\geq 1
,
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
, it halts. For
p_{t}\in(\bar{p},p^{*})
, the mechanism may halt or continue arbitrarily. Ideally, we would like
\bar{p}
to be as close to
p^{*}
as possible.
21
gave a
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP mechanism that achieves
\bar{p}\approx p^{*}(\beta^{2}/T)^{12\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon
and
\delta
are the privacy loss parameters of the testing mechanism,
\beta
is a given failure probability, and
T
is the number of input mechanisms.

4
showed how to unify the generalized private selection and testing problems into one framework by introducing
correlated random dropping
.
Ghazi, Kamath, Knop, Kumar, Manurangsi and Zhang
13

gave similar algorithms for generalized private selection and testing but with new accuracy guarantees for selection in the
ex-post setting

28
. In contrast with
21
, both of these works provide a pure DP mechanism when the input mechanisms are also pure DP, and an approximate DP mechanism for approximate DP input mechanisms. However, while the testing mechanism in
21
can achieve any output privacy loss parameter
\varepsilon
regardless of the input privacy loss parameter
\varepsilon_{1}
,
4
;
13
require that
\varepsilon>2\varepsilon_{1}
and do not state an accuracy guarantee along the lines of
21
for generalized testing.

We show in
Section

9.3
that approaches based on correlated random dropping
cannot
achieve a rejection threshold better than
\bar{p}\approx p^{*}(\beta/T)
; this is asymptotically
worse
than
21
. To be more precise, regardless of the choice of privacy parameters,
p^{*}/\bar{p}
increases with
T/\beta
. In contrast, for the mechanism of
21
,
p^{*}/\bar{p}
can be reduced to
(T/\beta)^{c}
for any arbitrarily small constant
c
.

To summarize, existing mechanisms for generalized private testing have

(1)

either a large constant factor coefficient in the exponent of
\beta/T
in
\bar{p}
(i.e,
\bar{p}\approx p^{*}(\beta^{2}/T)^{12\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
), restrictive privacy analyses (i.e., achieving only approximate privacy and only for pure DP inputs), and a large number of evaluations of each mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}

21
; or

(2)

tight privacy guarantees, but with an accuracy that scales poorly with
T/\beta
, regardless of the choice of
\varepsilon

(
4
)
.

Further, while mechanisms for generalized private selection are used extensively, mechanisms for generalized private testing have not seen many applications. In this paper, we address these gaps by introducing new algorithms, lower bounds, and applications for generalized private testing.

The rest of this section is organized as follows. We give a formal problem statement in
Section

1.1
followed by our contributions in
Section

1.2
, applications in
Section

1.3
, a high-level technical overview of our proof in
Section

1.4
, and practical implications of our mechanism in
Section

1.5
.

1.1.
Problem description

Let
\mathcal{X}
denote the dataset universe, equipped with a fixed symmetric adjacency relation; we call
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}

neighboring
if they are adjacent.
All results hold for any choice of adjacency relation. We consider a sequence of binary mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{t}:\mathcal{X}\to\{-1,+1\}
for
t=1,2,\ldots
, where each
\mathcal{M}_{t}
satisfies
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-differential privacy, i.e. for all neighboring
X,X^{\prime}
and all
v\in\{-1,+1\}
,

\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X)=v]\leq e^{\varepsilon_{t}}\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X^{\prime})=v]+\delta_{t}.

We also consider a sequence of datasets
(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and privacy parameters
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})_{t\geq 1}
, which, along with the mechanisms
(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
, may be chosen adaptively based on prior public outputs, subject only to the constraint that
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP.
We write
p_{t}:=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})=+1]
for the success probability at step
t
.

A
direction

s_{t}\in\{-1,+1\}
and a
target threshold

p^{*}_{t}\in(0,1)

are associated with each step.
The
target regime
at step
t
is

\{p_{t}:s_{t}\,p_{t}\geq s_{t}\,p^{*}_{t}\}
. In other words, when
s_{t}=+1
, then we want a tester to halt when
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, and when
s_{t}=-1
, we want the tester to halt when
p_{t}\leq p^{*}_{t}
.

Definition 1

(Generalized private tester)
.

Fix a global privacy budget
\varepsilon>0
.
A
generalized private tester
is a randomized procedure that, at each step
t
, interacts with the dataset
X_{t}
only through i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
.
It draws a (potentially random) number of independent evaluations, observes their outcomes, and outputs
a_{t}\in\{-s_{t},s_{t}\}
, where
a_{t}=s_{t}
denotes
halting
and
a_{t}=-s_{t}
denotes
continuation
.
The
transcript
is
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{T})
where
T
is the halting step (
a_{T}=s_{T}
,
a_{t}=-s_{t}
for
t<T
), or the infinite sequence
(-s_{1},-s_{2},\ldots)
if the tester never halts.
The tester must be
\varepsilon
-DP, i.e. for every
t\geq 1
, every pair of dataset sequences
(X_{j})_{j\geq 1}
,
(X^{\prime}_{j})_{j\geq 1}
with
X_{j},X^{\prime}_{j}
neighboring for all
j
,
and every measurable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\{-1,+1\}^{t}
,

\Pr\!\big[(a_{1},\cdots,a_{t})\in\mathcal{E}\big]\;\leq\;e^{\varepsilon}\,\Pr\!\big[(a_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,a_{t}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}\big].

Definition 2

(Accuracy and sample complexity)
.

We say that a generalized private tester when run with a failure probability of
\beta
and target thresholds
(p^{*}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
achieves
rejection thresholds

(\bar{p}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
if with probability
1-\beta
for all
t\geq 1
,
s_{t}p_{t}\leq s_{t}\bar{p}_{t}\Rightarrow a_{t}=-s_{t}
(no false positives), and
s_{t}p_{t}\geq s_{t}p^{*}_{t}\Rightarrow a_{t}=s_{t}
(no false negatives). The
sample complexity
of a generalized private tester is the number of times it evaluates
\mathcal{M}_{t}
on the dataset
X_{t}
.

This problem setting is a mild reformulation of the version introduced by
21
. In prior work, the notion of
\beta
-global accuracy was considered only by
21
, and all work implicitly set
s_{t}=+1
for all
t\geq 1
, i.e. the
Above Threshold
formulation.

1.2.
Our contributions.

Our main contribution is a new mechanism for generalized private testing.

Theorem 3

(Upper Bound, Informal version of
Theorem

21
and
25
)
.

Given a sequence of target thresholds
(p^{*}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
, a privacy parameter
\varepsilon>0
, a tuning parameter
\theta>0
, a failure probability
\beta
, a ratio parameter
\gamma\in(1,2]
, and a stream of mechanisms and dataset pairs
(\mathcal{M}_{t},X_{t})
where
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP, there is an
\varepsilon
-DP mechanism called the Generalized Thresholding Mechanism (
GTM
,
Algorithm

1
).
For all
t\geq 1
, define
\Lambda_{t}=(5t\ln^{3}(t+2))^{(2+\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}\,(4/\beta)^{(3+\theta+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
and the rejection thresholds

\bar{p}_{t}\;:=\;\max\!\left(\frac{p^{*}_{t}}{\gamma\,\Lambda_{t}},\;\;1-\gamma\,\Lambda_{t}\,(1-p^{*}_{t})\right).

Then, with probability
1-\beta
over the randomness of the
GTM
, for all
t\geq 1
, the following statements hold:

(1)

If
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}+\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
, then the mechanism halts.

(2)

If
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
, then the mechanism continues.

(3)

The number of evaluations of the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at step
t
is

N_{t}
drawn from a Poisson distribution
\Po(\lambda_{t})
for

\lambda_{t}\leq\tilde{A}_{t}\cdot w_{t}
, where

\tilde{A}_{t}\;:=\;O\!\left(\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\max\!\left(\frac{(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}}{p^{*}_{t}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}\right)\right)

and
w_{t}:=e^{\varepsilon_{t}\eta_{2,t}}
.
w_{t}
is distributed as a Pareto distribution
\Par(\sigma_{t})
with

\sigma_{t}:=\varepsilon/(\varepsilon_{t}(\theta+2))
, independently across steps.

We see that for a generic value of
p^{*}_{t}
,
3
achieves the rejection threshold

\bar{p}_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}\,\cdot\,\tilde{\Theta}(t^{-(2+\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}\beta^{(3+\theta+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}).

Our sample complexity is randomized, but in
22
we show that with probability
1-\beta
, for all
t\geq 1
,
N_{t}\leq\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\max\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}}{p^{*}_{t}},\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}\right)
. Further, in the setting where
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon/(2+\theta)
for all
t\geq 1
, the
amortized
sample complexity obeys the bound:

\Pr\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}\geq\tilde{O}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-(2+\theta)\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\max\left(\frac{(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}}{p^{*}},\frac{1}{1-p^{*}}\right)\right)\right]<\beta.

Comparison with
21
.
The exponent of
t
can be driven down to
(2+\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon
for any
\theta>0
at the cost of a reciprocally larger exponent of
\beta
, but the coefficients of both
t
and
\beta
are simultaneously smaller than those achieved in
21
, for which
\Lambda_{t}=(T/\beta^{2})^{12\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
. The ratio between the thresholds is large but finite for any choice of
\varepsilon
, and on studying the
\max
expression for
\bar{p}_{t}
in
3
we see that the gap between the thresholds
|p^{*}-\bar{p}_{t}|
reduces to
0
as the success threshold
p^{*}
approaches
0
or
1
; this is the case for both our work and
21
, but more rapidly in our case since our value of
\Lambda_{t}
is smaller. Apart from the improvement in the exponent, the more significant points of comparison between our works are the privacy guarantees and sample complexities. Our mechanism guarantees pure DP regardless of whether
(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
are pure or approx-DP, while
21
requires
(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
to be pure DP and their mechanism achieves only approximate DP guarantees.

21
have a
per-round
sample complexity of
\approx\tfrac{(T/\beta)^{12\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}\ln(T/\delta)}{\min\{p^{*},1-p^{*}\}}
.
As described above, our sample complexity obeys a high probability bound with a smaller exponent, and when
\varepsilon>(2+\theta)\varepsilon_{t}
, the amortized sample complexity scales only
logarithmically
with the time-step index, and incurs a
(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
dependence on the failure probability
\beta
.

Comparison with
4
.

4
do not state their accuracy bound along the lines of
21
(and the one used in this work). Therefore, to make a fair comparison, in
48
, we derive an accuracy guarantee for their mechanism.
It has a similar multiplicative relationship between
p^{*}
and
\bar{p}
but with two caveats: (1) the rejection threshold is at best
\bar{p}_{t}=\beta p^{*}/t
, regardless of the choice of
\varepsilon_{t}
and
\varepsilon
; and (2) the gap between the thresholds vanishes when
p^{*}
approaches
0
, but not when it approaches
1
.
Further, they require that
\varepsilon>2\varepsilon_{1}
.
Although our analysis does not preclude a more refined analysis, we prove a lower bound (
51
) showing that the factor of
\beta/t
is unavoidable for their algorithm.
The work of
13
uses similar algorithmic techniques for generalized private testing and selection, and when applied to this problem, suffers the same
\beta/t
slack (see
Section

9.4
).

For ease of comparison, we tabulate accuracy and sample complexity bounds in
Table

1
. We compare the rejection thresholds described in terms of the
noise penalty
(which captures the impact of privatizing perturbations), the rejection threshold achieved, and the sample complexity. For a fair comparison we mention only our high-probability sample complexity bound above, but as mentioned before, our amortized scaling is significantly better.

Table 1.
Comparison of accuracy and sample complexity for generalized private testing. Expressions suppress lower-order terms. For our results,
\theta>0
and
\gamma\in(1,2]
are free parameters. For
21
,
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
is an auxiliary parameter.
†
21
’s guarantee is conditional on halting at step
t
and does not guarantee halting at
p_{t}=p^{*}
, but at a value somewhat larger than
p^{*}
.

)

)

)

, finite

We complement our upper bound on the generalized private testing with the following lower bound on the accuracy of any generalized private tester.

Theorem 4

(Lower Bound, Informal version of
30
and
31
)
.

Given a target threshold
p^{*}
, a stream length
T
, an input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, output privacy parameters
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,1)
and a failure probability
\beta
, define
\delta^{\prime}:=\delta/(e^{\varepsilon}-1)
, and suppose
\beta+\delta^{\prime}<1/2
. Define
\overline{\Lambda}_{T}=e^{-2\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\left(T/(4(\beta+T\delta^{\prime})(\beta+\delta^{\prime}))\right)^{\!\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
. Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is any generalized private tester that achieves the rejection threshold
\bar{p}
. Then:

(1)

If
p^{*},\bar{p}\leq 1/2
, then
\bar{p}\leq p^{*}/\overline{\Lambda}_{T}
.

(2)

If
p^{*},\bar{p}\geq 1/2
, then
\bar{p}\leq 1-\overline{\Lambda}_{T}(1-p^{*})
.

(3)

If
\bar{p}<1/2<p^{*}
, then
\bar{p}\leq 1/(4(1-p^{*})\overline{\Lambda}_{T})
.

Further, we show that any generalized private tester for a given target threshold
p^{*}<1/2
with failure probability at most
\beta
must satisfy:

\Ex[N_{t}]=\Omega(1/(p^{*}\cdot\beta^{\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon})).

When
p^{*}
approaches
0
or
1
, the functional form of our upper and lower bounds is identical: (i)
\bar{p}_{t}=p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t})
when
p^{*}
is close to
0
compared with
\bar{p}\leq p^{*}_{t}/\overline{\Lambda}_{T}
in the lower bound and (ii)
\bar{p}_{t}=1-\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})
compared with
1-\overline{\Lambda}_{T}(1-p^{*}_{t})
in the lower bound. In these domains, the comparison reduces to the gap between
\gamma\Lambda_{t}
and
\overline{\Lambda}_{T}
; we note that
\gamma\leq 2
so this factor has limited impact. The lower bound for pure DP inputs and approximate DP outputs still requires that
\overline{\Lambda}_{t}\approx(T/((\beta+T\delta)(\beta+\delta)))^{\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}\approx(T/\beta^{2})^{\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
as
\delta\ll\beta
. If we have
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon_{1}
, our upper bound achieves a noisy penalty term
\Lambda_{t}=t^{(2+\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}\beta^{-(3+\theta+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
. The gap between the upper and lower bounds is thus reduced to small constants in the exponents of
t^{\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
and
\beta^{\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
in all privacy settings. We can reduce the gap in the exponent of
t
to a near-
2
factor at the cost of a larger exponent on
\beta^{\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
; conversely, when we minimize the exponent of
\beta^{\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
, then we have that
\Lambda_{t}\approx\beta^{-(3+2\sqrt{2})\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}t^{(2+\sqrt{2})\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
.

Our upper and lower bounds show that solving generalized private testing necessitates a loss in accuracy captured by a
\Lambda_{T}
factor, that scales essentially as
(T/\beta)^{c\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
, for some constant
c
, and an average sample complexity that scales as
(1/p^{*})\beta^{c^{\prime}\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
for some constant
c^{\prime}
. The lower bounds show that it is impossible to get rid of these polynomial in
t
and
\beta
factors, but that the impact of this factor in terms of the absolute gap between the acceptance and rejection thresholds
p^{*}
and
\bar{p}
vanishes as
p^{*}
approaches
0
or
1
.

Our upper bound suggests another way of mitigating the
\Lambda_{t}
-factor in the gap between
p^{*}
and
\bar{p}
, which is to let the ratio between the privacy parameters
\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{t}
scale as
\ln(t/\beta)
. We formalize this as an
ex-post
guarantee.

Definition 5

(Ex-post privacy
28
)
.

Given a randomized algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, define the ex-post privacy loss of
\mathcal{A}
on
o\in\mathcal{Y}
to be a function
\tilde{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
if for all neighboring
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\Pr[\mathcal{A}(X)=o]\leq e^{\tilde{\varepsilon}(o)}\Pr[\mathcal{A}(X^{\prime})=o]
.

Theorem 6

(Ex-post setting, Informal statement of
Theorem

23
)
.

In the setting of
3
, define
\Lambda_{0}=e^{2}\left(4/\beta\right)^{\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
. For all
t\geq 1
,
Algorithm

1
suffers ex-post privacy loss at most

\tilde{\varepsilon}((a_{1},\dots,a_{t}))=\begin{cases}\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{t}\ln(t/\beta)&\mbox{ if }a_{t}=s_{t}\\
\varepsilon&\mbox{ if }a_{t}=-s_{t}.\end{cases}

The accuracy and high-probability sample complexity guarantees are identical to that of
3
, with
\Lambda_{t}
replaced by
\Lambda_{0}
.

1.3.
Applications

We have seen that in
Algorithm

1
, one can reduce this loss in accuracy
\Lambda_{t}
to any arbitrarily small constant
C_{\Lambda}>1
in two ways:
(i)
by allowing the output privacy loss to scale logarithmically with
t/\beta
, i.e. setting
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{t}\ln(t/\beta)
, or
(ii)
by forcing the input privacy guarantees to become more restrictive, setting
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon\cdot 1/\ln(t/\beta)
.

The former guarantee serves as a way to solve the testing problem with a constant gap between the rejection threshold and incurring privacy loss scaling logarithmically with the length of the stream. In comparison, a fixed choice of
\varepsilon
and desired constant rejection threshold of
\bar{p}
would eventually cause false positives to occur at a constant rate; the privacy loss incurred by each false positive must be accounted for via basic or advanced composition, leading to a net privacy loss of
\approx\sqrt{t}\varepsilon_{t}
for long streams with an approximate DP guarantee. In contrast, by committing to a logarithmically scaling privacy loss, we are guaranteed to not incur any false positives with high probability and solve the same testing problem with a pure DP guarantee with privacy loss
=\varepsilon_{1}\ln t
.
The benefits of the latter guarantee, i.e. when
\varepsilon_{t}=\Theta(\varepsilon/\ln(t/\beta))
, are best illustrated by our next contribution that we discuss now.

Reduction from Optimization in the Continual Observation setting to the Batch setting.
Over the past few years, the
continual observation
(CO) model of differential privacy
9
has received a lot of interest. Unlike the standard
batch setting
, where the entire dataset
X
is known
a priori
, in the CO setting, the data is constantly modified by a stream of updates, with the dataset after the
t
-th update denoted by
X_{t}
. In the CO setting a solution
Y_{t}
must be output after every update, and the privacy requirement is that the entire output stream
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is differentially private with respect to sequence of datasets seen in the input
(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
.

Dwork, Naor, Pitassi, and Rothblum in their seminal work
9
posed a question whether the existence of a DP algorithm for a problem in the batch setting implies the existence of a DP algorithm in the continual observation setting.
They
(
9
, Section 6)
proposed an approach in which a mechanism in the CO setting maintains and repeatedly outputs a fixed solution. It modifies the output only when it no longer serves as a good solution for the updated dataset. Detecting when the prior solution is no longer of adequate quality is a
threshold monitoring problem
: at each time step, recomputation is triggered when the test passes and indicates that the function has changed significantly. By trading off some accuracy on average, over the length of the entire stream, one can achieve superior trade-offs between accuracy and privacy.

In the standard private testing setting, the
sparse vector technique
(SVT) is used to solve threshold monitoring efficiently when monitored function has bounded Lipschitz sensitivity. Unfortunately, the quality of the solutions tested by the CO scheme described above admits no tractable Lipschitz bound in general, and none of these privacy accounting techniques apply. This limits the scope of the general technique introduced by
9
. We give a brief overview of how our mechanism for generalized testing allows us to extend this reduction for a broad class of problems, and we refer the readers to
Section

7
for more detail.

When a mechanism for an optimization problem exists in the batch setting, we can circumvent the bounded Lipschitz requirement of
9
as follows. Rather than attempting to directly monitor the optimal utility
\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
at time
t
, we use a black-box batch DP algorithm
A(\cdot)
. At each time step, we give the
GTM
query access to
A(X_{t})
to test whether the quality of solutions generated by
A(X_{t})
is significantly better compared to the quality of the previous most recently generated output.

More precisely, let
f:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
be a maximization problem, which for a given dataset
X
asks for
\argmax_{Y\in\mathcal{Y}}f(X,Y)
; with this notation
\mathrm{OPT}_{t}=\max_{Y\in\mathcal{Y}}f(X_{t},Y)
. Let
A
be an algorithm that is
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP for any
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, and such that for
Y\leftarrow A(X)
, with probability
1-\beta_{A}
,
f(X,Y)\geq\alpha\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-E_{A}(\varepsilon_{1})
for some multiplicative approximation
\alpha\in(0,1)
and additive error
E_{A}(\varepsilon_{1})\geq 0
. Our reduction gives an algorithm in the continual observation setting that for any
\varepsilon<1
, with probability
1-O(\beta)
, for all
t\geq 1
, generates a sequence of outputs
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 1}
such that

f(X_{t},Y_{t})\geq\frac{\alpha\mathrm{OPT}_{t}}{1+\kappa}-E_{A}\left(\frac{\kappa\varepsilon}{\ln^{2}(t/\beta)}\right)-O\left(\frac{\ln^{3}(t/\beta)}{\kappa\varepsilon}\left(1+\ln\frac{1}{1-\beta_{A}}\right)\right).

Further, the total number of evaluations of
A
at time-step
t
is only
O(\ln(t/\beta))
.

The fact that we only need the batch algorithms to be differentially private, and not fulfill some more restrictive Lipschitz bound constraint, gives us significantly more flexibility in using algorithms that apply in the batch setting for constructing new ones in the CO setting.

Another key feature of our generalized threshold mechanism that makes this reduction effective is that, when we use the batch DP algorithm with
\varepsilon_{t}<\varepsilon/\ln(t/\beta)
(we set
\varepsilon_{t}<\varepsilon/\ln^{2}(t/\beta)
for technical reasons), the ex-post accuracy guarantee ensures that the noise penalty
\Lambda_{0}
is bounded by a universal constant independent of
t
, so the per-step accuracy of each threshold test does not degrade over time. This implies that we can invoke the batch algorithm at each step with a constant failure probability
\beta_{A}
, rather than one that decays with the stream length.
This time-independent failure probability requirement even allows us to adapt batch algorithms that provide only in-expectation accuracy guarantees, rather than high-probability bounds on accuracy. As an in-expectation accuracy bound of the form
\mathbb{E}[f(X,A(X))]\geq\alpha\cdot\text{OPT}(X)-E_{A}
implies a bounded constant failure probability (see
33
), such algorithms can be plugged directly into the reduction. In other words, there is no accumulation of the failure probabilities of the invocations of the batch algorithm.

Our reduction applies to any bounded-sensitivity, data-monotone maximization problem, and yields the first DP algorithms under continual observation for several fundamental problems, including submodular maximization under cardinality and matroid constraints, weighted densest subgraph, and Max-Cut. Crucially, the invocations of the batch algorithm in the
t
-th time-step occur with multiplicative privacy parameter set to equal
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon/\ln^{2}(t/\beta)
; this is in stark contrast with the
\sqrt{t}
scaling needed via a direct application of advanced composition. In Table
2
, we cover some examples to illustrate the benefits of these flexibilities, and direct the reader to
Section

8
for a more complete treatment.

to batch DP submodular maximization algorithms.

: batch optimum;
\mathrm{OPT}_{t}

t
;

: cardinality or matroid rank constraint;
\kappa,\eta>0
: user-defined parameters;
\beta
: failure probability. Monotone/non-monotone refers to argument-monotonicity; our framework additionally requires data-monotonicity. For in-expectation batch guarantees,

.

1.4.
Technical Overview of the Generalized Thresholding Mechanism

The starting point of our approach is that any mechanism for generalized private testing needs to evaluate
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
a (potentially randomized)
N_{t}
number of times to gain information about
p_{t}
, where
p_{t}=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})=+1]
.
The number
K_{t}
of
+1
outputs of these evaluations should be a sufficient statistic to reason about whether
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
should be accepted or rejected. The problem then reduces to defining (i) how to pick
N_{t}
; and (ii) for what values of
K_{t}
should one accept
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
, while ensuring a tight privacy-accuracy trade-off.

Achieving Privacy, Step 1: Poissonized sampling.

Our first key technical insight is that if we pick
N_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t})
for mean parameter
\lambda_{t}
, then
K_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t}p_{t})
as a consequence of
Poisson thinning
.

Achieving Privacy, Step 2: Randomizing
\lambda_{t}
.

Next, we perturb
\lambda_{t}
just enough to obfuscate
p_{t}
and
e^{-\varepsilon_{t}}p_{t}
, which in turn allows us to use a coupling argument for the privacy proof similar to the proof of the SVT.
To perturb
\lambda_{t}
, let it be a function of a random variable
Z
. Then
K_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t}(z)p_{t})
, where
z
is the value assumed by
Z
, and the algorithm decides to stop if
K_{t}>c_{t}
for a suitably chosen threshold
c_{t}
.
For all
t\geq 1
,
p_{t}^{\prime}=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t}^{\prime})]
where
X_{t}^{\prime}
is some dataset neighboring
X_{t}
.
As we are guaranteed that
p_{t}\geq e^{-\varepsilon_{t}}p^{\prime}_{t}
, we pick
\lambda_{t}(z)
such that
\lambda_{t}(z)p_{t}\geq\lambda_{t}(z-1)p^{\prime}_{t}
.
Specifically we choose
\lambda_{t}(z)=\rho_{t}e^{\varepsilon_{t}z}
, where
\rho_{t}
is a parameter that is needed for the accuracy bound and discussed further below.
This is useful as the CDF of the Poisson random variable
K_{t}
is non-increasing in
\lambda_{t}(z)p_{t}
.
Thus, for any
z
,
F(c_{t},\lambda_{t}(z)p_{t})\leq F(c_{t},\lambda_{t}(z-1)p^{\prime}_{t})
, where
F(c,\ell):=\Pr_{A\sim\Po(\ell)}[A\leq c]
is the probability that a Poisson random variable with parameter
\ell
returns a value less than
c.

Let
a_{t}
(
a^{\prime}_{t}
, resp.) denote the output of the mechanism at time
t
with input
X_{t}
(
X^{\prime}_{t}
, resp.). We show here the privacy argument for only the first time step, the detailed proof is given in Section
3.1
.
The probability that the mechanism when started on
X_{t}
does not stop in the first time step is

\Pr(a_{1}=-1)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F(c,\lambda(z)p_{t})f_{Z}(z)dz\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F(c,\lambda(z-1)p^{\prime}_{t})f_{Z}(z)dz.

Shifting the variable to
u=z-1
and assuming that
f_{Z}(u+1)\leq e^{\varepsilon}f_{Z}(u)
upper bounds the previous expression by

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F(c,\lambda(u)p^{\prime}_{t})f_{Z}(u+1)du\leq e^{\varepsilon}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F(c,\lambda(u)p^{\prime}_{t})f_{Z}(u)du=e^{\varepsilon}\Pr(a^{\prime}_{1}=-1)

To guarantee
f_{Z}(u+1)\leq e^{\varepsilon}f_{Z}(u)
, we sample a random variable
\eta_{1}
using an exponential distribution with scale
1/\varepsilon
and could set
Z=-\eta_{1}
.
However, for the privacy proof of the halting case and for the accuracy analysis, we need to choose
Z
in a more refined way, without destroying the crucial property of
f_{Z}
:
Setting
\lambda_{t}
in this manner allows us to bound the privacy loss by
\varepsilon
for an arbitrarily long sequence of failures, i.e.,
a_{1}=\dots=a_{t}=-1
; however, we need to introduce an appropriately scaled noise at every input,
\eta_{2,t}
, to bound the privacy loss to account for
a_{t}=+1
at some indeterminate time-step
t
(similar to the coupling argument in the standard private testing setting). We sample
\eta_{2,t}
using an exponential distribution with scale
2/\varepsilon
, i.e.,
\eta_{2,t}\sim\Exp(2/\varepsilon)
and for the coupling argument to work we could set
Z=-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}.

However, for the purposes of the accuracy analysis, it will be convenient to introduce a constant offset
\mu_{1}
to have
Z
be positive with high probability. Formally, let
\mu_{1}=(1/\varepsilon)\ln(3/\beta)
be a high-probability bound on the magnitude of
\eta_{1}
. Then we define

Z_{t}:=\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}.

Using the tail bound on exponential distribution, we can show that
Z_{t}\geq 0
for all
t\geq 1
with probability
1-\beta/3
over
\eta_{1}\sim\Exp(1/\varepsilon)
.

For point (ii) mentioned above, we test
K_{t}\geq c_{t}
for an appropriately chosen
c_{t}
(see
eq.

1
). Since threshold tests
K_{t}\geq c_{t}
are monotonic in the value of
K_{t}
, by post-processing, the outcomes of the tests will also be
\varepsilon
-DP.

Achieving Tight Accuracy, Step 1: Choosing Base Rate,
\rho_{t}
.

Recall that we define
\lambda_{t}
to equal
\rho_{t}\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})
with
\rho_{t}>0
and
Z_{t}:=\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}
and that there are so far no further restrictions on
\rho_{t}
.
By the choice of
\mu_{1}
and since
\eta_{2,t}\geq 0
unconditionally, we have that with probability
1-\beta/3
over the draw of
\eta_{1}
, we have
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}
.
For the accuracy analysis we also need an upper bound on
\eta_{2,t}
for all
t
.
We define a sequence of failure probabilities
\beta_{t}=\Theta(\beta/(t\ln^{3}t))
such that
\sum_{t\geq 1}\beta_{t}=\beta/6
.
Thus, with probability at least
1-\beta/2
, it holds that for all
t
,
\eta_{2,t}\leq(2/\varepsilon)\ln(1/\beta_{t})
and
\rho_{t}\leq\lambda_{t}\leq\Lambda_{t}\rho_{t}

with
\Lambda_{t}=\exp((\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon)(\ln(3/\beta)+\ln(1/\beta_{t})))
.

We now reason about how to conduct threshold tests where
N_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t})
is the number of such tests and
K_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t}p_{t})
is the number of
+1
outcomes.
At a high level,
the distribution of
K_{t}
has left and right tails roughly
\lambda_{t}p_{t}\pm\sqrt{6\lambda_{t}p_{t}\ln(1/\beta_{t})}
with probability at least
1-2\beta_{t}
; both grow monotonically with
\lambda_{t}p_{t}
.
We set
\rho_{t}:=\rho_{t}^{\dagger}/p^{*}_{t}
for appropriately chosen
\rho_{t}^{\dagger}
.
Thus with probability at least
1-\beta/2-\beta/3=1-5\beta/6
for all
t
it holds that

\displaystyle K_{t}

\displaystyle\geq\rho_{t}^{\dagger}-\sqrt{6\rho_{t}^{\dagger}\ln(1/\beta_{t})}\penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{ for }p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}\quad\textrm{ and}\quad K_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\rho_{t}^{\dagger}\Lambda_{t}\bar{p_{t}}}{p^{*}_{t}}+\sqrt{\frac{6\rho_{t}^{\dagger}\Lambda_{t}\bar{p_{t}}}{p^{*}_{t}}\ln(1/\beta_{t})}\penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{ for }p_{t}\leq\bar{p_{t}}.

At a high level, we need to set
\overline{p_{t}}
so as to compensate for the magnitude
\Lambda_{t}
of the privatizing noise
\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})
.
Setting
\overline{p_{t}}=p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t})
, for some fixed constant
\gamma>1
, allows us to offset the
\Lambda_{t}
factor and gives the following bound when
p_{t}\leq\bar{p_{t}}
:

K_{t}\leq\frac{\rho_{t}^{\dagger}}{\gamma}+\sqrt{\frac{6\rho_{t}^{\dagger}}{\gamma}}\ln(1/\beta_{t}).

To summarize, we require as threshold for our test a value of
c_{t}
such that

(1)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{\dagger}/{\gamma}+\sqrt{6(\rho_{t}^{\dagger}/{\gamma})\ln\left(1/\beta_{t}\right)}\,\leq\quad c_{t}\quad\leq\,\rho_{t}^{\dagger}-\sqrt{6\rho_{t}^{\dagger}\ln\left(1/\beta_{t}\right)}.

Note that, as
\rho_{t}^{\dagger}
increases, the leading term of both bounds dominates, and for any
\gamma>1
, these bounds become simultaneously satisfiable. Working out the details we get that there is a value
\rho_{t}^{\dagger}=\Theta\big(\frac{\ln(1/\beta_{t})}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\big)
that suffices. Combining everything together, we arrive at the expression

(2)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}=\Theta\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}\ln(1/\beta_{t})}{p^{*}(\gamma-1)^{2}}\right)=\Theta\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}\ln(t/\beta)}{p^{*}(\gamma-1)^{2}}\right).

The Flipped variant.
In the above discussion, we have generalized private testing with the rejection threshold
\overline{p_{t}}=p^{*}/\gamma\Lambda_{t}
. When
p^{*}
approaches
0
, the gap
p^{*}-\overline{p_{t}}
in absolute terms vanishes, even though
p^{*}/\overline{p_{t}}
remains
\gamma\Lambda_{t}
. This is desirable because it means there are few values for
p_{t}
where we do not have a guarantee on the performance of the mechanism.
However, when
p^{*}
is close to
1
, then
p^{*}-\overline{p_{t}}
is large, which is undesirable for the same reason. This suggests the following modification when
p^{*}
is close to
1
: instead of running the mechanism as is, we
flip
each test by running a threshold test on the evaluations of
-\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
with the target threshold
1-p^{*}
. Since
\Pr[\mathcal{M}(X_{t})=-1]=1-p_{t}
, this is the same test as
1-p_{t}<1-p^{*}
iff
p_{t}>p^{*}
. Now, this is the
Below Threshold
variant of the Thresholding Mechanism, i.e., the mechanism halts when
K_{t}\leq c_{t}.

Recall that the noise added to ensure privacy is one-sided with different scale (i.e.,
\eta_{1}\sim\Exp(1/\varepsilon)
and
\eta_{2,t}\sim\Exp(2/\varepsilon)
). Therefore, to account for the difference in the problem (i.e., Below threshold vs Above threshold), we also need to flip the noise variable: let
s_{t}
denote whether we want the mechanism to halt on acceptance or on rejection, then we define
Z_{t}=s_{t}(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}).

Clearly, this is equivalent to the description above when
s_{t}=+1
. Subtle adjustments are required, but otherwise the mechanism is identical, with the caveat that it must halt when it rejects some stream element. On implementing this modification, the analyst is free to pick whichever variant offers us less error, and, thus, we are able to show that the rejection threshold equals

\bar{p_{t}}\;:=\;\max\!\left\{\frac{p^{*}_{t}}{\gamma\,\Lambda_{t}},\;\;1-\gamma\,\Lambda_{t}\,(1-p^{*}_{t})\right\}.

Furthermore, since the variable
Z_{t}
is negative with high probability for
s_{t}=-1
, this implies that the Poisson mean parameter is deflated by up to a factor of
\Lambda_{t}
, instead of being inflated. In other words,
\rho_{t}=\Theta\left(\tfrac{\ln(t/\beta)}{p^{*}(\gamma-1)^{2}}\right)
leading (with a failure probability of at most
\beta/6
) to a high probability sample complexity bound of

N_{t}=\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\max\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}}{p^{*}_{t}},\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}\right).

The first term of the
\max
expression occurs on the direct variant, and the latter occurs on the flipped variant. There is
no inflation
in the sample complexity via the noise penalty term
\Lambda_{t}
in the flipped variant.

Purification via randomized response.
Thus far we have only reasoned about pure DP inputs. To deal with mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
that only fulfill a weaker
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP guarantee, we appeal to a black box modification based on randomized response. Each evaluation of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
is flipped with probability
\phi=\delta_{t}/(e^{\varepsilon_{t}}-1+2\delta_{t})\leq\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
. We denote this “
\phi-
smoothed” mechanism by
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
, and its success probability by
\widetilde{p}_{t}
. It is not hard to see that
|p_{t}-\widetilde{p}_{t}|\leq\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
; in other words, the perturbation to the success probabilities is vanishingly small.

The advantage of this
\phi
-smoothed mechanism is that it is
pure

\varepsilon_{t}
-DP. The key idea here is that if a mechanism with co-domain
\{+1,-1\}
is
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP, then in some sense the worst privacy loss occurs when for
v\in\{+1,-1\}
,
\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})=v]=\delta_{t}
, but
\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X^{\prime}_{t})=v]=0
. Clearly such a mechanism cannot be
\varepsilon
-DP for any value of
\varepsilon
. However, if we increase the probability of both outputs by
\delta_{t}
, then the ratio of these two outputs changes from being undefined to
2
, and the effective privacy loss as measured by the multiplicative privacy parameter changes from
\infty
to
\ln 2
. Refining this outline, we get the stated claim.

We can now run
Algorithm

1
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(X_{t})
, and the bound on
|p_{t}-\widetilde{p}_{t}|
implies that essentially the same acceptance and rejection thresholds hold, perturbed by at most a value of
\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
. This technique of using randomized response as post-processing to purify approximate-DP mechanisms with finite co-domains is attributed to folklore by
17
. The term purification was coined by
19
to describe the generation of pure DP outputs via randomized post-processing, and they give further generalizations and applications of this approach.

1.5.
Discussion on Private Testing vs Selection

A major motivation behind the introduction of generalized private selection and testing in
21
was private training of machine learning models. In this section, we discuss potential benefits of mechanisms like
GTM
for generalized private testing in this context.

Implication I: Hyperparameter Optimization.

Hyperparameter optimization (HPO) in private learning is the process of selecting parameters (like noise level, clipping norm, batch size, and learning rate) that balance model accuracy with privacy guarantees. Since finding these parameters itself can leak information, it must be done via privacy-preserving methods. Solving HPO was one of the motivations for generalized private selection and testing in
21
.

While subsequent works
26
;
13
used generalized private selection instead of testing for this problem, they mention that algorithms for generalized private testing have potential advantages over selection, but point out that if the acceptance threshold were set too high, one could evaluate large sets of hyperparameter choices without identifying a useful candidate, and conversely if we set it too low, then we would get poor quality outputs (see
(
26
, Appendix C)
).

Algorithm

1
addresses this challenge in part by allowing the analyst to set the acceptance threshold
p^{*}_{t}
adaptively in every step. This suggests a way to deal with the challenge mentioned by
26
: initialize
p^{*}_{t}
with a restrictive value at the beginning of the stream and make it gradually more permissive as
t
increases. Further, in the ex-post setting, if we commit to an ex-post privacy loss guarantee that scales logarithmically in the index of hyperparameter value output by the
GTM
, then we are guaranteed a rejection threshold
\bar{p}_{t}=p^{*}_{t}/\gamma\Lambda_{0}
in the notation of
6
and
3
, where
1/\gamma\Lambda_{0}
does not depend on the number of hyperparameter values tested. Further, the privacy loss incurred is pure and constant if no hyperparameter value is accepted even when testing on approximate DP mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{t}
.

Implication II: Reproducibility in Private Machine Learning.

13
suggested the following approach to use generalized private selection in practical private training: instead of trying to find the best hyperparameter directly, one privately trains models using all possible choices of hyperparameters and
selects
the output model that has the best generalization error (on a given set of test data). They prove that with sufficiently many repetitions per hyperparameter, one is guaranteed to output a model that is competitive with the best model generated (see
Section

9.4
for a complete discussion). However, this does not imply that the selection identified the best set of hyperparameters. Typically, there are a large number of hyperparameters with a small probability of generating a good trained model, and a few set of hyperparameters with a high probability of generating a good model. For
reproducibility
in learning (and considering intrinsic stochasticity of all learning algorithms), one would ideally like to use the hyperparameters of the latter type.

The guarantee of
13
(
52
) cannot circumvent the hardness of generalized private testing and identifying a good model; we formalize this via an idealized toy example in
53
. We find that regardless of the choice of privacy parameters, when we measure the quality of their private selection algorithm for
testing
, their approach incurs a
\approx\beta/T
loss in accuracy over a set of
T
hyperparameter values. At a high level, although correlated random dropping works well for generalized private selection, it seems to always incur a
T/\beta
factor in the ratio between acceptance and rejection thresholds when used for generalized private testing.

2.
Preliminaries

We collect some common notational choices here for reference throughout this paper.

Definition 7

(Notation)
.

We make the following definitions of convenience.

(1)

We define

\omega_{t}\;=\;\frac{1}{\zeta\cdot t\cdot\ln^{3}(t+2)},\qquad\zeta\;=\;\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j\,\ln^{3}(j+2)}.

With this definition,
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\omega_{t}=1
. One can check that
\zeta\in(1,5/4)
, and that therefore
1/\omega_{t}\in(t\ln^{3}(t+2),2t\ln^{3}(t+2))
.

(2)

We define
\beta_{t}\;:=\;\omega_{t}\beta
,
whence we have that
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\beta_{t}=\beta
, and
\beta_{t}\geq\frac{\beta}{\zeta t\ln^{3}(t+2)}
.
These expressions will be used heavily in all failure probability accounting.

We recall the definitions and some properties of the distributions used in the sequel.

Definition 8

(Distributions)
.

We use the following standard distributions.

(1)

Bernoulli.
For
p\in[0,1]
, a random variable
B\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p)
takes values in
\{0,1\}
with
\Pr[B=1]=p
and
\Pr[B=0]=1-p
.

(2)

Exponential.
For
\tau>0
, a random variable
\eta\sim\Exp(\tau)
has support
[0,\infty)
and density
f(z)=\frac{1}{\tau}\,e^{-z/\tau}
for
z\geq 0
.

(3)

Laplace.
For
\tau>0
, a random variable
\nu\sim\mathrm{Lap}(\tau)
has support
\mathbb{R}
and density
f(z)=\frac{1}{2\tau}e^{-|z|/(\tau)}
for
z\in\mathbb{R}
.

(4)

Poisson.
For
\lambda\geq 0
, a random variable
M\sim\Po(\lambda)
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with mass function
\Pr[M=k]=e^{-\lambda}\,\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

(5)

Pareto.
For
\sigma>0
, a random variable
w\sim\Par(\sigma)

has support
[1,\infty)
with
\Pr[w>u]=u^{-\sigma}
for
u\geq 1

and density
f(u)=\sigma\,u^{-\sigma-1}
for
u\geq 1
.

Definition 9

(Differential Privacy fundamentals)
.

(1)

We define the
sensitivity
of a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\Delta f:=\max_{d(X,X^{\prime})=1}|f(X)-f(X^{\prime})|
, where
d(X,X^{\prime})=1
is shorthand for
X
and
X^{\prime}
are neighboring.

(2)

Given a sensitivity-
1
query
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, the Laplace mechanism (
8
) releases
f(X)+\nu
for
\nu\sim\mathrm{Lap}(1/\varepsilon)
. The Laplace mechanism is
\varepsilon
-DP.

Poisson mean thresholds.

For
x\geq 0
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, define the
left
and
right mean thresholds

(3)

\displaystyle\lambda^{\ell}(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle:=x+\ln(1/\alpha)+\sqrt{2x\ln(1/\alpha)+\ln^{2}(1/\alpha)},

(4)

\displaystyle\lambda^{r}(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle:=x+1-\sqrt{2(x+1)\ln(1/\alpha)}.

These thresholds are derived from Poisson Chernoff bounds (
54
) and provide sufficient conditions for tail control: if
M\sim\Po(\lambda)
, then
\lambda\geq\lambda^{\ell}(x,\alpha)
guarantees
\Pr[M\leq x]\leq\alpha
, and
\lambda\leq\lambda^{r}(x,\alpha)
guarantees
\Pr[M\geq x+1]\leq\alpha
(under a mild validity condition that
x
not be too small). The thresholds may not be tight with respect to the exact Poisson tail probabilities (see
10
, item 3).

We define the mean thresholds for real valued
x
as opposed to just integer valued so as to be able to exploit the algebraic inversion property. See
10
, item 4.

Lemma 10

(Poisson and exponential facts)
.

The following properties are used throughout.

(1)

Additivity.

If
M_{1}\sim\Po(\lambda_{1})
and
M_{2}\sim\Po(\lambda_{2})
are independent, then
M_{1}+M_{2}\sim\Po(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})
.

(2)

Thinning.

Let
M\sim\Po(\lambda)
, and conditional on
M
, let
B_{1},\dots,B_{M}
be i.i.d.
\mathsf{Ber}(p)
random variables, independent of
M
. Then
K:=\sum_{i=1}^{M}B_{i}\sim\Po(\lambda p)
.

(3)

Tail bounds.

Let
M\sim\Po(\lambda)
and fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

(a)

(Left tail.)
If
\lambda\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c,\alpha)
, then
\Pr[M\leq c]\leq\alpha
.

(b)

(Right tail.)
If
c+1\geq 2\ln(1/\alpha)
and
\lambda\leq\lambda^{r}(c,\alpha)
, then
\Pr[M\geq c+1]\leq\alpha
.

(4)

Algebraic inversion.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
and let
L=\ln(1/\alpha)
. For
\lambda\geq 0
and
x\geq 0
with
x+1>2L
,

\lambda\leq\lambda^{r}(x,\alpha)\quad\iff\quad x+1\geq\lambda^{\ell}(\lambda,\alpha).

(5)

Exponential density shift.

Let
\eta\sim\Exp(\tau)
with density
f(z)=\frac{1}{\tau}\,e^{-z/\tau}
for
z\geq 0
. For any
z\geq 0
and
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}
with
z+\Delta\geq 0
,

f(z+\Delta)=e^{-\Delta/\tau}\,f(z).

Proof.

Items 1–2.
Standard; see, e.g.,
25
.

Item 3.

Write
L=\ln(1/\alpha)
.

Left tail.

We apply
54
(lower tail) with shift
u=\lambda-c
.
The assumption
\lambda\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c,\alpha)\geq c
ensures
u\geq 0
, so the bound gives

\Pr[M\leq c]=\Pr[M\leq\lambda-u]\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{(\lambda-c)^{2}}{2\lambda}\right).

We require this to be at most
\alpha
, i.e.,
(\lambda-c)^{2}\geq 2\lambda L
.
Expanding the left side and rearranging by powers of
\lambda
yields the quadratic inequality

\lambda^{2}-2(c+L)\lambda+c^{2}\geq 0.

The roots of the corresponding equation are

\lambda=c+L\pm\sqrt{(c+L)^{2}-c^{2}}=c+L\pm\sqrt{2cL+L^{2}}.

Since
u\geq 0
requires
\lambda\geq c
, we need
\lambda
at or above the larger root.
Recognizing the larger root as
\lambda^{\ell}(c,\alpha)
, the bound
\Pr[M\leq c]\leq\alpha
holds for all
\lambda\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c,\alpha)
.

Right tail.

We apply
54
(upper tail) with shift
u=c+1-\lambda
for
u\geq 0
.
Note that the denominator in the Chernoff exponent becomes
2(\lambda+u)=2(c+1)
, giving

\Pr[M\geq c+1]=\Pr[M\geq\lambda+u]\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{(c+1-\lambda)^{2}}{2(c+1)}\right).

We require this to be at most
\alpha
, i.e.,
(c+1-\lambda)^{2}\geq 2(c+1)L
as
L=\ln(1/\alpha)
.
We first show that this implies
c+1\geq 2L
.
The Chernoff upper tail bound requires the shift
u=c+1-\lambda
to be nonnegative, so we must have
\lambda\leq c+1
.
If
c+1<2L
, then for every
\lambda\in[0,\,c+1]
we have
(c+1-\lambda)^{2}\leq(c+1)^{2}<2(c+1)L
, so the required inequality
(c+1-\lambda)^{2}\geq 2(c+1)L
cannot hold.
The condition
c+1\geq 2L
is therefore necessary.

Under this condition, we take the square root of both sides.
Since the shift
u=c+1-\lambda
must be nonnegative,
the left side
c+1-\lambda
is nonnegative, and we obtain

c+1-\lambda\geq\sqrt{2(c+1)L}.

Isolating
\lambda
yields
\lambda\leq c+1-\sqrt{2(c+1)L}=\lambda^{r}(c,\alpha)
.
Note that this automatically enforces
\lambda\leq c+1
, so the nonnegative shift requirement
u\geq 0
is satisfied.

Item 4.

Let
L=\ln(1/\alpha)
and set
y=x+1
, so the validity condition is
y>2L
. Starting from
\lambda\leq\lambda^{r}(x,\alpha)
:

\lambda\leq y-\sqrt{2yL}\quad\iff\quad y-\lambda\geq\sqrt{2yL}.

Since
\sqrt{2yL}\geq 0
, this implies
y\geq\lambda
. So squaring
y-\lambda\geq\sqrt{2yL}
gives
(y-\lambda)^{2}\geq 2yL
, i.e.,
y^{2}-2(\lambda+L)y+\lambda^{2}\geq 0
. Because
y\geq\lambda
, the variable
y
lies at or above the larger root:

y\geq\lambda+L+\sqrt{2\lambda L+L^{2}}=\lambda^{\ell}(\lambda,\alpha).

Substituting
y=x+1
yields
x+1\geq\lambda^{\ell}(\lambda,\alpha)
. All steps are reversible (the implication
y\geq\lambda
is enforced by
y-\lambda\geq\sqrt{2yL}\geq 0
), establishing the equivalence.

Item 5.

Direct computation:
f(z+\Delta)=\frac{1}{\tau}\,e^{-(z+\Delta)/\tau}=e^{-\Delta/\tau}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau}\,e^{-z/\tau}=e^{-\Delta/\tau}\,f(z)
.
∎

Lemma 11

(Poisson overhead control)
.

Fix error parameters
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
and a target ratio
\gamma\in(1,2]
.
Let
A=\ln(1/\alpha)
,
B=\ln(1/\beta)
and
c=\max(\lfloor 2\max(A,B)\rfloor,\lceil r^{2}\rceil)
, where

r=\frac{\sqrt{2A}+\gamma\sqrt{2B}+\sqrt{(\sqrt{2A}+\gamma\sqrt{2B})^{2}+8(\gamma-1)A}}{2(\gamma-1)}.

Then, the following statements hold:

(1)

\lambda^{\ell}(c,\alpha)\leq\gamma\,\lambda^{r}(c,\beta)
,

(2)

c+1>2\max\!\big(\ln(1/\alpha),\;\ln(1/\beta)\big)
,

(3)

c=O\!\big((\ln(1/\alpha)+\ln(1/\beta))/(\gamma-1)^{2}\big)
.

Proof.

Setting
m=\sqrt{c+1}
, the right threshold expands as

\lambda^{r}(c,\beta)=c+1-\sqrt{2(c+1)B}=m^{2}-m\sqrt{2B}.

For the left threshold, bounding
c<m^{2}
and applying

\sqrt{2cA+A^{2}}<\sqrt{2m^{2}A+A^{2}}\leq m\sqrt{2A}+A
gives

\lambda^{\ell}(c,\alpha)=c+A+\sqrt{2cA+A^{2}}<m^{2}+2A+m\sqrt{2A}.

The condition
\lambda^{\ell}(c,\alpha)\leq\gamma\,\lambda^{r}(c,\beta)
is therefore implied by

m^{2}+2A+m\sqrt{2A}\;\leq\;\gamma\!\left(m^{2}-m\sqrt{2B}\right),

which rearranges to

(5)

(\gamma-1)\,m^{2}-\bigl(\sqrt{2A}+\gamma\sqrt{2B}\bigr)\,m-2A\;\geq\;0.

Since
\gamma>1
, this quadratic inequality holds for all
m
at or above its larger positive root

r\;:=\;\frac{\sqrt{2A}+\gamma\sqrt{2B}+\sqrt{\bigl(\sqrt{2A}+\gamma\sqrt{2B}\bigr)^{2}+8(\gamma-1)A}}{2(\gamma-1)}.

Define

c\;:=\;\max\!\Big(\big\lfloor 2\max(A,B)\big\rfloor,\;\big\lceil r^{2}\big\rceil\Big).

Item 1.

Since
c\geq\lceil r^{2}\rceil\geq r^{2}
, we have
m=\sqrt{c+1}>r
,
so (
5
) holds and the gap condition follows.

Item 2.

Since
\lfloor y\rfloor+1>y
for all
y
, we have

c+1\geq\lfloor 2\max(A,B)\rfloor+1>2\max(A,B)
.

Item 3.

Write
a=\sqrt{2A}+\gamma\sqrt{2B}
for the linear coefficient in (
5
).
Using
\sqrt{a^{2}+b}\leq a+\sqrt{b}
for
a\geq 0
, the numerator of
r
satisfies

a+\sqrt{a^{2}+8(\gamma-1)A}\;\leq\;2a+\sqrt{8(\gamma-1)A}.

Since
\gamma\leq 2
, we have
a\leq\sqrt{2A}+2\sqrt{2B}
and

\sqrt{8(\gamma-1)A}\leq\sqrt{8A}
, so the numerator is

O(\sqrt{A}+\sqrt{B})
. Therefore

r=O((\sqrt{A}+\sqrt{B})/(\gamma-1))
and

r^{2}=O((A+B)/(\gamma-1)^{2})
.
Since
\lfloor 2\max(A,B)\rfloor\leq 2(A+B)
,
the conclusion
c=O((A+B)/(\gamma-1)^{2})
follows.
∎

Lemma 12

(Pareto facts)
.

The following properties are used throughout.

(1)

Exponential to Pareto transformation.

If
\eta\sim\Exp(\tau)
, then
e^{\eta/(\sigma\tau)}\sim\Par(\sigma)
.

(2)

Moments.

Let
w\sim\Par(\sigma)
. For any
k>0
,

\Ex[w^{-k}]=\sigma/(\sigma+k)
.
For any
0<k<\sigma
,

\Ex[w^{k}]=\sigma/(\sigma-k)
.
If
k\geq\sigma
, then
\Ex[w^{k}]=\infty
.

(3)

Truncated moments.

Let
w\sim\Par(\sigma)
with
\sigma>1
, and define
\tilde{w}:=\min(w,W)
for
W\geq 1
. Then:

(a)

\displaystyle\Ex[\tilde{w}]=\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}-\frac{W^{1-\sigma}}{\sigma-1}\leq\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}
.

(b)

\displaystyle\Ex[\tilde{w}^{2}]=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{2\,W^{2-\sigma}-\sigma}{2-\sigma}&\text{if }\sigma\neq 2,\\[8.0pt]
2\ln W+1&\text{if }\sigma=2.\end{cases}

In particular: when
1<\sigma<2
,
\Ex[\tilde{w}^{2}]\leq\frac{2}{2-\sigma}\,W^{2-\sigma}
;
when
\sigma=2
,
\Ex[\tilde{w}^{2}]=2\ln W+1
;
when
\sigma>2
,
\Ex[\tilde{w}^{2}]\leq\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-2}
.

Proof.

Item 1.

For
u\geq 1
,
\Pr[e^{\eta/(\sigma\tau)}>u]=\Pr[\eta>\sigma\tau\ln u]=e^{-\sigma\ln u}=u^{-\sigma}
.

Item 2.

By direct integration,

\Ex[w^{k}]=\sigma\int_{1}^{\infty}u^{k-\sigma-1}\,\mathsf{d}u=\sigma/(\sigma-k)

when
k<\sigma
, and the integral diverges when
k\geq\sigma
.
Similarly,

\Ex[w^{-k}]=\sigma\int_{1}^{\infty}u^{-k-\sigma-1}\,\mathsf{d}u=\sigma/(\sigma+k)

for all
k>0
.

Item 3(a).

\Ex[\tilde{w}]=\int_{1}^{W}u\cdot\sigma u^{-\sigma-1}\,\mathsf{d}u+W\cdot W^{-\sigma}
.
The integral equals
\frac{\sigma}{1-\sigma}(W^{1-\sigma}-1)
for
\sigma>1
.
Adding
W^{1-\sigma}
and simplifying gives

\Ex[\tilde{w}]=\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}-\frac{W^{1-\sigma}}{\sigma-1}
.
Since
W\geq 1
and
\sigma>1
, the subtracted term is nonnegative.

Item 3(b).

\Ex[\tilde{w}^{2}]=\sigma\int_{1}^{W}u^{1-\sigma}\,\mathsf{d}u+W^{2-\sigma}
.
For
\sigma\neq 2
, the integral equals
\frac{\sigma}{2-\sigma}(W^{2-\sigma}-1)
,
and adding
W^{2-\sigma}
gives
\frac{2\,W^{2-\sigma}-\sigma}{2-\sigma}
.
For
\sigma=2
, the integral equals
2\ln W
, and adding
1
gives
2\ln W+1
.

For the bounds: when
1<\sigma<2
,
2-\sigma>0
,
so
\frac{2W^{2-\sigma}-\sigma}{2-\sigma}\leq\frac{2W^{2-\sigma}}{2-\sigma}
.
When
\sigma>2
, rewrite
\frac{2W^{2-\sigma}-\sigma}{2-\sigma}=\frac{\sigma-2W^{2-\sigma}}{\sigma-2}\leq\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-2}

since
W^{2-\sigma}\geq 0
.
∎

We will find it convenient to reason about the accuracy of generalized private testers in terms of their
per-step type I and type II
errors.

Definition 13

(Accuracy)
.

Consider a generalized private tester that for some given failure probability
\beta
and target thresholds
(p^{*}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
achieves rejection thresholds
(\bar{p}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
.
A
Type I error
(false halt) at step
t
is the event that the tester halts and
s_{t}\,p_{t}\leq s_{t}\,\bar{p}_{t}
.
A
Type II error
(missed halt) at step
t
is the event that the tester continues and
s_{t}\,p_{t}\geq s_{t}\,p^{*}_{t}
.
Then we define the following notions of accuracy.

(1)

Global accuracy.

The tester is
\beta
-globally accurate if,
with probability at least
1-\beta
,
no Type I or Type II error occurs at any step.

(2)

Per-step accuracy.

The tester has
(\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{I}},\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}})
per-step accuracy
at step
t
if the Type I and Type II error probabilities
at step
t
are at most
\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{I}}
and
\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}
, respectively.

For the given choice of thresholds, the generalized private tester considered in this definition is
\beta
-globally accurate.

3.
The Generalized Thresholding Mechanism

In this section, we introduce the Generalized Thresholding Mechanism (
Algorithm

1
). The algorithm monitors a sequence of mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{t}:\mathcal{X}\to\{-1,+1\}
, each evaluated on a dataset
X_{t}
with an input privacy promise
\varepsilon_{t}\geq 0
, and halts when it believes that the success probability
p_{t}:=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})=+1]
lies in the target regime.

The sequence is provided by an
adaptive analyst
.
At the beginning of the stream, the analyst fixes (a) a value
\tau_{1}
that determines the scale parameter of a privatizing noise value
\eta_{1}\sim\Exp(\tau_{1})
that is shared across the stream, and (b) a
calibration parameter

\mu_{1}
which will be a high probability upper bound on the magnitude of
\eta_{1}
.
At each step
t
, after observing the prior outputs
a_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}
, the analyst selects (c) the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}
, (d) dataset
X_{t}
, (e) privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{t}
, subject only to the constraint that
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is
\varepsilon_{t}
-differentially private,
and (f) a noise scale
\tau_{2,t}
.
The mechanism will choose
various per-step tuning parameters, namely (1) a threshold
c_{t}
, (2) a base rate
\rho_{t}
, and (3) a direction
s_{t}
. The privacy guarantee (
17
) holds against all such adaptive choices; it is parameterized by
\tau_{1}
and by the per-step noise scales
\tau_{2,t}
(which directly determines the privacy cost at halting time, regardless of the values of
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{t}
).
However, different values of
s_{t}
,
\rho_{t}
, and
c_{t}
yield qualitatively different accuracy guarantees in terms of the rejection threshold
\bar{p}
and we give below values that minimize accuracy loss.

procedure

GTM.Init
(\tau_{1},\,\mu_{1})
:

Input:

Shared noise scale
\tau_{1}>0
; calibration offset
\mu_{1}>0
.

Draw
\eta_{1}\sim\Exp(\tau_{1})
;

Store
\eta_{1}
and
\mu_{1}
;

procedure

GTM.Step
(\mathcal{M}_{t},\,X_{t},\,\varepsilon_{t},\,\tau_{2,t},\,c_{t},\,\rho_{t},\,s_{t})
:

Input:

privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{t}\geq 0
;
Mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}:\mathcal{X}\to\{-1,+1\}
(
\varepsilon_{t}
-DP);
dataset
X_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
;
per-step noise scale
\tau_{2,t}>0
;
threshold
c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
;
base rate
\rho_{t}>0
;
direction
s_{t}\in\{-1,+1\}
.

Output:

a_{t}\in\{-s_{t},\,s_{t}\}
, where
a_{t}=s_{t}
denotes halting
and
a_{t}=-s_{t}
denotes continuation.

Draw
\eta_{2,t}\sim\Exp(\tau_{2,t})
;

Z_{t}\leftarrow s_{t}\bigl(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}\bigr)
;

\lambda_{t}\leftarrow\rho_{t}\cdot\exp\!\left(\varepsilon_{t}\,Z_{t}\right)
;

Draw
N_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t})
;

K_{t}\leftarrow 0
;

for

i=1,\ldots,N_{t}

do

Draw
y_{t,i}\sim\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
;

if

y_{t,i}=+1

then

K_{t}\leftarrow K_{t}+1
;

end for

if

s_{t}\cdot(K_{t}-c_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2})>0

then

Output

a_{t}\leftarrow s_{t}
and
halt
;

else

Output

a_{t}\leftarrow-s_{t}
;

Algorithm 1

Generalized Thresholding Mechanism

The algorithm uses two sources of randomness for privacy. A shared noise value
\eta_{1}\sim\Exp(\tau_{1})
is drawn once at initialization and reused at every step. At each step
t
, a fresh noise value
\eta_{2,t}\sim\Exp(\tau_{2,t})
is drawn independently. They are combined with the calibration offset
\mu_{1}>0
and the direction
s_{t}\in\{-1,+1\}
as follows to form the noise term

Z_{t}=s_{t}\cdot(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}).

The noise
Z_{t}
controls the number of mechanism evaluations at step
t
. The algorithm makes
N_{t}
i.i.d. evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
, where

N_{t}\sim\Po(\rho_{t}\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})).

The exponential scaling
\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})
scales the perturbation with the input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{t}
; when
\varepsilon_{t}=0
, the mechanism already satisfies
0
-DP and no perturbation is needed, so
N_{t}
reduces to the base rate
\rho_{t}
.

The algorithm counts the number
K_{t}
of
+1
outcomes among the
N_{t}
-many evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
. The direction parameter
s_{t}
determines two modes of operation.

(1)

When
s_{t}=+1
(
above threshold
), the algorithm halts if
K_{t}\geq c_{t}+1
, indicating that
p_{t}
is large.

(2)

When
s_{t}=-1
(
below threshold
), the algorithm halts if
K_{t}\leq c_{t}
, indicating that
p_{t}
is small.

Conditioned on
\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}
, the signed quantity
s_{t}Z_{t}=\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}
is nonnegative. Therefore,
Z_{t}
is positive when
s_{t}=+1
and negative when
s_{t}=-1
. This means that above threshold (
s_{t}=+1
) never deflates
N_{t}
below
\rho_{t}
, while the below threshold (
s_{t}=-1
) never inflates it above
\rho_{t}
.

Definition 14

(Execution variables and continuation probability)
.

Fix a step
t\geq 1
. Let the parameters up to step
t
be as specified in
Algorithm

1
. Write
p_{t}:=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})=+1]
.

Algorithm

1
induces the following random variables at step
t
:

•

Aggregated noise and conditional Poisson mean.
Define

Z_{t}:=s_{t}(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}),\qquad\lambda_{t}(z):=\rho_{t}\exp(\varepsilon_{t}\,z)\quad\text{for }z\in\mathbb{R},

and write
\lambda_{t}:=\lambda_{t}(Z_{t})=\rho_{t}\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})
for the
realized Poisson mean
. The algorithm draws
N_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t})
evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
.

•

Count and thinning.
Let
K_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{t}}\mathbf{1}\{y_{t,i}=+1\}
denote the number of
+1
outcomes. By Poisson thinning (
10
), conditional on
\lambda_{t}
,

K_{t}\;\sim\;\Po(\lambda_{t}\,p_{t}).

•

Continuation probability.
Define
g_{t}(z)
to be the probability that step
t
produces the non-halting output
a_{t}=-s_{t}
, given
Z_{t}=z
:

g_{t}(z):=\Pr\!\big[a_{t}=-s_{t}\;\big|\;Z_{t}=z\big].

For a neighboring dataset
X^{\prime}_{t}
, write
p^{\prime}_{t}:=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X^{\prime}_{t})=+1]
, and let
g^{\prime}_{t}(z)
denote the corresponding continuation probability when
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is evaluated on
X^{\prime}_{t}
.

3.1.
Privacy analysis

Lemma 15

(Poisson representation and pointwise shifts)
.

Fix a step
t\geq 1
and neighboring datasets
X_{t},X^{\prime}_{t}
.
Let
g_{t}(\cdot)
and
g^{\prime}_{t}(\cdot)
be the continuation probabilities from
14
, and define the Poisson cumulative distribution function
F(c;\lambda):=\Pr[M\leq c]
where
M\sim\Po(\lambda)
.
Then for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
, the following hold:

(1)

(Poisson representation.)

g_{t}(z)=\begin{cases}F\!\big(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\big),&\text{if }s_{t}=+1,\\[4.30554pt]
1-F\!\big(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\big),&\text{if }s_{t}=-1,\end{cases}

and an identical representation holds for
g^{\prime}_{t}(z)
with
p^{\prime}_{t}
in place of
p_{t}
.

(2)

(Shift identities for the conditional mean.)

\lambda_{t}(z-s_{t})=e^{-s_{t}\varepsilon_{t}}\,\lambda_{t}(z)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lambda_{t}(z+s_{t})=e^{s_{t}\varepsilon_{t}}\,\lambda_{t}(z).

(3)

(Shift inequalities for continuation probability.)

g_{t}(z)\leq g^{\prime}_{t}(z-s_{t})\qquad\text{and}\qquad 1-g_{t}(z)\leq 1-g^{\prime}_{t}(z+s_{t}).

Proof.

Fix
z\in\mathbb{R}
.

Part (1).

Condition on the event
\{Z_{t}=z\}
. Under this conditioning, the algorithm draws
N_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t}(z))
evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
, and counts
K_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{t}}\mathbf{1}\{y_{t,i}=+1\}
. By Poisson thinning (
10
),

K_{t}\sim\Po\!\big(\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\big).

The step outputs
-s_{t}
exactly when
s_{t}\cdot(K_{t}-c_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2})\leq 0
.
For
s_{t}=+1
, this condition is
K_{t}\leq c_{t}
as
c_{t}
is an integer, yielding
g_{t}(z)=F(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t})
.
For
s_{t}=-1
, this condition is
K_{t}\geq c_{t}+1
, yielding
g_{t}(z)=1-F(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t})
.
The derivation for
g^{\prime}_{t}(z)
is identical with
p^{\prime}_{t}
replacing
p_{t}
.

Part (2).

This follows directly from the definition of
\lambda_{t}(\cdot)
:

\lambda_{t}(z\pm s_{t})=\rho_{t}\exp\!\big(\varepsilon_{t}(z\pm s_{t})\big)=e^{\pm s_{t}\varepsilon_{t}}\,\lambda_{t}(z).

Part (3).

We establish the shift inequalities by considering the two cases of
s_{t}
.

Case
s_{t}=+1
:

By part (1),
g_{t}(z)=F\!\big(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\big)
and
g^{\prime}_{t}(z-1)=F\!\big(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z-1)\,p^{\prime}_{t}\big)
.
By
\varepsilon_{t}
-differential privacy,
p_{t}\geq e^{-\varepsilon_{t}}p^{\prime}_{t}
. By part (2),
\lambda_{t}(z)=e^{\varepsilon_{t}}\lambda_{t}(z-1)
. Multiplying these yields
\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\geq\lambda_{t}(z-1)\,p^{\prime}_{t}
.
Because the Poisson CDF
\lambda\mapsto F(c;\lambda)
is non-increasing in
\lambda
, it follows that

g_{t}(z)=F\!\big(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\big)\leq F\!\big(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z-1)\,p^{\prime}_{t}\big)=g^{\prime}_{t}(z-1).

For the second inequality, we evaluate the halting probability
1-g_{t}(z)=\Pr[\Po(\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t})\geq c_{t}+1]
. By differential privacy,
p_{t}\leq e^{\varepsilon_{t}}p^{\prime}_{t}
, and by part (2),
\lambda_{t}(z+1)=e^{\varepsilon_{t}}\lambda_{t}(z)
, yielding
\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\leq\lambda_{t}(z+1)\,p^{\prime}_{t}
. Since
1-F(c;\lambda)
is non-decreasing in
\lambda
, we obtain

1-g_{t}(z)\leq 1-g^{\prime}_{t}(z+1).

Case
s_{t}=-1
:

By part (1),
g_{t}(z)=1-F\!\big(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\big)
and
g^{\prime}_{t}(z+1)=1-F\!\big(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z+1)\,p^{\prime}_{t}\big)
.
By differential privacy,
p_{t}\leq e^{\varepsilon_{t}}p^{\prime}_{t}
, and by part (2),
\lambda_{t}(z+1)=e^{\varepsilon_{t}}\lambda_{t}(z)
, yielding
\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\leq\lambda_{t}(z+1)\,p^{\prime}_{t}
.
Because
F(c_{t};\lambda)
is non-increasing in
\lambda
, the complement
1-F(c_{t};\lambda)
is non-decreasing. Thus

g_{t}(z)\leq g^{\prime}_{t}(z+1)=g^{\prime}_{t}(z-s_{t}).

For the second inequality, note that
1-g_{t}(z)=F(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t})
and
1-g^{\prime}_{t}(z-1)=F(c_{t};\,\lambda_{t}(z-1)\,p^{\prime}_{t})
.
Using
p_{t}\geq e^{-\varepsilon_{t}}p^{\prime}_{t}
and
\lambda_{t}(z)=e^{\varepsilon_{t}}\lambda_{t}(z-1)
, we find
\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}\geq\lambda_{t}(z-1)\,p^{\prime}_{t}
.
Applying the non-increasing property of
F(c_{t};\lambda)
gives

1-g_{t}(z)\leq 1-g^{\prime}_{t}(z-1)=1-g^{\prime}_{t}(z+s_{t}).

∎

Remark 16

(Asymmetric use of input privacy)
.

The shift inequalities in Part (3) depend on the input privacy guarantee only through bounds on the ratio
p_{t}/p^{\prime}_{t}
of the
+1
output probabilities. In all four sub-cases, the Poisson thinning mean is
\lambda_{t}(z)\,p_{t}
, and the comparison is with
\lambda_{t}(z\pm 1)\,p^{\prime}_{t}
; the ratio
(1-p_{t})/(1-p^{\prime}_{t})
of the
-1
output probabilities never appears.
When the algorithm operates on the negated mechanism (flipped variant,
s_{t}=-1
), the role of
p_{t}
and
1-p_{t}
is exchanged, and the relevant ratio becomes
(1-p_{t})/(1-p^{\prime}_{t})
in terms of the original mechanism.

Lemma 17

(Privacy of the generalized thresholding mechanism)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and
(X^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
be dataset sequences with
X_{t}
neighboring
X^{\prime}_{t}
for every
t
.
Run
Algorithm

1
on
(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and on
(X^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
with the same sequences
(\varepsilon_{t})
,
(c_{t})
,
(\rho_{t})
,
(\tau_{2,t})
, and
(s_{t})
. Let
(a_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and
(a^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
denote the resulting output sequences.

Then for every
t\geq 1
,

(6)

\displaystyle\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\big]

\displaystyle\leq e^{1/\tau_{1}}\,\Pr\!\big[a^{\prime}_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\big],

(7)

\displaystyle\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t-1},s_{t})\big]

\displaystyle\leq e^{1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2,t}}\,\Pr\!\big[a^{\prime}_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t-1},s_{t})\big].

In particular, if
\tau_{2,t}=\tau_{2}
for all
t
, the algorithm satisfies pure
\varepsilon
-differential privacy with
\varepsilon=1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2}
.

Proof.

Write
f_{1}
for the pdf of
\eta_{1}\sim\Exp(\tau_{1})
and
f_{2,t}
for the density of
\eta_{2,t}\sim\Exp(\tau_{2,t})
. By the exponential density shift (
10
, item 5),

(8)

f_{1}(x+1)=e^{-1/\tau_{1}}\,f_{1}(x)\quad\text{for }x\geq 0,\qquad f_{2,t}(y+2)=e^{-2/\tau_{2,t}}\,f_{2,t}(y)\quad\text{for }y\geq 0,

or equivalently,
f_{1}(u-1)=e^{1/\tau_{1}}\,f_{1}(u)
for
u\geq 1
and
f_{2,t}(v-2)=e^{2/\tau_{2,t}}\,f_{2,t}(v)
for
v\geq 2
.

Conditional factorization.

Conditional on
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2,1},\ldots,\eta_{2,t}
, each
Z_{i}
is deterministic, and the Poisson draw and mechanism evaluations at distinct time steps are mutually independent. By the chain rule,

\displaystyle\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\;\big|\;\eta_{1},\eta_{2,[t]}\big]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Pr\!\big[a_{i}=-s_{i}\;\big|\;a_{[i-1]}=(-s_{1},\ldots,-s_{i-1}),\,\eta_{1},\eta_{2,[t]}\big].

The prefix
(-s_{1},\ldots,-s_{i-1})
determines the mechanism, dataset, and parameters that the analyst selects at step
i
. Given these, the conditional probability
\Pr[a_{i}=-s_{i}\mid a_{[i-1]}=(-s_{1},\ldots,-s_{i-1}),\,\eta_{1},\eta_{2,[t]}]
depends only on
Z_{i}
, where the probability is taken over the random variables
N_{i}
and
y_{i,j}
, and equals
g_{i}(Z_{i})
by
14
. Therefore,

(9)

\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\;\big|\;\eta_{1},\eta_{2,[t]}\big]=\prod_{i=1}^{t}g_{i}(Z_{i}).

Similarly, the halt-at-
t
probability factors as

(10)

\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t-1},s_{t})\;\big|\;\eta_{1},\eta_{2,[t]}\big]=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}g_{i}(Z_{i})\right)(1-g_{t}(Z_{t})).

The shift inequality
g_{i}(z)\leq g^{\prime}_{i}(z-s_{i})
from
15
holds for every
\varepsilon_{i}
-DP mechanism, regardless of how the analyst selected it.

All-continuation transcript.

We bound the probability that the algorithm continues through all
t
steps. Integrating over
\eta_{1}
and taking the expectation over the independent per-step noises
\eta_{2,[t]}:=(\eta_{2,1},\dots,\eta_{2,t})
:

(11)

\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\big]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Ex_{\eta_{2,[t]}}\!\left[\underbrace{\prod_{i=1}^{t}g_{i}\!\big(s_{i}(\mu_{1}-x+\eta_{2,i})\big)}_{S}\right]f_{1}(x)\,\mathsf{d}x.

By
15
(item
3
),
g_{i}(z)\leq g^{\prime}_{i}(z-s_{i})
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Evaluating at
z=s_{i}(\mu_{1}-x+\eta_{2,i})
gives
z-s_{i}=s_{i}(\mu_{1}-(x+1)+\eta_{2,i})
, so the term in the integrand in (
11
) is bounded above by

S\leq\prod_{i=1}^{t}g^{\prime}_{i}\!\big(s_{i}(\mu_{1}-(x+1)+\eta_{2,i})\big).

That is,

\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\big]\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\Ex_{\eta_{2,[t]}}\!\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t}g^{\prime}_{i}\!\big(s_{i}(\mu_{1}-(x+1)+\eta_{2,i})\big)\right]f_{1}(x)\,\mathsf{d}x

Let
u=x+1
. Then the integration domain
\{x\geq 0\}
maps to
\{u\geq 1\}
, and we obtain

\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\big]\leq\int_{1}^{\infty}\Ex_{\eta_{2,[t]}}\!\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t}g^{\prime}_{i}\!\big(s_{i}(\mu_{1}-u+\eta_{2,i})\big)\right]f_{1}(u-1)\,\mathsf{d}u.

In the region
\{u\geq 1\}
, the density shift (
8
) gives
f_{1}(u-1)=e^{1/\tau_{1}}\,f_{1}(u)
. Since the integrand is non-negative, enlarging the domain from
\{u\geq 1\}
back to
\{u\geq 0\}
only increases the value of integration, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\big]

\displaystyle\leq e^{1/\tau_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Ex_{\eta_{2,[t]}}\!\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t}g^{\prime}_{i}\!\big(s_{i}(\mu_{1}-u+\eta_{2,i})\big)\right]f_{1}(u)\,\mathsf{d}u

\displaystyle=e^{1/\tau_{1}}\,\Pr\!\big[a^{\prime}_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t})\big].

Halt-at-
t
transcript.

We bound the probability that the algorithm continues through steps
1,\dots,t-1
and halts at step
t
. Writing
x
for the integration variable of
\eta_{1}
and
y
for that of
\eta_{2,t}
, and taking the expectation over the remaining per-step noises
\eta_{2,[t-1]}:=(\eta_{2,1},\dots,\eta_{2,t-1})
:

\displaystyle\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t-1},s_{t})\big]

(12)

\displaystyle\quad=\Ex_{\eta_{2,[t-1]}}\!\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}g_{i}\!\big(s_{i}(\mu_{1}-x+\eta_{2,i})\big)\right)\bigl(1-g_{t}\!\big(s_{t}(\mu_{1}-x+y)\big)\bigr)\,f_{1}(x)\,f_{2,t}(y)\,\mathsf{d}y\,\mathsf{d}x\right].

The proof idea remains the same as above except that we have to treat the halting step differently. In particular, as in the previous case, we first apply the shift inequalities from
15
(item
3
):
for each continuation step
i\leq t-1
, we use
g_{i}(z)\leq g^{\prime}_{i}(z-s_{i})
, and for the halting step,
1-g_{t}(z)\leq 1-g^{\prime}_{t}(z+s_{t})
. Evaluating the shifted arguments:

\displaystyle s_{i}(\mu_{1}-x+\eta_{2,i})-s_{i}

\displaystyle=s_{i}\!\big(\mu_{1}-(x+1)+\eta_{2,i}\big),

\displaystyle s_{t}(\mu_{1}-x+y)+s_{t}

\displaystyle=s_{t}(\mu_{1}-x+y+1).

We perform a simultaneous change of variables:
u=x+1
and
v=y+2
, where the variable
v
deals with the halting step. Then
x=u-1
and
y=v-2
, and the arguments become

\displaystyle s_{i}\!\big(\mu_{1}-(x+1)+\eta_{2,i}\big)

\displaystyle=s_{i}(\mu_{1}-u+\eta_{2,i}),

\displaystyle s_{t}(\mu_{1}-x+y+1)

\displaystyle=s_{t}\!\big(\mu_{1}-(u-1)+(v-2)+1\big)=s_{t}(\mu_{1}-u+v).

The integration domain
\{x\geq 0,\,y\geq 0\}
maps to
\{u\geq 1,\,v\geq 2\}
. Substituting into (
12
) and applying the shift inequalities:

\displaystyle\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t-1},s_{t})\big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Ex_{\eta_{2,[t-1]}}\!\left[\int_{1}^{\infty}\!\int_{2}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}g^{\prime}_{i}\!\big(s_{i}(\mu_{1}-u+\eta_{2,i})\big)\right)\bigl(1-g^{\prime}_{t}\!\big(s_{t}(\mu_{1}-u+v)\big)\bigr)\,f_{1}(u-1)\,f_{2,t}(v-2)\,\mathsf{d}v\,\mathsf{d}u\right].

In the restricted region
\{u\geq 1,\,v\geq 2\}
, we apply the density shifts (
8
):
f_{1}(u-1)=e^{1/\tau_{1}}\,f_{1}(u)
and
f_{2,t}(v-2)=e^{2/\tau_{2,t}}\,f_{2,t}(v)
. Enlarging the integration region back to
\{u\geq 0,\,v\geq 0\}
yields

\displaystyle\Pr\!\big[a_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t-1},s_{t})\big]

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2,t}}\Ex_{\eta_{2,[t-1]}}\!\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}g^{\prime}_{i}\!\big(s_{i}(\mu_{1}-u+\eta_{2,i})\big)\right)\bigl(1-g^{\prime}_{t}\!\big(s_{t}(\mu_{1}-u+v)\big)\bigr)\,f_{1}(u)\,f_{2,t}(v)\,\mathsf{d}v\,\mathsf{d}u\right]

\displaystyle\quad=e^{1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2,t}}\,\Pr\!\big[a^{\prime}_{[t]}=(-s_{1},\dots,-s_{t-1},s_{t})\big].

Overall privacy.

When
\tau_{2,t}=\tau_{2}
for all
t
, the multiplicative factor in both equations (
6
) and (
7
) is at most
e^{1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2}}
. Every reachable transcript of the algorithm is either an all-continuation prefix or a halt-at-
t
transcript for some
t\geq 1
. Summing the per-transcript bounds over all
t
yields
\Pr[\text{output}\in F]\leq e^{1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2}}\,\Pr[\text{output}^{\prime}\in F]
for every measurable event
F
, establishing pure
\varepsilon
-differential privacy with
\varepsilon=1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2}
.
∎

Remark 18

(Sample complexity)
.

Run
Algorithm

1
for
t
steps. At each step
i\leq t
, the algorithm draws
N_{i}\sim\Po(\lambda_{i})
evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{i}(X_{i})
, where
\lambda_{i}=\rho_{i}\exp(\varepsilon_{i}Z_{i})
. The total number of evaluations through step
t
is
N_{\leq t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}N_{i}
.

Conditional on the shared noise
\eta_{1}
and the per-step noises
\eta_{2,1},\dots,\eta_{2,t}
, each
Z_{i}=s_{i}(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,i})
is deterministic, so the counts
N_{1},\dots,N_{t}
are conditionally independent with
N_{i}\mid\eta_{1},\eta_{2,i}\sim\Po(\lambda_{i})
. By Poisson additivity (
10
),
