N_{\leq t}\mid\eta_{1},\eta_{2,[t]}\;\sim\;\Po\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{t}\lambda_{i}\right).

Since
Z_{i}
depends on
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2,i}
but not on
\eta_{2,j}
for
j\neq i
, the conditional total mean
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\lambda_{i}
depends on
\eta_{1}
through every term, and on each
\eta_{2,i}
only through the
i
-th term.

4.
Accuracy Guarantees

The privacy guarantee of the
GTM
holds for adaptive choices of threshold parameter
c_{t}
and base rate
\rho_{t}
. We now show how to set these parameters to achieve concrete accuracy guarantees. In
Theorem

21
, we derive a uniform high probability accuracy guarantee, and in
Theorem

23
we derive a similar accuracy guarantee, but under an ex-post privacy guarantee. Both of these results follow from a technical lemma,
20
.

To motivate
20
, consider a step with
s_{t}=+1
(above threshold). If
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, we want the algorithm to halt.
The algorithm generates a count
K_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t}\,p_{t})
and halts if
K_{t}>c_{t}
. Since
\lambda_{t}=\rho_{t}\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})
, there is inherent uncertainty in the value of
\lambda_{t}
due to the privatizing noise
Z_{t}=s_{t}(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t})
.
We call the event
\mathcal{G}
the event that certain high-probability bounds hold on the value of
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2,t}
, see
19
.

Now, conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
, there is a value
\Lambda_{t}>1
such that
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t},\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}]
.
To ensure that the probability of a Type II error (missed halt) is at most
\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
, we need that even under a worst-case draw of
\lambda_{t}
, the thinned Poisson mean
\lambda_{t}\,p^{*}_{t}
is large enough so that
\Pr[K_{t}\leq c_{t}]
is small; by the left tail bound of Poisson distribution (i.e., item
3a
in
10
), this requires
\lambda_{t}\,p^{*}_{t}\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
. If
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, then conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
, since
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}
, it suffices that
\rho_{t}\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
for
K_{t}>c_{t}
with probability
1-\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
. In particular, combining
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
and
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}
, if
\rho_{t}\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
, then we get the inequality:

\lambda_{t}\,\geq\,\rho_{t}\,\geq\,\frac{\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})}{p^{*}_{t}}\,\geq\,\frac{\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})}{p_{t}}.

Conversely, to ensure that the probability of a Type I error (i.e., false halt) is at most
\beta^{\mathrm{I}}
when
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}
for some
\bar{p_{t}}<p^{*}_{t}
, we need that for any value of
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t},\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}]
, the mean
\lambda_{t}\,\bar{p}_{t}
is small enough that
\Pr[K_{t}>c_{t}]
is small. By item
3b
in
10
, this requires
\lambda_{t}\,\bar{p}_{t}\leq\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})
. Again, conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
, this implies that for continuation, it suffices that

\bar{p}_{t}\leq\frac{\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})}{\rho_{t}\,\Lambda_{t}}.

We now have two requirements: the halting condition
\rho_{t}\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
, and the continuation condition
\bar{p}_{t}\leq\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})/(\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t})
. Since larger
\rho_{t}
means higher sample complexity (the Poisson mean
\lambda_{t}=\rho_{t}\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})
scales linearly with
\rho_{t}
), we set
\rho_{t}
as small as possible, namely
\rho_{t}=\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
. Substituting into the continuation condition, the rejection threshold below which the test is guaranteed to continue becomes

\bar{p}_{t}=\frac{\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})}{\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}}=\frac{\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})}{\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})}\cdot\frac{p^{*}_{t}}{\Lambda_{t}}.

This factorization isolates the two sources of accuracy loss. The factor
p^{*}_{t}/\Lambda_{t}
is the cost of privacy: it reflects the worst-case multiplicative perturbation of
\lambda_{t}
by the noise
\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})
, and we show in
Section

6
that it is unavoidable up to constant factors in the exponent. The ratio
\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})/\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\leq 1
is the cost of finite-sample Poisson testing: both thresholds grow linearly in
c_{t}
, but their gap grows sublinearly, so the ratio approaches
1
as
c_{t}\to\infty
.
20
makes this precise: for any target ratio
\gamma\in(1,2]
, it constructs a value of
c_{t}
such that
\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\leq\gamma\cdot\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})
, giving
\bar{p}_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t})
. The required base rate scales as
\rho_{t}=O((\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{I}})+\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{II}}))/((\gamma-1)^{2}p^{*}_{t}))
.

The case
s_{t}=-1
(below threshold) is analogous, with
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t}/\Lambda_{t},\rho_{t}]
(conditioned on high probability bounds on
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2,t}
), and with the roles of the left and right tails exchanged: halting uses the right tail (with parameter
\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
) and continuation uses the left tail (with parameter
\beta^{\mathrm{I}}
).

In this description, we have described the above threshold variant of
Algorithm

1
, where for every step
t
,
s_{t}=+1
; the below threshold analysis is entirely analogous. However, there is another consequence of this functionality.

The choice of direction parameter
s_{t}
gives us two ways of running the algorithm to solve the standard above threshold formulation of the generalized private testing problem, where we are required to halt for
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
.

(1)

Direct variant:
We set
s_{t}=+1
for all
t\geq 1
, and run the mechanism as is

(2)

Flipped variant:
We set
s_{t}=-1
for all
t\geq 1
, and feed the
GTM
the ‘flipped’ input mechanisms
-\mathcal{M}_{t}
with success probabilities
q_{t}=1-p_{t}
, a flipped target success threshold
q^{*}_{t}=1-p^{*}_{t}
.

For the second formulation, we see that the
GTM
halts when
s_{t}=-1
, indicating that
q_{t}\leq q^{*}_{t}\Leftrightarrow p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, so clearly both variants solve the same problem.

We find that the flipped variant offers better accuracy when
p^{*}_{t}
is close to
1
. From the description above, we see that the direct variant with
s_{t}=+1
for all
t\geq 1
achieves a rejection threshold of
\bar{p}_{t}=p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\,\Lambda_{t})
, for some
\gamma,\Lambda_{t}>1
. It follows that
p^{*}_{t}-\bar{p}_{t}=p^{*}_{t}(1-1/\gamma\Lambda_{t})
, i.e the gap between the thresholds is large when
p^{*}_{t}
approaches
1
, but small when
p^{*}_{t}
approaches
0
. Similarly, for the flipped variant, we get that
\bar{q}_{t}=q^{*}_{t}/\gamma\Lambda_{t}
, and the gap between the thresholds is large when
q^{*}_{t}
approaches
1
and small when
q^{*}_{t}
approaches
0
. However, since
q^{*}_{t}=1-p^{*}_{t}
, this implies that the flipped variant has a smaller threshold gap when the given value of
p^{*}_{t}
approaches
1
. By choosing the variant adaptively that maximizes our accuracy, we can achieve a tight accuracy for both regimes, i.e. when
p^{*}_{t}
approaches
0

and
when
p^{*}_{t}
approaches
1
.

Lemma 19

(Bounding
\lambda_{t}
)
.

Run Algorithm
1
with parameters
\tau_{1}>0
and
\mu_{1}>0
, per-step noise scales
\tau_{2,t}>0
, and per-step privacy parameters
\varepsilon_{t}\geq 0
. Fix a constant
c_{\mathcal{G}}\leq 1
, and define the
good noise event

\mathcal{G}\;:=\;\bigl\{\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}\bigr\}\;\cap\;\bigcap_{t\geq 1}\bigl\{\eta_{2,t}\leq\tau_{2,t}\ln(1/c_{\mathcal{G}}\beta_{t})\bigr\}.

Then the following hold.

(1)

Failure probability.

\Pr[\mathcal{G}^{c}]\leq e^{-\mu_{1}/\tau_{1}}+c_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\beta_{t}
.

(2)

Noise range conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
.

Define
\Lambda_{t}:=\exp\!\bigl(\varepsilon_{t}(\mu_{1}+\tau_{2,t}\ln(1/(c_{\mathcal{G}}\beta_{t})))\bigr)
.
Conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
, the signed noise satisfies
s_{t}Z_{t}\in[0,\;\mu_{1}+\tau_{2,t}\ln(1/(c_{\mathcal{G}}\beta_{t}))]
, and the Poisson mean satisfies

\displaystyle s_{t}=+1

\displaystyle\implies\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t},\;\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}],

\displaystyle s_{t}=-1

\displaystyle\implies\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t}/\Lambda_{t},\;\rho_{t}].

(3)

Halting range on
\{\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}\}
.

Conditioned on the event
\{\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}\}
alone (and not on all of
\mathcal{G}
),
s_{t}Z_{t}\geq 0
for all
t
, and therefore
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}
when
s_{t}=+1
and
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}
when
s_{t}=-1
.

Proof.

By the tail bound of the exponential distribution,
\Pr[\eta_{1}>\mu_{1}]=e^{-\mu_{1}/\tau_{1}}
and
\Pr[\eta_{2,t}>\tau_{2,t}\ln(1/c_{\mathcal{G}}\beta_{t})]=c_{\mathcal{G}}\beta_{t}
for each
t
. A union bound gives item 1.

For Items 2 and 3, recall that
Z_{t}=s_{t}(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t})
and
s_{t}\in\{-1,1\}
. Therefore,
s_{t}Z_{t}=\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t}
. When
\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}
, the lower bound
s_{t}Z_{t}\geq\eta_{2,t}\geq 0
holds since
\eta_{2,t}
has nonnegative support and the rest of Item 3 holds by the definition of
\lambda_{t}
. On
\mathcal{G}
, the upper bound
s_{t}Z_{t}\leq\mu_{1}+\tau_{2,t}\ln(1/(c_{\mathcal{G}}\beta_{t}))
holds by the per-step noise constraint. Exponentiating and recalling
\lambda_{t}=\rho_{t}\exp(\varepsilon_{t}Z_{t})
gives the stated ranges.
∎

Lemma 20

(Parameter selection)
.

Fix a target threshold
p^{*}_{t}\in(0,1)
, per-step error parameters
\beta^{\mathrm{I}},\beta^{\mathrm{II}}\in(0,1)
, a parameter
\Lambda\geq 1
, and a target ratio
\gamma\in(1,2]
. Let

p\in(0,1),\quad\lambda>0,\quad\mbox{and}\quad K\sim\Po(\lambda\,p).

For
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
, let
c_{\alpha,\beta}
be defined as in
11
, i.e. for
A=\ln(1/\alpha)
and
B=\ln(1/\beta)
,
c_{\alpha,\beta}=\max(\lfloor 2\max(A,B)\rfloor,\lceil r^{2}\rceil)
, where

r=\frac{\sqrt{2A}+\gamma\sqrt{2B}+\sqrt{(\sqrt{2A}+\gamma\sqrt{2B})^{2}+8(\gamma-1)A}}{2(\gamma-1)}.

(1)

Above threshold
(
s=+1
).

Let
(\alpha,\beta)=(\beta^{\mathrm{II}},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})
, and let
c=c_{\alpha,\beta}
. Let
\rho=\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
. Then the following statements hold:

(a)

Halting:
If
\lambda\geq\rho
and
p\geq p^{*}_{t}
, then
\Pr[K\leq c]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
.

(b)

Continuation:
If
\lambda\leq\rho\Lambda
and
p\leq p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda)
, then
\Pr[K\geq c+1]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{I}}
.

(2)

Below threshold
(
s=-1
).

Let
(\alpha,\beta)=(\beta^{\mathrm{I}},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
and let
c=c_{\alpha,\beta}
. Set
\rho=\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
.

(a)

Halting:
If
\lambda\leq\rho
and
p\leq p^{*}_{t}
, then
\Pr[K\geq c+1]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
.

(b)

Continuation:
If
\lambda\geq\rho/\Lambda
and
p\geq\gamma\Lambda p^{*}_{t}
, then
\Pr[K\leq c]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{I}}
.

Further, in both cases (i.e.,
s\in\{+1,-1\}
), we have the base rate upper bound
\rho=O\!\big(\tfrac{\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{I}})+\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{II}})}{(\gamma-1)^{2}\,p^{*}_{t}}\big)
, and the base rate lower bound that if
\beta^{\mathrm{I}},\beta^{\mathrm{II}}\leq 1/e
, then
\rho\geq\tfrac{1}{p^{*}_{t}}\max\!\big\{\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{I}}),\,\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\big\}
.

Proof.

We prove each of the parts separately.

Above threshold.

Since
c
is defined via
11
with
(\alpha,\beta)=(\beta^{\mathrm{II}},\beta^{\mathrm{I}})
, it follows from
11
that
\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\leq\gamma\,\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{I}})
and
c+1>2\max(L^{\mathrm{I}},L^{\mathrm{II}})
, where
L^{\mathrm{I}}=\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{I}})
and
L^{\mathrm{II}}=\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
. Since we set
\rho=\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
, rearranging,

\rho\,p^{*}_{t}=\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}}).

Halting.

If
p\geq p^{*}_{t}
and
\lambda\geq\rho
, then
\lambda\,p\geq\rho\,p^{*}_{t}=\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
. By
10
(3a),
\Pr[K\leq c]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
.

Continuation.

If
p\leq p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda)
, and
\lambda\leq\rho\Lambda
, then

\lambda\,p\leq\rho\,p^{*}_{t}/\gamma=\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/\gamma\leq\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{I}}).

The hypothesis
c+1>2\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{I}})
ensures the domain condition of
10
(3b), giving
\Pr[K\geq c+1]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{I}}
.

Below threshold.

Since
c
is defined via
11
with
(\alpha,\beta)=(\beta^{\mathrm{I}},\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
, it follows from
11
that
\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{I}})\leq\gamma\,\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
and
c+1>2\max(L^{\mathrm{I}},L^{\mathrm{II}})
where
L^{\mathrm{I}}=\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{I}})
and
L^{\mathrm{II}}=\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
. Since we set
\rho=\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
, rearranging,

\rho\,p^{*}_{t}=\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}}).

Halting.

If
p\leq p^{*}_{t}
and
\lambda\leq\rho
, then
\lambda\,p\leq\rho\,p^{*}_{t}=\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
. Since
c+1>2\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{II}})
the domain condition of
10
(3b) is fulfilled, and so
\Pr[K\geq c+1]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
.

Continuation.

If
p\geq\gamma\Lambda p^{*}_{t}

and
\lambda\geq\rho/\Lambda
, then

\lambda\,p\geq\gamma\,\rho\,p^{*}_{t}=\gamma\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{I}}).

By item
3a
in
10
,
\Pr[K\leq c]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{I}}
.

Base rate bounds

Upper bound.

In the above-threshold setting,
\rho=\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
and
\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})=O(c+\sqrt{c\,L^{\mathrm{II}}}+L^{\mathrm{II}})
. In the below-threshold setting,
\rho=\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})/p^{*}_{t}
and
\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\leq c+1
. In both cases,
c=O((L^{\mathrm{I}}+L^{\mathrm{II}})/(\gamma-1)^{2})
by
11
(3), and dividing by
p^{*}_{t}
gives the stated bounds.

Lower bound.

Write
L^{\mathrm{I}}=\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{I}})\geq 1
and

L^{\mathrm{II}}=\ln(1/\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\geq 1
.
From (
3
),

\lambda^{\ell}(c,\alpha)\geq c+2\ln(1/\alpha)

for all
c\geq 0
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
since
\sqrt{2c\ln(1/\alpha)+\ln^{2}(1/\alpha)}\geq\ln(1/\alpha)
.
From
11
(2) and integrality of
c
,

c\geq 2\max(L^{\mathrm{I}},L^{\mathrm{II}})-1
.

In the above threshold setting,
\rho\,p^{*}_{t}=\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\geq c+2L^{\mathrm{II}}\geq(2L^{\mathrm{I}}-1)+2L^{\mathrm{II}}
.
Since
L^{\mathrm{I}}\geq 1
, this gives

\rho\,p^{*}_{t}\geq L^{\mathrm{I}}+2L^{\mathrm{II}}\geq\max(L^{\mathrm{I}},L^{\mathrm{II}})
.

In the below threshold setting, by
11
(1),

\gamma\,\rho\,p^{*}_{t}=\gamma\,\lambda^{r}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{II}})\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c,\beta^{\mathrm{I}})\geq c+2L^{\mathrm{I}}\geq(2L^{\mathrm{II}}-1)+2L^{\mathrm{I}}
.
Dividing by
\gamma\leq 2
gives

\rho\,p^{*}_{t}\geq L^{\mathrm{I}}+L^{\mathrm{II}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\geq\max(L^{\mathrm{I}},L^{\mathrm{II}})
,
since
\min(L^{\mathrm{I}},L^{\mathrm{II}})\geq 1>\tfrac{1}{2}
.
∎

4.1.
Global accuracy guarantee

Theorem

21
instantiates
17
,
19
, and
20
with specific choices of
\gamma
,
\beta^{\mathrm{I}}
,
\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
, and
\Lambda_{t}
determined by the privacy budget and failure probability allocation, and establishes a worst-case accuracy bound for
Algorithm

1
.

Theorem 21

(Global utility)
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
,
\theta>0
, a gap parameter
\gamma\in(1,2]
, and a failure probability
\beta\in(0,1)
. Define

\Lambda_{t}\;:=\;\left(\frac{4}{\beta}\right)^{(\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon)(1+2/\theta)}\left(\frac{4}{\beta_{t}}\right)^{(\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon)(2+\theta)}.

For any choice of
p^{*}_{t}
,
s_{t}
,
\rho_{t}
and
c_{t}
,
Algorithm

1
is
\varepsilon
-differentially private. Further, there exists a choice of parameter settings for
s_{t}
,
\rho_{t}
and
c_{t}
such that, with probability at least
1-\beta
, the following hold simultaneously for all
t\geq 1
:

(1)

Halting.

At every step
t
where
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, the algorithm halts.

(2)

Continuation.

The algorithm continues past every step
t
satisfying
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}
, where the
rejection threshold

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{t}\;:=\;\max\!\left(\frac{p^{*}_{t}}{\gamma\,\Lambda_{t}},\;\;1-\gamma\,\Lambda_{t}\,(1-p^{*}_{t})\right).

(3)

Sample complexity.

The number of evaluations of the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at step
t
is

N_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t})
for

\lambda_{t}\leq\tilde{A}_{t}\cdot w_{t}
, where

\tilde{A}_{t}\;:=\;\frac{C_{A}\cdot\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\max\!\left(\frac{(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}}{p^{*}_{t}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}\right)

for some universal constant
C_{A}
and
w_{t}:=e^{\varepsilon_{t}\eta_{2,t}}\sim\Par(\sigma_{t})
with

\sigma_{t}:=\varepsilon/(\varepsilon_{t}(\theta+2))
, independently across steps.

Proof of
Theorem

21
.

We run Algorithm
1
with the following parameter setting.
For
\theta>0
, define

\frac{1}{\tau_{1}}=\frac{\theta\varepsilon}{\theta+2},\qquad\frac{2}{\tau_{2}}=\frac{2\varepsilon}{\theta+2},\qquad\forall t\ \ \tau_{2,t}=\tau_{2}\qquad\mu_{1}=\tau_{1}\ln\!\bigl(\tfrac{4}{\beta}\bigr).

Privacy.

Since
1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2}=\varepsilon
and
\tau_{2,t}=\tau_{2}
for all
t
,
17
gives pure
\varepsilon
-differential privacy.

Bounding
\lambda_{t}
.

Applying
19
with
c_{\mathcal{G}}=1/4
, since
e^{-\mu_{1}/\tau_{1}}=\beta/4
and
c_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{t}\beta_{t}=\beta/4
, we can write

\Pr[\mathcal{G}^{c}]\leq\frac{\beta}{4}+\frac{\beta}{4}=\frac{\beta}{2}.

From the definition of
\Lambda_{t}
in
19
and using that
\mu_{1}=\tau_{1}\ln(4/\beta)
, we can write

\Lambda_{t}=\exp\!\bigl(\varepsilon_{t}(\mu_{1}+\tau_{2,t}\ln(4/\beta_{t}))\bigr)=\left(\frac{4}{\beta}\right)^{\tau_{1}\varepsilon_{t}}\left(\frac{4}{\beta_{t}}\right)^{\tau_{2,t}\varepsilon_{t}}

From the definition of
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2,t}
, we have that

\tau_{1}\varepsilon_{t}=\frac{\varepsilon_{t}}{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{2}{\theta}+1\right)\quad\tau_{2,t}\varepsilon_{t}=\frac{\varepsilon_{t}}{\varepsilon}\left(2+\theta\right)\quad\Rightarrow\Lambda_{t}=\left(\frac{4}{\beta}\right)^{(\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon)(1+2/\theta)}\left(\frac{4}{\beta_{t}}\right)^{(\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon)(2+\theta)}.

Now,
19
shows that conditioning on
\mathcal{G}
,
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t},\,\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}]
when
s_{t}=+1
and
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t}/\Lambda_{t},\,\rho_{t}]
when
s_{t}=-1
. When
\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}
, then
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}
when
s_{t}=+1
and
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}
when
s_{t}=-1
.

Accuracy.

Conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
, we know that for all time-steps
t
, for any choice of
\rho_{t}
, if
s_{t}=+1
then
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t},\rho_{t}\,\Lambda_{t}]
, and if
s_{t}=-1
then
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t}/\Lambda_{t},\rho_{t}]
.

If
s_{t}=+1
: applying item
1
of
20
with target
p^{*}_{t}
, error parameters
(\beta_{t}/4,\beta_{t}/4)
, gap
\gamma
, and parameter
\Lambda=\Lambda_{t}
gives us that there exists an integer
c_{t}
such that for
\rho_{t}=\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/4)/p^{*}_{t}
, we have

(1)

Halting: if
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}
and
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, then
\Pr[K_{t}\leq c_{t}]\leq\beta_{t}/4
.

(2)

Continuation: if
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}
and
p_{t}\leq p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t})
, then
\Pr[K_{t}\geq c_{t}+1]\leq\beta_{t}/4
.

(3)

Base rate upper bound
\rho_{t}=O(\ln(1/\beta_{t})/((\gamma-1)^{2}p^{*}_{t}))
, and lower bound
\rho_{t}\geq(1/p^{*}_{t})\ln(4/\beta_{t})
.

Similarly, if
s_{t}=-1
: item
2
of
20
applied to the
negated mechanism with target
1-p^{*}_{t}
, error parameters

(\beta_{t}/4,\beta_{t}/4)
, gap
\gamma
, parameter
\Lambda=\Lambda_{t}

gives us that there exists an integer
c_{t}
such that for

\rho_{t}=\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/4)/(1-p^{*}_{t})
, we have

(1)

Halting: if
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}
and
1-p_{t}\leq 1-p^{*}_{t}\Leftrightarrow p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, then
\Pr[K_{t}\geq c_{t}+1]\leq\beta_{t}/4
.

(2)

Continuation: if
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}/\Lambda_{t}
and

1-p_{t}\geq\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})\Leftrightarrow p_{t}\leq 1-\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})
, then

\Pr[K_{t}\leq c_{t}]\leq\beta_{t}/4
.

(3)

Base rate:

\rho_{t}=O(\ln(1/\beta_{t})/((\gamma-1)^{2}(1-p^{*}_{t})))
, and lower bound
\rho_{t}\geq(1/(1-p^{*}_{t}))\ln(4/\beta_{t})
.

Flipping rule.

The direct execution guarantees continuation at
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
for
p_{t}\leq p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t})

and the flipped execution for
p_{t}\leq 1-\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})
.
The analyst may select whichever threshold is larger for a better test.
Writing
x=\gamma\Lambda_{t}\geq 1
,

\frac{p^{*}_{t}}{x}-\bigl(1-x(1-p^{*}_{t})\bigr)\;=\;\frac{(1-p^{*}_{t})(x-1)(x-p^{*}_{t}/(1-p^{*}_{t}))}{x}.

Since
x\geq 1
, the sign is determined by

x-p^{*}_{t}/(1-p^{*}_{t})
. It follows that the direct path has a higher rejection threshold when

\gamma\Lambda_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}/(1-p^{*}_{t})
, equivalently

\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})\geq p^{*}_{t}
, and the flipped path
is better otherwise.

Therefore, to achieve the stated accuracy bound, the analyst can therefore set

s_{t}=\begin{cases}+1&\text{where}\penalty\ \gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})>p^{*}_{t}\\
-1&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

achieving continuation at

p_{t}\leq\max(p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t}),\;1-\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t}))=\bar{p}_{t}
.

Sample complexity.

Condition on
\mathcal{G}_{0}:=\{\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}\}\supset\mathcal{G}
,
which holds with probability at least
1-\beta/4
.
On
\mathcal{G}_{0}
,
\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}\geq 0
and

e^{\varepsilon_{t}(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1})}\leq e^{\varepsilon_{t}\mu_{1}}=(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}
.
Write
w_{t}:=e^{\varepsilon_{t}\eta_{2,t}}\sim\Par(\sigma_{t})
with

\sigma_{t}=1/(\varepsilon_{t}\tau_{2})=\varepsilon/(\varepsilon_{t}(\theta+2))
,
independently across steps by
12
(1).

We show that
\lambda_{t}\leq\tilde{A}_{t}\cdot w_{t}
on
\mathcal{G}_{0}

for all
t
, where
\tilde{A}_{t}
is defined below.

On the direct path (
s_{t}=+1
):

\lambda_{t}=\rho_{t}\,e^{\varepsilon_{t}(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1})}\,w_{t}\leq\rho_{t}\,e^{\varepsilon_{t}\mu_{1}}\,w_{t}
.
By the base rate upper bound (
20
),

\rho_{t}\,e^{\varepsilon_{t}\mu_{1}}=O(e^{\varepsilon_{t}\mu_{1}}\ln(t/\beta)/(p^{*}_{t}(\gamma-1)^{2}))
.

On the flipped path (
s_{t}=-1
):
on
\mathcal{G}_{0}
,
\lambda_{t}=\rho_{t}\,e^{-\varepsilon_{t}(\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2,t})}\leq\rho_{t}

since
\mu_{1}-\eta_{1}\geq 0
and
\eta_{2,t}\geq 0
.
By the base rate upper bound,

\rho_{t}=O(\ln(t/\beta)/((1-p^{*}_{t})(\gamma-1)^{2}))
.
Since
w_{t}\geq 1
, we have
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}\leq\rho_{t}\,w_{t}
.

Define

\tilde{A}_{t}\;:=\;C_{A}\cdot\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\max\!\left(\frac{e^{\varepsilon_{t}\mu_{1}}}{p^{*}_{t}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}\right).

On the direct path,
\rho_{t}\,e^{\varepsilon_{t}\mu_{1}}=O(\frac{e^{\varepsilon_{t}\mu_{1}}\ln(t/\beta)}{p^{*}_{t}(\gamma-1)^{2}})
, and on the flipped path,
\rho_{t}=O(\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{(1-p^{*}_{t})(\gamma-1)^{2}})
. Let
C^{\prime}
be the constant for which both of these big-Oh expressions are explicit upper bounds. It follows that for
C_{A}=\max(\gamma\,C^{\prime},2)
,
\lambda_{t}\leq\tilde{A}_{t}\,w_{t}
.

Substituting
e^{\varepsilon_{t}\mu_{1}}=(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}

gives the stated expression for
\tilde{A}_{t}
; since this bound holds conditioned on
\mathcal{G}_{0}
, it holds on
\mathcal{G}
.

Failure probability accounting.

We apply a union bound across all points of failure - the probability of the event
\mathcal{G}
not holding, and the probability of a type I or type II error at any time-step.

\underbrace{\Pr[\mathcal{G}^{c}]}_{\leq\,\beta/2}\;+\;\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\Pr[\text{Type I Error at }t\mid\mathcal{G}]}_{\leq\,\beta/4}\;+\;\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\Pr[\text{Type II Error at }t\mid\mathcal{G}]}_{\leq\,\beta/4}\;\leq\;\beta.

The bound on the first term comes from the application of
19
at the beginning of this proof, and the bounds on the second and third terms come from the application of
20
with type I and type II failure probabilities
\beta_{t}/4
.
∎

Lemma 22

(Sample complexity)
.

Under the hypotheses and parameter settings of
Theorem

21
,
assume
\beta\leq 1/e
, and
let
N_{\leq T}:=\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}
denote the total number of evaluations through step
T
.
The following hold.

(1)

Per-step bound.

Conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
, for all
t\geq 1
, the Poisson mean satisfies

\lambda_{t}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{t}
, where

\bar{\lambda}_{t}\;:=\;\max\!\left(\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\max\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}}{p^{*}_{t}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}\right),\;\;\ln(4/\beta_{t})\right).

In particular,
\Pr[N_{t}>e\,\bar{\lambda}_{t}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\beta_{t}/4
.

(2)

Amortized bound.

Assume additionally that
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon_{1}
and
p^{*}_{t}=p^{*}
are constant across steps,
that
\sigma:=\varepsilon/(\varepsilon_{1}(\theta+2))>1
,
and that
T\geq 2\ln(16/\beta)
.
Define
\Lambda_{0}:=(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
and

R\;:=\;\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(T/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\max\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{p^{*}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}}\right).

Then with probability at least
1-\beta/2
,

\frac{N_{\leq T}}{T}\;\leq\;\frac{2\sigma}{\sigma-1}\,R\;+\;O\!\left(R\cdot T^{1/\sigma-1}\,\beta^{-1/\sigma}\right).

Proof.

Throughout,
C_{A}=\max(\gamma\,C^{\prime},2)
is the constant defined in the proof of
Theorem

21
, where
C^{\prime}
is the base rate upper bound constant from
20
. In particular,
C_{A}\geq\gamma\,C^{\prime}
and
C_{A}\geq 2
.

Item 1.

We show
\lambda_{t}\leq\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\max(\Lambda_{t}/p^{*}_{t},\;1/(1-p^{*}_{t}))
on
\mathcal{G}
, which implies
\lambda_{t}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{t}
since
\bar{\lambda}_{t}
is defined as the maximum of this expression and
\ln(4/\beta_{t})
.

From the proof of
Theorem

21
,
conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
,

\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}
when
s_{t}=+1

and
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}
when
s_{t}=-1
.
By the base rate upper bound from
20
,

\rho_{t}\leq C^{\prime}\ln(t/\beta)/((\gamma-1)^{2}p^{*}_{t})
when
s_{t}=+1

and
\rho_{t}\leq C^{\prime}\ln(t/\beta)/((\gamma-1)^{2}(1-p^{*}_{t}))
when
s_{t}=-1
.

Case
s_{t}=+1
.

The bound on
\lambda_{t}
is:

\lambda_{t}\;\leq\;\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}\;\leq\;\frac{C^{\prime}\,\Lambda_{t}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{p^{*}_{t}\,(\gamma-1)^{2}}.

The flipping rule selects
s_{t}=+1
only when
\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})>p^{*}_{t}
,
which rearranges to
1/(1-p^{*}_{t})<\gamma\Lambda_{t}/p^{*}_{t}
.
Therefore the max is controlled by the first term:

\max\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}}{p^{*}_{t}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}\right)\;\leq\;\gamma\cdot\frac{\Lambda_{t}}{p^{*}_{t}}.

Since
C_{A}\geq\gamma\,C^{\prime}
, we conclude

\lambda_{t}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}\,\Lambda_{t}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{p^{*}_{t}(\gamma-1)^{2}}\leq\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\Lambda_{t}}{p^{*}_{t}}\leq\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\max(\Lambda_{t}/p^{*}_{t},\;1/(1-p^{*}_{t}))
.

Case
s_{t}=-1
.

The bound on
\lambda_{t}
is:

\lambda_{t}\;\leq\;\rho_{t}\;\leq\;\frac{C^{\prime}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(1-p^{*}_{t})\,(\gamma-1)^{2}}.

The flipping rule selects
s_{t}=-1
when
\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})\leq p^{*}_{t}
,
which rearranges to
\Lambda_{t}/p^{*}_{t}\leq 1/(\gamma(1-p^{*}_{t}))\leq 1/(1-p^{*}_{t})
.
Therefore the max equals the second term:

\max\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}}{p^{*}_{t}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}\right)\;=\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}.

Since
C_{A}\geq C^{\prime}
, we conclude

\lambda_{t}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(1-p^{*}_{t})(\gamma-1)^{2}}\leq\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{1-p^{*}_{t}}=\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\max(\Lambda_{t}/p^{*}_{t},\;1/(1-p^{*}_{t}))
.

Tail bound.

Since
\bar{\lambda}_{t}\geq\ln(4/\beta_{t})
by definition,
and
N_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t})
with
\lambda_{t}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{t}
on
\mathcal{G}
,
by stochastic dominance
N_{t}\preceq\Po(\bar{\lambda}_{t})
on
\mathcal{G}
.
By
55
(upper tail),

\Pr[\Po(\bar{\lambda}_{t})>e\,\bar{\lambda}_{t}]\leq e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{t}}
.
Since
\bar{\lambda}_{t}\geq\ln(4/\beta_{t})
,

e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{t}}\leq\beta_{t}/4
.
Therefore
\Pr[N_{t}>e\,\bar{\lambda}_{t}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq\beta_{t}/4
.

Item 2.

The proof proceeds in four stages: we use the Pareto decomposition from
Theorem

21
to write
\sum_{t}\lambda_{t}
as a weighted sum of i.i.d. Pareto random variables, then apply truncation, Chebyshev, and Poisson concentration.

Setup.

By Item 3 of
Theorem

21
,
on the event
\mathcal{G}_{0}=\{\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}\}

(which holds with probability at least
1-\beta/4
),
we have
\lambda_{t}\leq\tilde{A}_{t}\,w_{t}
for all
t
,
where
w_{t}\sim\Par(\sigma_{t})
independently across steps.
Since
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon_{1}
is constant,

\sigma_{t}=\varepsilon/(\varepsilon_{1}(\theta+2))=\sigma
for all
t
,
so the
w_{t}
are i.i.d.
\Par(\sigma)
.
Since
p^{*}_{t}=p^{*}
is constant and

(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon}=(4/\beta)^{(1+2/\theta)\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}=\Lambda_{0}

for all
t
, the expression for
\tilde{A}_{t}
from
Theorem

21
specializes to

\tilde{A}_{t}\;=\;\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\max\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{p^{*}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}}\right).

We bound
\tilde{A}_{t}
from above and below.
First, since
\ln(t/\beta)\leq\ln(T/\beta)
for
t\leq T
,

\tilde{A}_{t}\;\leq\;\frac{C_{A}\,\ln(T/\beta)}{(\gamma-1)^{2}}\cdot\max\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{0}}{p^{*}},\;\frac{1}{1-p^{*}}\right)\;=\;R.

Second, since
C_{A}\geq 2
,

(\gamma-1)^{2}\leq 1
(as
\gamma\in(1,2]
),

\max(\Lambda_{0}/p^{*},\;1/(1-p^{*}))\geq 1

(as
\Lambda_{0}\geq 1
and
p^{*}\leq 1
),
and
\ln(t/\beta)\geq\ln(1/\beta)\geq 1
(as
\beta\leq 1/e
),
we have

\tilde{A}_{t}\;\geq\;C_{A}\cdot 1\cdot 1\cdot 1\;\geq\;2.

Define
B:=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}
and
A_{\max}:=\max_{t\leq T}\tilde{A}_{t}
.
By the two properties above,
A_{\max}\leq R
and
B\leq T\,R
.

Truncation.

Define
W:=(16T/\beta)^{1/\sigma}
and
\tilde{w}_{t}:=\min(w_{t},W)
.
Since
\Pr[w_{t}>W]=W^{-\sigma}=\beta/(16T)
for each
t
,
a union bound gives

\Pr[\exists\,t\leq T:w_{t}>W]\leq T\cdot\beta/(16T)=\beta/16
.
On the complementary event,
w_{t}=\tilde{w}_{t}
for all
t\leq T
, so

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\lambda_{t}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}\,w_{t}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}\,\tilde{w}_{t}
.

Chebyshev.

Since the
\tilde{w}_{t}
are i.i.d. and the
\tilde{A}_{t}
are deterministic on
\mathcal{G}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\Ex\!\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}\,\tilde{w}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\;=\;\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}\,\Ex[\tilde{w}_{t}]\;\leq\;\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}\,B,

where we used
\Ex[\tilde{w}_{t}]\leq\sigma/(\sigma-1)
from
12
(3a).
For the variance, since the
\tilde{w}_{t}
are independent:

\displaystyle\Var\!\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}\,\tilde{w}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\;=\;\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}^{2}\,\Var(\tilde{w}_{t})\;\leq\;\Var(\tilde{w})\cdot A_{\max}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}\;=\;\Var(\tilde{w})\cdot A_{\max}\cdot B,

where
\Var(\tilde{w}):=\Var(\tilde{w}_{1})
is bounded by
12
(3b).
By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\Pr\!\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}\,\tilde{w}_{t}>\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}\,B+u\right]\;\leq\;\frac{\Var(\tilde{w})\cdot A_{\max}\cdot B}{u^{2}}.

Setting the right-hand side equal to
\beta/16
and solving:

u:=4\sqrt{\Var(\tilde{w})\cdot A_{\max}\cdot B/\beta}
.
Since
A_{\max}\leq R
and
B\leq T\,R
,

u^{2}\leq 16\,\Var(\tilde{w})\,T\,R^{2}/\beta
.
By
12
(3b),

\Var(\tilde{w})\leq\frac{2}{2-\sigma}W^{2-\sigma}
when
1<\sigma<2
,

\Var(\tilde{w})\leq 2\ln W+1
when
\sigma=2
,
and
\Var(\tilde{w})\leq\sigma/(\sigma-2)
when
\sigma>2
.
Substituting
W=(16T/\beta)^{1/\sigma}
and dividing by
T
:

u/T=O(R\cdot T^{1/\sigma-1}\,\beta^{-1/\sigma})

when
1<\sigma<2
(and strictly smaller when
\sigma\geq 2
).

Poisson concentration.

Recall
B:=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{A}_{t}
and define
M:=\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}B+u
.
On the event that
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\lambda_{t}\leq M

(which holds with probability at least
1-\beta/16-\beta/16

by the truncation and Chebyshev bounds),
the total sample count

N_{\leq T}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}\sim\Po(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\lambda_{t})\preceq\Po(M)

by Poisson additivity and stochastic dominance.
Since
T\geq 2\ln(16/\beta)
,
we have
M/4\geq T/2\geq\ln(16/\beta)
, so

e^{-M/4}\leq e^{-T/2}\leq\beta/16
.
Therefore
\Pr[\Po(M)>2M]\leq e^{-M/4}\leq\beta/16
,
where the first inequality is by
54
(upper tail) with
u=M
.
On the complementary event:

\frac{N_{\leq T}}{T}\;\leq\;\frac{2M}{T}\;=\;\frac{2\sigma}{\sigma-1}\cdot\frac{B}{T}+\frac{2u}{T}\;\leq\;\frac{2\sigma}{\sigma-1}\,R+O\!\left(R\cdot T^{1/\sigma-1}\,\beta^{-1/\sigma}\right).

Failure probability.

The total failure probability is the sum of four terms:

\Pr[\mathcal{G}_{0}^{c}]\leq\beta/4
,

\Pr[\text{truncation fails}]\leq\beta/16
,

\Pr[\text{Chebyshev fails}]\leq\beta/16
, and

\Pr[\Po(M)>2M]\leq\beta/16
.
The total is at most
\beta/4+3\beta/16<\beta/2
.
∎

4.2.
GTM
with Ex-post privacy guarantee

The drawback of the choice of parameters
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2,t}
, and
\mu_{1}
in
Theorem

21
is the worst-case polynomial decay in accuracy forced by the
\Lambda_{t}
factor, which equals essentially
(t/\beta)^{\Theta(\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon)}
(see
Theorem

21
). In this subsection, we present a different choice of parameters leading to different tradeoffs.
More specifically,
Theorem

23
below circumvents this poor scaling with the length of the stream by allowing the ratio of the output privacy parameter
\varepsilon
to the input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{t}
to scale as
\ln(t/\beta)
. With this scaling, the
\Lambda_{t}
slack factor is bounded from above by a constant.
We describe
Theorem

23
as an ex-post privacy loss guarantee, but it can be achieved in two ways: either (a) the input privacy parameter is forced to decay logarithmically or (b) the output privacy parameter is allowed to grow logarithmically. Both these settings have natural applications.

The setting in (a) is most reasonable for our application in reducing optimization under continual observation to the batch setting, as described later in Section
7
, and gives us a standard uniform privacy loss bound.

The setting in (b) is the standard setting for ex-post privacy loss guarantees, and is well suited for potential applications in hyperparameter optimization. It allows
Algorithm

1
to achieve a privacy loss bound that scales only logarithmically with the length of the stream. Further, if the whole stream is processed without halting at any step, then the privacy loss is constant instead of growing with the input length.

Theorem 23

(Ex-post privacy guarantee)
.

Fix a gap parameter
\gamma\in(1,2]
, a failure probability
\beta\in(0,1)
, privacy parameters
\varepsilon_{C}
and a constant
C_{H}>0
. Define

\Lambda_{t}\;:=\;\left(\frac{4}{\beta}\right)^{\!\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon_{C}}\cdot e^{2/C_{H}}.

Define the rejection threshold

\bar{p}_{t}\;:=\;\max\!\left(\frac{p^{*}_{t}}{\gamma\Lambda_{t}},\;\;1-\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})\right).

There exists a parameter setting for Algorithm
1
for which we achieve an
ex-post
privacy guarantee with privacy loss function

\tilde{\varepsilon}((a_{1},\dots,a_{t}))=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{C}+C_{H}\varepsilon_{t}\ln(4/\beta_{t})&\mbox{ if }a_{t}=s_{t}\\
\varepsilon_{C}&\mbox{ if }a_{t}=-s_{t}\end{cases}.

Further, with probability
1-\beta
, for all
t\geq 1
,

(1)

Halting.

At every step
t
where
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, the algorithm halts.

(2)

Continuation.

The algorithm continues past every step
t
satisfying
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}
.

(3)

Sample complexity.

The number of evaluations of the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at each step satisfies

N_{t}\;\leq\;\begin{cases}\displaystyle O\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}\,\ln(t/\beta)}{p^{*}_{t}\,(\gamma-1)^{2}}\right)&\text{if }\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})>p^{*}_{t},\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle O\!\left(\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{(1-p^{*}_{t})\,(\gamma-1)^{2}}\right)&\text{if }\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})\leq p^{*}_{t}.\end{cases}

Note that, as in the case of
Theorem

21
, the gap parameter
\gamma
controls the trade-off between the rejection threshold and the per-step sample complexity. The sample complexity grows as
\ln(t/\beta)
per step, identical to
Theorem

21
; what improves is the gap between the thresholds
p^{*}_{t}
and
\bar{p}_{t}
.

Proof of
Theorem

23
.

The proof follows the same structure as
Theorem

21
, with the following parameter changes. In the initialization of
Algorithm

1
, set
1/\tau_{1}=\varepsilon_{C}
and
\mu_{1}=\ln(4/\beta)/\varepsilon_{C}=\tau_{1}\ln(4/\beta)
, so that
\Pr[\eta_{1}>\mu_{1}]=\beta/4
. At each step
t
, set
\tau_{2,t}=2/(C_{H}\varepsilon_{t}\ln(4/\beta_{t}))
.

Privacy.

The result follows using
17
and observing that
1/\tau_{1}=\varepsilon_{C}
and
1/\tau_{1}+2/\tau_{2,t}=\varepsilon_{C}+C_{H}\varepsilon_{t}\ln(4/\beta_{t})
for any
t\geq 1
.

Bounding
\lambda_{t}
.

Apply
19
with
c_{\mathcal{G}}=1/4
. Since
e^{-\mu_{1}/\tau_{1}}=\beta/4
and
c_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{t}\beta_{t}=\beta/4
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{G}^{c}]\leq\beta/2
. The choice of
\tau_{2,t}
ensures exact cancellation:

\varepsilon_{t}\,\tau_{2,t}\,\ln(4/\beta_{t})\;=\;\frac{2}{C_{H}},\qquad\Lambda_{t}\;=\;\exp\!\bigl(\varepsilon_{t}(\mu_{1}+\tau_{2,t}\ln(1/(c_{\mathcal{G}}\beta_{t})))\bigr)\;=\;\left(\frac{4}{\beta}\right)^{\!\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon_{C}}\cdot e^{2/C_{H}}.

19
(part (
2
)) shows that conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
, for all time-steps
t
and any choice of
\rho_{t}
, if
s_{t}=+1
then
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t},\rho_{t}\,\Lambda_{t}]
, and if
s_{t}=-1
then
\lambda_{t}\in[\rho_{t}/\Lambda_{t},\rho_{t}]
.
Further, part (
3
) in
19
shows that, when
\eta_{1}\leq\mu_{1}
, then
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}
when
s_{t}=+1
and
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}
when
s_{t}=-1
.

Accuracy.
We analyze the case depending on
s_{t}=-1
or
s_{t}=+1
separately.
When
s_{t}=+1
, then applying
20
(case (
1
)) with target
p^{*}_{t}
, error parameters
(\beta_{t}/6,\beta_{t}/6)
, gap
\gamma
, parameter
\Lambda_{t}
gives us that there exists an integer
c_{t}
such that for
\rho_{t}=\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/6)/p^{*}_{t}
, we have

(1)

Halting: if
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}
and
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, then
\Pr[K_{t}\leq c_{t}]\leq\beta_{t}/6
.

(2)

Continuation: if
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}
and
p_{t}\leq p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t})
, then
\Pr[K_{t}\geq c_{t}+1]\leq\beta_{t}/6
.

(3)

Base rate:
\rho_{t}=O(\ln(6/\beta_{t})/((\gamma-1)^{2}p^{*}_{t}))
.

Similarly, if
s_{t}=-1
, then
20
(case (
2
)) applied to the negated mechanism with target
1-p^{*}_{t}
, error parameters
(\beta_{t}/6,\beta_{t}/6)
, gap
\gamma
, parameter
\Lambda_{t}
gives us that there exists an integer
c_{t}
such that for
\rho_{t}=\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/6)/(1-p^{*}_{t})
, we have

(1)

Halting: if
\lambda_{t}\leq\rho_{t}
and
1-p_{t}\leq 1-p^{*}_{t}
, then
\Pr[K_{t}\geq c_{t}+1]\leq\beta_{t}/6
.

(2)

Continuation: if
\lambda_{t}\geq\rho_{t}/\Lambda_{t}
and
1-p_{t}\geq\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})
, then
\Pr[K_{t}\leq c_{t}]\leq\beta_{t}/6
.

(3)

Base rate:
\rho_{t}=O(\ln(6/\beta_{t})/((\gamma-1)^{2}(1-p^{*}_{t})))
.

Flipping rule.

The direct path guarantees continuation at
p_{t}\leq p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t})
and the flipped path at
p_{t}\leq 1-\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})
. When
\varepsilon_{t}
and
p^{*}_{t}
are constant,
\Lambda_{t}
is time-independent, the flipping rule is the same at every step. Writing
x=\gamma\Lambda_{t}\geq 1
,

\frac{p^{*}_{t}}{x}-\bigl(1-x(1-p^{*}_{t})\bigr)\;=\;\frac{(1-p^{*}_{t})(x-1)(x-p^{*}_{t}/(1-p^{*}_{t}))}{x}=\frac{(1-p^{*}_{t})(x-1)}{x}\left(x-\frac{p^{*}_{t}}{(1-p^{*}_{t})}\right).

Since
x\geq 1
, the sign is determined by
x-p^{*}_{t}/(1-p^{*}_{t})=\gamma\Lambda_{t}-p^{*}_{t}/(1-p^{*}_{t})
. It follows that the direct path has a higher rejection threshold when
\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})\geq p^{*}_{t}
, and the flipped path is better otherwise. The analyst sets
s_{t}=+1
when
\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t})>p^{*}_{t}
and
s_{t}=-1
otherwise, achieving continuation at
p_{t}\leq\max(p^{*}_{t}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t}),\;1-\gamma\Lambda_{t}(1-p^{*}_{t}))=\bar{p}_{t}
.

Sample complexity.

Conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
,
N_{t}\sim\Po(\lambda_{t})
with
\lambda_{t}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{t}
, where

\bar{\lambda}_{t}=\begin{cases}\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t}&s_{t}=+1\\
\rho_{t}&s_{t}=-1.\end{cases}

We claim that
\bar{\lambda}_{t}\geq\ln(6/\beta_{t})
in both cases. On the direct path,

\bar{\lambda}_{t}\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/6)/p^{*}_{t}\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/6)\geq c_{t}+\ln(6/\beta_{t})\geq\ln(6/\beta_{t}),

where the third inequality is from (
3
) and the fourth uses
c_{t}+1>2\ln(6/\beta_{t})
from
11
(2).

On the flipped path (selected only when
p^{*}_{t}\geq 1/2
), the gap condition of
11
(1) gives
\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/6)\geq\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/6)/\gamma
, and since
\lambda^{\ell}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/6)\geq 2\ln(6/\beta_{t})
and
\gamma\leq 2
, we have

\bar{\lambda}_{t}=\lambda^{r}(c_{t},\beta_{t}/6)/(1-p^{*}_{t})\geq\ln(6/\beta_{t})/(1-p^{*}_{t})\geq\ln(6/\beta_{t}).

Conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
,
\lambda_{t}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{t}
, so by stochastic dominance of
\Po(\lambda_{t})
by
\Po(\bar{\lambda}_{t})
, and the upper tail of the multiplicative Poisson Chernoff bound (
55
),
\Pr[N_{t}\geq e\,\bar{\lambda}_{t}\mid\mathcal{G}]\leq e^{-\bar{\lambda}_{t}}\leq\beta_{t}/6
. Substituting the base rates, we get

N_{t}=\begin{cases}O(\rho_{t}\Lambda_{t})=\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\Lambda_{t}\ln(t/\beta)}{p^{*}_{t}(\gamma-1)^{2}}\right)&s_{t}=+1\\
\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{(1-p^{*}_{t})(\gamma-1)^{2}}\right)&s_{t}=-1.\end{cases}

Failure probability accounting.

We apply a union bound across all points of failure - the probability of the event
\mathcal{G}
not holding, the probability of a type I or type II error at any time-step, and the probability of the sample complexity bound failing.

\underbrace{\Pr[\mathcal{G}^{c}]}_{\leq\,\beta/2}\;+\;\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\Pr[\text{Type I Error at }t\mid\mathcal{G}]}_{\leq\,\beta/6}\;+\;\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\Pr[\text{Type II Error at }t\mid\mathcal{G}]}_{\leq\,\beta/6}\;+\;\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\Pr[N_{t}\geq e\,\bar{\lambda}_{t}\mid\mathcal{G}]}_{\leq\,\beta/6}\;\leq\;\beta.

∎

5.
Purification

In this section, we formally prove a simple purification lemma that allows us to convert approximate DP mechanisms to pure ones with minimal perturbation to their accuracy. As mentioned in the introduction, this technique of using randomized response as post-processing to purify approximate-DP mechanisms with finite co-domains is attributed to folklore by
17
. We give complete proofs for ease of reference.

Lemma 24

(Purification)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{X}\to\{+1,-1\}
be an
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{1})
-DP mechanism,
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
a pair of neighboring data sets, and

p=\Pr[\mathcal{M}(X)=+1]\qquad p^{\prime}=\Pr[\mathcal{M}(X^{\prime})=+1].

Define the
\phi
-smoothed mechanism
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{X}\to\{+1,-1\}
that for any input dataset
X^{\prime\prime}
evaluates
a\leftarrow\mathcal{M}(X^{\prime\prime})
and passes
a
through a binary symmetric channel with crossover probability
\phi
. That is, we draw an independent bit
b\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\phi)
and output
-a
if
b=1
, and
a
if
b=0
. Let

\widetilde{p}=\Pr[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(X)=+1]\qquad\widetilde{p}^{\prime}=\Pr[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(X^{\prime})=+1].

. The following statements are true:

(1)

\widetilde{p}=p+\phi(1-2p)
, and if
\phi\leq 1/2
, then
\widetilde{p}\geq\widetilde{p}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow p\geq p^{\prime}
.

(2)

Assuming
\phi\leq 1/2
, if for some
c\geq 1
,

\phi\geq\frac{\delta_{1}}{e^{c\varepsilon_{1}}-1+2\delta_{1}}

then the
\phi
-smoothed mechanism
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is
(c\cdot\varepsilon_{1},0)
-DP.

(3)

In particular, setting

\phi=\frac{\delta_{1}}{e^{\varepsilon_{1}}-1+2\delta_{1}}

we have that
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP and that
|\widetilde{p}-p|\leq\delta_{1}/\varepsilon_{1}
.

Proof.

(1)

By the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
, the output probabilities are convex combinations:

(13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{p}&=(1-\phi)p+\phi(1-p)=(1-2\phi)p+\phi.\end{split}

The mapping
p\mapsto\widetilde{p}
is affine. If
\phi\leq 1/2
, then the coefficient of
p
is non-negative, and this mapping is order preserving, so
\widetilde{p}\geq\widetilde{p}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow p\geq p^{\prime}
.

(2)

Since
\mathcal{M}
is
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{1})
-DP,
p\leq e^{\varepsilon_{1}}p^{\prime}+\delta_{1}
. Since
\phi\leq 1/2
, we are guaranteed that
1-2\phi\geq 0
, and it follows that

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}

\displaystyle\leq(1-2\phi)(e^{\varepsilon_{1}}p^{\prime}+\delta_{1})+\phi

\displaystyle=e^{\varepsilon_{1}}\big((1-2\phi)p^{\prime}\big)+\delta_{1}(1-2\phi)+\phi.

Using the relation
(1-2\phi)p^{\prime}=\widetilde{p}^{\prime}-\phi
, we rewrite this entirely in terms of the smoothed probabilities:

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\varepsilon_{1}}(\widetilde{p}^{\prime}-\phi)+\delta_{1}(1-2\phi)+\phi

\displaystyle=e^{\varepsilon_{1}}\widetilde{p}^{\prime}+\delta_{1}(1-2\phi)-\phi(e^{\varepsilon_{1}}-1).

To ensure strict
c\varepsilon_{1}
-indistinguishability for any
c\geq 1
, we require
\widetilde{p}\leq e^{c\varepsilon_{1}}\widetilde{p}^{\prime}
. Thus, it is sufficient that the residual terms satisfy

\delta_{1}(1-2\phi)-\phi(e^{\varepsilon_{1}}-1)\leq(e^{c\varepsilon_{1}}-e^{\varepsilon_{1}})\widetilde{p}^{\prime}.

Since
c\geq 1
, the right-hand side is non-negative and monotonically increasing with respect to
\widetilde{p}^{\prime}
. Therefore, it suffices for the inequality to hold at the minimum possible valid value, which is
\widetilde{p}^{\prime}=\phi
.

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(1-2\phi)-\phi(e^{\varepsilon_{1}}-1)

\displaystyle\leq(e^{c\varepsilon_{1}}-e^{\varepsilon_{1}})\phi

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\delta_{1}-2\phi\delta_{1}-\phi e^{\varepsilon_{1}}+\phi

\displaystyle\leq\phi e^{c\varepsilon_{1}}-\phi e^{\varepsilon_{1}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\delta_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\phi(e^{c\varepsilon_{1}}-1+2\delta_{1}).

Rearranging for
\phi
yields the generalized bound.

(3)

Substituting
c=1
gives us the relevant expression for
\phi
. The bound on the perturbation follows from a direct calculation:

|\tilde{p}-p|=|\phi(1-2p)|\leq\frac{\delta_{1}}{e^{\varepsilon_{1}}-1+2\delta_{1}}\leq\delta_{1}/\varepsilon_{1}.

∎

Corollary 25

(Approximate DP inputs)
.

Suppose each mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP with
\delta_{t}\geq 0
. For each
t
, apply
24
with
c=1
and crossover
\phi_{t}=\delta_{t}/(e^{\varepsilon_{t}}-1+2\delta_{t})
to obtain a pure
\varepsilon_{t}
-DP mechanism
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
. Run
Algorithm

1
on the purified stream with targets
(p^{*}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
. Then
Theorems

21
and
23
apply to the purified stream, and their guarantees transfer to the original success probabilities
p_{t}
as follows. Write
\phi_{t}<\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
for the purification slack at step
t
.

(1)

Halting
(
Theorems

21
and
23
). The algorithm halts at every step
t
where
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}+\phi_{t}
.

(2)

Continuation
(
Theorems

21
and
23
). The algorithm continues past every step
t
where
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}-\phi_{t}
, where
\bar{p}_{t}
is the rejection threshold from the relevant theorem.

(3)

Privacy and sample complexity
are unchanged.

Since
\phi_{t}\leq\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
, the perturbation to the acceptance and rejection thresholds
p^{*}_{t}
and
\bar{p}_{t}
is asymptotically negligible and all bounds match their pure-DP counterparts up to lower-order terms.

Proof.

By
24
(3), the purified success probability satisfies
|\widetilde{p}_{t}-p_{t}|\leq\phi_{t}\leq\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
. Items 1 and 2 follow from the triangle inequality:
p_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}+\phi_{t}
implies
\widetilde{p}_{t}\geq p_{t}-\phi_{t}\geq p^{*}_{t}
, and
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}-\phi_{t}
implies
\widetilde{p}_{t}\leq p_{t}+\phi_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}
. Sample complexity is unchanged since each purified evaluation requires exactly one evaluation of
\mathcal{M}_{t}
plus one independent coin flip.
∎

6.
Lower Bounds

We establish lower bounds for the generalized private tester by reduction from the classical Above Threshold problem
(
11
, Chapter 3)
. We first prove a per-step lower bound for Above Threshold, then lift it to a multi-step bound by an averaging argument. We finally transfer it to the generalized testing problem via the Laplace mechanism.

Standard threshold test.

Let
\varepsilon>0,\delta\geq 0
and let
\mathcal{X}
denote the data universe with a fixed symmetric adjacency relation. Let
X\in\mathcal{X}
be the private input of the testing mechanism.
A
(standard) threshold test
receives a sensitivity-
1
query
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, a public threshold
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, and outputs
a\in\{-1,+1\}
.
We consider a sequence of such queries
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots
presented in an online fashion, where
a_{t}=+1
denotes halting and
a_{t}=-1
denotes continuation.
We say an
\varepsilon
-differentially private threshold test has
global accuracy

(\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-},\beta)
if, with probability at least
1-\beta
over the algorithm’s randomness, for all
t\geq 1
:

f_{t}(X)\geq\tau+\alpha_{+}\implies a_{t}=+1,\qquad\qquad f_{t}(X)\leq\tau-\alpha_{-}\implies a_{t}=-1.

We first show that for two datasets
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
such that the probability of
M(X_{0})=1
is clearly less than 1/2 and the probability of
M(X_{1})=1
is clearly above 1/2, then
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
must have “large enough” distance
d
in the adjacency relation.

Definition 26
.

(1)

Let
M:\mathcal{X}\to\{-1,+1\}
be an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private mechanism.

(2)

Let
X_{0}=Y_{0},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d}=X_{1}
be a chain of neighboring datasets with
Y_{j}
and
Y_{j+1}
neighboring for each
j
.

(3)

Define
h_{j}:=\Pr[M(Y_{j})=+1]
,
\delta^{\prime}:=\delta/(e^{\varepsilon}-1)
,
u_{j}:=h_{j}+\delta^{\prime}
, and
v_{j}:=(1-h_{j})+\delta^{\prime}
. Note
u_{j}+v_{j}=1+2\delta^{\prime}
for all
j
, and
\delta+\delta^{\prime}=e^{\varepsilon}\,\delta^{\prime}
.

Lemma 27

(Acceptance probability propagation)
.

For all
j\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
:

(1)

e^{-\varepsilon}\,u_{j}\leq u_{j+1}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\,u_{j}
.

(2)

e^{-\varepsilon}\,v_{j}\leq\,v_{j+1}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\,v_{j}
.

Proof.

Item 1.
Since
Y_{j}
and
Y_{j+1}
are neighboring, and
M
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP,
h_{j+1}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\,h_{j}+\delta
, so
u_{j+1}=h_{j+1}+\delta^{\prime}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\,h_{j}+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=e^{\varepsilon}(h_{j}+\delta^{\prime})=e^{\varepsilon}\,u_{j}
. The other bound follows by flipping the role of
Y_{j}
and
Y_{j+1}
.

Item 2.
Similarly, by reasoning about the
-1
output, we have that
(1-h_{j})\leq e^{\varepsilon}(1-h_{j+1})+\delta
, so
v_{j}=(1-h_{j})+\delta^{\prime}\leq e^{\varepsilon}(1-h_{j+1})+\delta+\delta^{\prime}=e^{\varepsilon}((1-h_{j+1})+\delta^{\prime})=e^{\varepsilon}\,v_{j+1}
. As before, the other bound follows by flipping the role of
Y_{j}
and
Y_{j+1}
.
∎

Lemma 28
.

If
\Pr[M(X_{0})=+1]\leq\beta^{\mathrm{I}}
and
\Pr[M(X_{1})=+1]\geq 1-\beta^{\mathrm{II}}
for some
\beta^{\mathrm{I}},\beta^{\mathrm{II}}\geq 0
with
\beta^{\mathrm{I}}+\delta^{\prime}<1/2
and
\beta^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, then

(14)

\displaystyle d\;\geq\;\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\frac{1}{2(\beta^{\mathrm{I}}+\delta^{\prime})}+\log\frac{1}{2(\beta^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime})}\right)-1.

In particular, when
\delta=0
this reduces to
d\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\log\frac{1}{2\beta^{\mathrm{I}}}+\log\frac{1}{2\beta^{\mathrm{II}}})-1
.

Proof.

By assumption,
u_{0}\leq\beta^{\mathrm{I}}+\delta^{\prime}<1/2
.
Since
\beta^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, we have
u_{d}=h_{d}+\delta^{\prime}\geq(1-\beta^{\mathrm{II}})+\delta^{\prime}>1/2
.
Since
u_{j+1}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\,u_{j}
, there exists
k\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
with
u_{k}\leq 1/2\leq u_{k+1}
.
From
u_{k}+v_{k}=1+2\delta^{\prime}
, it follows that
v_{k}\geq 1/2+2\delta^{\prime}\geq 1/2
.

Applying
u_{j+1}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\,u_{j}
from
j=0
to
j=k
gives

\frac{1}{2}\;\leq\;u_{k+1}\;\leq\;e^{(k+1)\varepsilon}\,u_{0}\;\leq\;e^{(k+1)\varepsilon}(\beta^{\mathrm{I}}+\delta^{\prime}),

so
k+1\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{2(\beta^{\mathrm{I}}+\delta^{\prime})}
.

By assumption,
v_{d}\leq\beta^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime}
.
Applying
v_{j}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\,v_{j+1}
from
j=k
to
j=d-1
gives

\frac{1}{2}\;\leq\;v_{k}\;\leq\;e^{(d-k)\varepsilon}\,v_{d}\;\leq\;e^{(d-k)\varepsilon}(\beta^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime}),

so
d-k\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{2(\beta^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime})}
.
Adding the two gives
d+1=(k+1)+(d-k)\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\log\frac{1}{2(\beta^{\mathrm{I}}+\delta^{\prime})}+\log\frac{1}{2(\beta^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime})})
.
∎

We next show a lower bound on any
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private standard threshold test

Theorem 29

(Lower bound for standard private threshold testers)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private standard threshold test with global accuracy
(\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-},\beta)
over
T
steps.
Define
\delta^{\prime}:=\delta/(e^{\varepsilon}-1)
.
If
\beta+\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, then

(15)

\displaystyle\alpha_{+}+\alpha_{-}\;\geq\;\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\frac{T}{2(\beta+T\delta^{\prime})}+\log\frac{1}{2(\beta+\delta^{\prime})}\right)-2.

Proof.

Fix queries
f_{1},\ldots,f_{T}
of sensitivity
1
with disjoint support in the dataset, i.e. the entries of
X
that affect
f_{t}
are disjoint from those affecting
f_{t^{\prime}}
for
t\neq t^{\prime}
. Recall that
\tau
is the public threshold.
Let
X_{0}
be a dataset with
f_{t}(X_{0})=\tau-\alpha_{-}
for all
t
.
For each
t\in[T]
, write
H_{t}(X)
for the event that the algorithm when run on a dataset
X\in\mathcal{X}
reaches step
t
and halts there, i.e.,
a_{[t]}=(-1,\ldots,-1,+1)
.

When
\mathcal{A}
is run on
X_{0}
, global accuracy gives
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Pr[H_{t}(X_{0})]\leq\beta
, so by averaging there exists
t^{*}\in[T]
with
\Pr[H_{t^{*}}(X_{0})]\leq\beta/T
.

Let
X_{1}
be the dataset obtained from
X_{0}
by modifying only the entries in the support of
f_{t^{*}}
so that
f_{t^{*}}(X_{1})=\tau+\alpha_{+}
.
Since
f_{t^{*}}
has sensitivity
1
, the datasets
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are at distance
\lceil\alpha_{+}+\alpha_{-}\rceil
in the adjacency relation.
By the disjointness of the support,
f_{t}(X_{1})=\tau-\alpha_{-}
for all
t\neq t^{*}
.
When
\mathcal{A}
is run on
X_{1}
, global accuracy requires that no error occurs at any step with probability at least
1-\beta
; since the only step with
f_{t}\geq\tau+\alpha_{+}
is
t=t^{*}
, this implies
\Pr[H_{t^{*}}(X_{1})]\geq 1-\beta
.

Define the mechanism
M(X):=+1
if
H_{t^{*}}(X)
occurs when
\mathcal{A}
is run with dataset
X
on the query sequence
f_{1},\ldots,f_{T}
, and
M(X):=-1
otherwise.
This mechanism is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP by post-processing.
Applying
28
to
M
with
\beta^{\mathrm{I}}=\beta/T
,
\beta^{\mathrm{II}}=\beta
, and
d=\lceil\alpha_{+}+\alpha_{-}\rceil\leq\alpha_{+}+\alpha_{-}+1
gives

\alpha_{+}+\alpha_{-}\;\geq\;\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\frac{T}{2(\beta+T\delta^{\prime})}+\log\frac{1}{2(\beta+\delta^{\prime})}\right)-2.\qed

Corollary 30

(Lower bound for generalized private testers)
.

Fix a target threshold
p^{*}\in(0,1)
, a constant input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, and an output privacy guarantee
(\varepsilon,\delta)
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a generalized private tester (
1
) for
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP input mechanisms with global accuracy
\beta\leq 1/2
over
T
steps.
Define
\delta^{\prime}:=\delta/(e^{\varepsilon}-1)
, suppose
\beta+\delta^{\prime}<1/2
, and let
\bar{p}
denote the background threshold of the tester.
Define

\Lambda_{0}\;:=\;e^{-2\varepsilon_{1}}\cdot\left(\frac{T}{2(\beta+T\delta^{\prime})}\cdot\frac{1}{2(\beta+\delta^{\prime})}\right)^{\!\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}.

Then:

(1)

If
p^{*},\bar{p}\leq 1/2
, then
p^{*}/\bar{p}\geq\Lambda_{0}
.

(2)

If
p^{*},\bar{p}\geq 1/2
, then
(1-\bar{p})/(1-p^{*})\geq\Lambda_{0}
.

(3)

If
\bar{p}<1/2<p^{*}
, then
1/(4\bar{p}(1-p^{*}))\geq\Lambda_{0}
.

These bounds imply a simplified unified description in terms of the
odds ratios
of
p^{*}
and
\bar{p}
unconditionally.

\frac{p^{*}/(1-p^{*})}{\bar{p}/(1-\bar{p})}\;\geq\;\Lambda_{0}.

Proof.

Fix sensitivity-
1
queries
f_{1},\ldots,f_{T}
with disjoint support, a dataset
X
, and a parameter
\tau_{L}\in\mathbb{R}
.
For each
t
, define
\mathcal{M}_{t}
by the mechanism that when given input
X
, evaluates
f_{t}(X)
, draws
\nu\sim\mathrm{Lap}(1/\varepsilon_{1})
, and outputs
+1
if
f_{t}(X)+\nu\geq\tau_{L}
, and
-1
otherwise.
Each
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP, and disjoint support ensures that changing the entries affecting
f_{t^{*}}
does not alter
\mathcal{M}_{t}
for
t\neq t^{*}
.
The success probability
p_{t}=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X)=+1]
satisfies

p_{t}\;=\;\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}\,e^{-\varepsilon_{1}(\tau_{L}-f_{t}(X))}&\text{if }f_{t}(X)\leq\tau_{L},\\[4.0pt]
1-\tfrac{1}{2}\,e^{-\varepsilon_{1}(f_{t}(X)-\tau_{L})}&\text{if }f_{t}(X)>\tau_{L}.\end{cases}

Note that
p_{t}\leq 1/2
iff
f_{t}(X)\leq\tau_{L}
.
For
p\in(0,1)
, define the quantile function
Q(p):=\tau_{L}+q(p)
where

q(p)\;:=\;\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{1}}\log(2p)&\text{if }p\leq 1/2,\\[4.0pt]
\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{1}}\log\frac{1}{2(1-p)}&\text{if }p>1/2.\end{cases}

It follows that
p_{t}=p
exactly when
f_{t}(X)=Q(p)
.
Note that
q(p)
is continuous, strictly increasing, and satisfies
q(1/2)=0
.

Note that
f_{t}(X)=Q(p^{*})
gives
p_{t}=p^{*}

and
f_{t}(X)=Q(\bar{p})
gives
p_{t}=\bar{p}
.
With
\alpha_{+}=0
and
\alpha_{-}=Q(p^{*})-Q(\bar{p})=q(p^{*})-q(\bar{p})
, feeding the mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{t}
into the generalized tester
\mathcal{A}
yields an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP (standard) threshold test with global accuracy
\beta
over
T
steps.
Applying
29
:

(16)

\displaystyle q(p^{*})-q(\bar{p})=\alpha_{+}+\alpha_{-}\;\geq\;\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{1}}\left(\log\frac{T}{2(\beta+T\delta^{\prime})}+\log\frac{1}{2(\beta+\delta^{\prime})}\right)-2.

Multiplying both sides by
\varepsilon_{1}
and exponentiating, the right-hand side becomes
\Lambda_{0}
.
We now evaluate
\varepsilon_{1}(q(p^{*})-q(\bar{p}))
in each case. Note that by the definition of the generalized private testing the case
p^{*}<\bar{p}
cannot happen.

Case 1:
p^{*},\bar{p}\leq 1/2
.

\varepsilon_{1}(q(p^{*})-q(\bar{p}))\;=\;\log(2p^{*})-\log(2\bar{p})\;=\;\log\frac{p^{*}}{\bar{p}}.

Therefore
p^{*}/\bar{p}\geq\Lambda_{0}
.

Case 2:
p^{*},\bar{p}\geq 1/2
.

\varepsilon_{1}(q(p^{*})-q(\bar{p}))\;=\;\log\frac{1}{2(1-p^{*})}-\log\frac{1}{2(1-\bar{p})}\;=\;\log\frac{1-\bar{p}}{1-p^{*}}.

Therefore
(1-\bar{p})/(1-p^{*})\geq\Lambda_{0}
.

Case 3:
\bar{p}<1/2<p^{*}
.

\varepsilon_{1}(q(p^{*})-q(\bar{p}))\;=\;\log\frac{1}{2(1-p^{*})}-\log(2\bar{p})\;=\;\log\frac{1}{4\bar{p}(1-p^{*})}.

Therefore
1/(4\bar{p}(1-p^{*}))\geq\Lambda_{0}
.

Unified bound via the odds ratio.

In all three cases, the per-case bound implies a lower bound on the odds ratio
\frac{p^{*}/(1-p^{*})}{\bar{p}/(1-\bar{p})}
. In Case 1, since
p^{*}\geq\bar{p}
and both are at most
1/2
, we have
\frac{1-\bar{p}}{1-p^{*}}\geq 1
, so

\frac{p^{*}/(1-p^{*})}{\bar{p}/(1-\bar{p})}\;=\;\frac{p^{*}}{\bar{p}}\cdot\frac{1-\bar{p}}{1-p^{*}}\;\geq\;\frac{p^{*}}{\bar{p}}\;\geq\;\Lambda_{0}.

In Case 2, since
p^{*}\geq\bar{p}
and both are at least
1/2
, we have
\frac{p^{*}}{\bar{p}}\geq 1
, so

\frac{p^{*}/(1-p^{*})}{\bar{p}/(1-\bar{p})}\;=\;\frac{p^{*}}{\bar{p}}\cdot\frac{1-\bar{p}}{1-p^{*}}\;\geq\;\frac{1-\bar{p}}{1-p^{*}}\;\geq\;\Lambda_{0}.

In Case 3, since
p^{*}>1/2
and
\bar{p}<1/2
, we have
p^{*}(1-\bar{p})>1/4
, so

\frac{p^{*}/(1-p^{*})}{\bar{p}/(1-\bar{p})}\;=\;\frac{p^{*}(1-\bar{p})}{\bar{p}(1-p^{*})}\;>\;\frac{1}{4\bar{p}(1-p^{*})}\;\geq\;\Lambda_{0}.

Therefore, in all cases,

(17)

\displaystyle\frac{p^{*}/(1-p^{*})}{\bar{p}/(1-\bar{p})}\;\geq\;\Lambda_{0}.

∎

6.1.
Sample complexity lower bounds

We now turn to lower bounds on the number of evaluations that any generalized private tester must perform.

Theorem 31

(Expected sample complexity lower bound)
.

Fix
p^{*}\in(0,1/2]
, a constant input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, and output privacy parameters
(\varepsilon,\delta)
with
\delta^{\prime}:=\delta/(e^{\varepsilon}-1)
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a generalized private tester (
1
) with per-step Type I and Type II error probabilities
\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{I}}
and
\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}
at step
t
, with
\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{I}}\leq 1/4
and
\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime}<1/2
.

Then there exists an input instance on which the expected number of evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at step
t
satisfies

\Ex[N_{t}]\;\geq\;\frac{e^{-\varepsilon_{1}}}{4\,p^{*}\,(2(\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime}))^{\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}}.

When
\delta=0
, this simplifies to
\Ex[N_{t}]\geq\frac{e^{-\varepsilon_{1}}}{4\,p^{*}\,(2\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}})^{\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}}
.

Proof.

We use the Laplace mechanism construction from the proof of
30
. Fix sensitivity-
1
queries
f_{1},\ldots,f_{T}
with disjoint support and a threshold
\tau_{L}
, and define
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X)=\sign(f_{t}(X)+\mathrm{Lap}(1/\varepsilon_{1})-\tau_{L})
. As in that proof, construct a chain of datasets
X^{(0)},X^{(1)},\ldots,X^{(k)}
differing only in the support of
f_{t}
, with
X^{(j)}
and
X^{(j+1)}
neighboring,
f_{t}(X^{(j)})=f_{t}(X^{(0)})-j
, and
f_{t^{\prime}}(X^{(j)})=f_{t^{\prime}}(X^{(0)})
for all
t^{\prime}\neq t
. Choosing
f_{t}(X^{(0)})=\tau_{L}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{1}}\ln(2p^{*})
gives
p_{t}^{(j)}=p^{*}\,e^{-j\varepsilon_{1}}\leq 1/2
for all
j\geq 0
. Write
h_{j}:=\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t}\mid X_{t}=X^{(j)}]
and
v_{j}:=(1-h_{j})+\delta^{\prime}
.

Step 1: propagation of acceptance probability.

The mapping
X_{t}\mapsto a_{t}
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP by the definition of a generalized private tester (
1
), and post-processing. The Type II accuracy guarantee gives
h_{0}\geq 1-\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}
, so
v_{0}\leq\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime}
. Set
k=\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{2(\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime})}\rfloor
, so that
e^{k\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{2(\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime})}
. Iterating Item 2 of
27
gives
v_{k}\leq e^{k\varepsilon}\,v_{0}\leq e^{k\varepsilon}(\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime})\leq 1/2
. It follows that
h_{k}=1+\delta^{\prime}-v_{k}\geq 1+\delta^{\prime}-1/2\geq 1/2
.

Step 2: bounding the joint probability of halting with no positive evaluations.

Let
K_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{t}}\mathbf{1}[y_{t,i}=+1]
denote the number of
+1
outcomes among the
N_{t}
evaluations. Let
R
denote all internal randomness of the algorithm at step
t
. Conditioned on
R=r
and the event that all evaluation outcomes are
-1
, the algorithm follows a deterministic execution path: it performs some number of evaluations
n_{\bot}(r)
and produces an output
a_{\bot}(r)\in\{-s_{t},s_{t}\}
, both determined by
r
alone. We can write

\displaystyle\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t},\,K_{t}=0\mid p_{t},\,R=r]

\displaystyle\;=\;\Pr[K_{t}=0\mid p_{t},\,R=r]\cdot\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t}\mid p_{t},\,R=r,\,K_{t}=0]

Since
\Pr[K_{t}=0\mid p_{t},\,R=r]=(1-p_{t})^{n_{\bot}(r)}
and
\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t}\mid p_{t},\,R=r,\,K_{t}=0]=\mathbf{1}[a_{\bot}(r)=s_{t}]
, by the law of total probability over
R
,

\displaystyle\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t},\,K_{t}=0\mid p_{t}]

\displaystyle\;=\;\Ex_{R}\!\left[(1-p_{t})^{n_{\bot}(R)}\,\mathbf{1}[a_{\bot}(R)=s_{t}]\right].

Since
(1-p_{t})^{n_{\bot}(R)}
is non-increasing in
p_{t}
for each
R
, the joint probability is non-increasing in
p_{t}
. In the Laplace construction,
p^{\prime}
can be made arbitrarily small (and in particular smaller than
\bar{p}_{t}
) by taking
f_{t}(X)
sufficiently small. For any such
p^{\prime}\leq\bar{p}_{t}
, the Type I accuracy guarantee gives
\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t}\mid p^{\prime}]\leq\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{I}}
. Therefore:

(18)

\displaystyle\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t},\,K_{t}=0\mid p_{k}]\;\leq\;\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t},\,K_{t}=0\mid p^{\prime}]\;\leq\;\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t}\mid p^{\prime}]\;\leq\;\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{I}}.

Step 3: lower bound on
\Ex[K_{t}\mid p_{k}]
.

Decomposing the acceptance probability at
p_{t}=p_{k}
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\;\leq\;h_{k}

\displaystyle\;=\;\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t},\,K_{t}=0\mid p_{k}]+\Pr[a_{t}=s_{t},\,K_{t}\geq 1\mid p_{k}]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{I}}+\Pr[K_{t}\geq 1\mid p_{k}].

Therefore
\Pr[K_{t}\geq 1\mid p_{k}]\geq 1/2-\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{I}}\geq 1/4
, and since
K_{t}
is a non-negative integer-valued random variable:

\Ex[K_{t}\mid p_{k}]\;\geq\;\Pr[K_{t}\geq 1\mid p_{k}]\;\geq\;\frac{1}{4}.

Step 4: from
\Ex[K_{t}]
to
\Ex[N_{t}]
.

Conditioned on the algorithm’s internal randomness
R=r
, the evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
are i.i.d.
\mathsf{Ber}(p_{k})
, and
N_{t}
is a stopping time with respect to the filtration generated by the evaluation outcomes. By Wald’s equation (see, e.g.,
(
25
, Theorem 12.3)
),

\Ex[K_{t}\mid p_{k},\,R=r]\;=\;p_{k}\cdot\Ex[N_{t}\mid p_{k},\,R=r].

Taking expectations over
R
:

\Ex[K_{t}\mid p_{k}]\;=\;p_{k}\cdot\Ex[N_{t}\mid p_{k}].

Combining with Step 3:

\Ex[N_{t}\mid p_{k}]\;=\;\frac{\Ex[K_{t}\mid p_{k}]}{p_{k}}\;\geq\;\frac{1}{4\,p_{k}}.

Since
k\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{2(\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime})}-1
:

p_{k}\;=\;p^{*}\,e^{-k\varepsilon_{1}}\;\leq\;p^{*}\,e^{\varepsilon_{1}}\,(2(\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}+\delta^{\prime}))^{\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon},

and the result follows.
∎

Remark 32

(Comparison with the amortized upper bound)
.

With
\delta=0
and global accuracy
\beta
, the Type II error satisfies
\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{II}}\leq\beta
at every step, so
31
gives
\Ex[N_{t}]=\Omega(1/(p^{*}\,\beta^{\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}))
per step. The amortized sample complexity upper bound (
22
), for
\sigma\geq 2
) gives
N_{\leq T}/T=O(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma-1}\cdot\frac{(1/\beta)^{\varepsilon_{1}/(\theta\varepsilon)}\,\ln(T/\beta)}{p^{*}\,(\gamma-1)^{2}})
. Both scale as
(1/\beta)^{\Theta(\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon)}/p^{*}
, matching up to the
\ln(T/\beta)/(\gamma-1)^{2}
factor and constants in the exponent on
1/\beta
.

7.
Reduction from Optimization under Continual Observation to the Batch Setting

In this section we present the meta-algorithm
BatchToCO
that converts any batch differentially private optimization algorithm into one that operates under continual observation. We recall the problem setting formally in
Section

7.1
, some technical preliminaries in
Section

7.2
, and the algorithm
BatchToCO
in
Section

7.3
. We prove its privacy and accuracy guarantee in
Section

7.4
and
Section

7.5
, and then show in
Section

8
how to construct various CO algorithms for problems of interest using
BatchToCO
.

7.1.
Problem Setup

Maximization problems.

Let
\mathcal{X}
denote the data universe. An (insertion-only)
data stream
is a sequence of datasets
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots\in\mathcal{X}
such that
X_{t+1}
is obtained from
X_{t}
by inserting a single data element at time
t+1
. Let
\mathcal{Y}
denote a set of candidate solutions. A maximization problem is specified by a function
f:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. We write
\mathrm{OPT}(X):=\max_{Y\in\mathcal{Y}}f(X,Y)
. We consider problems
f
that have
bounded sensitivity
, i.e. for all neighboring datasets
X,X^{\prime}
(differing in one element) and all
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
,

|f(X,Y)-f(X^{\prime},Y)|\leq 1.

Private maximization under continual observation.
We study optimization in the continual observation setting for bounded sensitivity maximization problems
f
that in addition satisfy
data monotonicity
, i.e. for all
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
and all
t\geq 1
,
f(X_{t},Y)\leq f(X_{t+1},Y)
. We write
\mathrm{OPT}_{t}:=\max_{Y\in\mathcal{Y}}f(X_{t},Y)
for the optimum at time
t
. Data monotonicity implies that
\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
is non-decreasing. By bounded sensitivity,
\mathrm{OPT}_{t+1}\leq\mathrm{OPT}_{t}+1
.

The goal is to produce, at every time
t
, a solution
Y_{t}\in\mathcal{Y}
such that, with probability at least
1-\beta
simultaneously for all
t\geq 1
,

f(X_{t},Y_{t})\geq\Phi\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-E_{\mathrm{total}}(t),

where
\Phi\in(0,1]
is a constant multiplicative approximation factor and
E_{\mathrm{total}}(t)\geq 0
is additive error incurred at time
t
. The output sequence
(Y_{t})
must satisfy
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy, where
\varepsilon
and
\delta
are prescribed global budgets. Here two streams
(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and
(X^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
are considered neighboring if for all
t
,
X_{t}\sim X^{\prime}_{t}
.

A multiplicative approximation is a standard relaxation introduced for dealing with optimization problems
f
that are NP-hard, and additive error is a consequence of working in the private setting. Prior work
15
;
2
proves lower bounds showing that for problems like submodular maximization additive error is unavoidable.

Batch DP algorithm guarantee.

We will construct algorithms for private maximization under continual observation by starting with a DP algorithm that works in the static i.e.
batch
setting. A batch DP algorithm
A
takes as input a dataset
X\in\mathcal{X}
, a privacy parameter
\varepsilon>0
, an approximate-DP parameter
\delta\geq 0
, and a failure probability
\beta_{A}\in(0,1)
, and returns a solution
\hat{Y}\in\mathcal{Y}
. The algorithm
A
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP. We consider two kinds of accuracy guarantees:

•

High-probability:
There exists some
\alpha\in(0,1]
and some function
E_{A}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta_{A}
,
f(X,A(X))\geq\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E_{A}(X,\varepsilon,\delta,\beta_{A})
.

•

In-expectation:

\mathbb{E}[f(X,A(X))]\geq\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E_{A}(X,\varepsilon,\delta)
.

We write
E_{A}(t)
as shorthand for
E_{A}
evaluated at the parameters in effect at time
t
.

7.2.
Preliminaries

1. Converting in-expectation guarantees to bounded error probability guarantees.
We use the following lemma to reduce reasoning about algorithms with in-expectation guarantees, to reasoning about algorithms with constant bounded error probabilities.

Lemma 33

(In-expectation to bounded-error conversion)
.

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and let
A
be a randomized algorithm
satisfying
\mathbb{E}[f(X,A(X))]\geq\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E
for some
\alpha\in(0,1]

and
E\geq 0
.
Then for any
c\in(0,1)
, writing
\alpha^{\prime}:=(1-c)\alpha

and
\beta_{c}:=1-c\alpha
,

\Pr\!\bigl[f(X,A(X))\geq\alpha^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E\bigr]\;\geq\;1-\beta_{c}.

Proof.

Set
\tau:=\alpha^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E
.
If
\tau\leq 0
, the bound holds since
f\geq 0
.
For
\tau>0
, we can write

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}[f(X,A(X))]

\displaystyle\;\leq\;\tau\,\Pr[f(X,A(X))<\tau]+\mathrm{OPT}(X)\,\Pr[f(X,A(X))\geq\tau]

\displaystyle\;=\;\tau+(\mathrm{OPT}(X)-\tau)\,\Pr[f(X,A(X))\geq\tau].

In the above, we use that
f(X,A(X))\in[0,\mathrm{OPT}(X)]
unconditionally. Rearranging:

\displaystyle\Pr[f(X,A(X))\geq\tau]

\displaystyle\;\geq\;\frac{\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E-\tau}{\mathrm{OPT}(X)-\tau}

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E-\alpha^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)+E}{\mathrm{OPT}(X)-\alpha^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)+E}

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{c\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)}{(1-\alpha^{\prime})\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)+E}.

Since
\tau>0
implies
E<\alpha^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)
,

\frac{c\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)}{(1-\alpha^{\prime})\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)+E}\;\geq\;c\alpha\;=\;1-\beta_{c}.\qed

2. Generalized Private Selection via Random Stopping
(
21
)
.
We recall one of the variants of the algorithm introduced by
21
for Generalized Private Selection, that we refer to as
ThresholdSelect
for convenience. We refer the reader to the arxiv version of their paper
(
20
)
for the statements and proofs that we refer to here.

ThresholdSelect
takes as input query access to an
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{1})
-DP mechanism
\mathcal{Q}
that for datasets
X\in\mathcal{X}
outputs pairs of elements and scores
(Y,v)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}
. It is also given a threshold
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, and parameters
\xi\in(0,1]
and
\varepsilon_{0}\in[0,1]
. It computes a number of iterations
T
that is the maximum number of times it will query
A
. It then proceeds to iterate over a counter
j\in[T]
, and draw values
(Y_{j},v_{j})\leftarrow\mathcal{Q}(X_{t})
. It checks whether
v_{j}\geq\tau
, i.e. whether the score beats the given threshold, and outputs
(Y_{j},v_{j})
if so. If this check fails, then it performs a randomized check by drawing
b\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\xi)
. If
b=+1
, then this check is considered to have failed, and
ThresholdSelect
halts immediately, outputting
-1
indicating failure. If
b=-1
, then this check has passed and the algorithm continues to iterate over
j
. If all
T
iterations result in no successful threshold test, then the algorithm outputs
-1
.

ThresholdSelect
can be used as a success probability amplification algorithm. When it is known that the probability of
\mathcal{Q}
outputting a value
(Y,v)
such that
v
beats
\tau
with probability at least
p_{1}
, then by setting
\xi
to scale roughly as
\beta p_{1}
, we are guaranteed that
\mathcal{Q}
will output a value that beats the threshold
\tau
with probability
1-\beta
. Further, the privacy cost of this procedure is roughly
(O(\varepsilon_{1}),O(\delta_{1}/\xi))
, in other words the privacy cost of success probability amplification is borne almost entirely by the additive privacy loss parameter in the approximate DP setting, and just the constant factor overhead in the multiplicative privacy loss parameter in the pure DP setting. Further, the sample complexity can in fact be bounded in terms of a Geometric distribution with stopping probability at least
p_{1}
.

Input:

Dataset
X\in\mathcal{X}
, Mechanism
Q:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}
;
threshold
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
;

\xi\in(0,1]
, parameter
\varepsilon_{0}\in[0,1]
.

Output:

A sample
(Y,v)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}
, or
-1
.

T\leftarrow\lceil\max\{(1/\xi)\ln(2/\varepsilon_{0}),\;1+1/(e\xi)\}\rceil
;

for

j=1,\ldots,T

do

(Y_{j},v_{j})\leftarrow Q(X)
;

if

v_{j}\geq\tau

then

return

(Y_{j},v_{j})
;

Draw
b\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\xi)
, if
b=+1
,
return

-1
;

end for

return

-1
;

Algorithm 2

ThresholdSelect
(Algorithm 1, Thresholding with a known threshold
\tau
in the arXiv version of
20
)

Lemma 34

(Thresholded Private Selection
(
20
, Theorem 3.1)
)
.

Let
Q:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}
be an
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{1})
-DP mechanism.
Fix
\xi\in(0,1]
,
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
, and

T\geq\max\!\bigl\{\frac{1}{\xi}\ln\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{0}},\;1+\frac{1}{e\xi}\bigr\}
.

(1)

Privacy.

The output of
Algorithm

2
is

\bigl(2\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{0},\;\;3e^{2\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{0}}\cdot\delta_{1}/\xi\bigr)
-differentially private.
When
\delta_{1}=0
, this simplifies to
(2\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{0},0)
-DP.

(2)

Failure probability.

Let
p_{1}:=\Pr_{(Y,v)\sim Q(X)}[v\geq\tau]
. Then

\Pr[\emph{output}=-1]\;\leq\;\frac{(1-p_{1})(1+\varepsilon_{0}/2)}{p_{1}}\,\xi.

(3)

Sample complexity.
The number of draws from
Q
is at most
T
. Let
p_{1}:=\Pr_{(Y,v)\sim Q(X)}[v\geq\tau]
. The number of draws is stochastically dominated by a
\mathrm{Geo}(p_{1}(1-\xi)+\xi)
-distributed random variable.

Proof.

Items 1 and 2 are parts (b), (c), and (e) of
(
20
, Theorem 3.1)
respectively.
Item 3 follows directly from the proof of part (d) of
(
20
, Theorem 3.1)
; in each iteration, the algorithm makes an evaluation of
\mathcal{Q}
that succeeds with probability at least
p_{1}
. If this evaluation fails, then the algorithm halts with probability
\xi
. These two Bernoulli draws are independent of each other. Therefore, the probability of halting in any one iteration is exactly
p_{1}(1-\xi)+\xi
. It follows that the total number of evaluations is dominated by
\mathrm{Geo}(p_{1}(1-\xi)+\xi)
.
∎

3. Simplified GTM.
We briefly recall a simplified version of
Theorem

23
that we will refer to in our reduction from optimization in the CO setting to the batch setting.

Corollary 35

(GTM with ExPost parameters)
.

Fix a threshold probability
p^{*}\in(0,1)
, a gap parameter
\gamma\in(1,2]
,
and suppose
\beta_{\textsc{GTM}}\leq 1/e
and
C_{1},C_{H}>0
.
There exists a GTM instance (
Algorithm

1
) such that
if
\varepsilon_{t}\leq\varepsilon/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta_{\textsc{GTM}})))

for all
t\geq 1
, then the following hold
with
\bar{p}:=p^{*}/(\gamma\Lambda)
,
where

\Lambda\;:=\;e^{1/C_{1}+2/C_{H}}

is a universal constant (independent of
t
and
\beta_{\textsc{GTM}}
).

(1)

Privacy.

The GTM transcript is
(1+C_{H}/C_{1})\varepsilon
-differentially private.

(2)

Accuracy.

With probability at least
1-\beta_{\textsc{GTM}}
,
simultaneously for all
t\geq 1
:

(a)

the algorithm halts at every step
t

where
p_{t}\geq p^{*}+\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
, and

(b)

the algorithm continues past every step
t

where
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
.

When
\delta_{t}=0
, the thresholds simplify to
p^{*}
and
\bar{p}
.

(3)

Sample complexity.

With probability at least
1-\beta_{\textsc{GTM}}
,
the number of evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at step
t
is at most

O\!\left(\ln(t/\beta_{\textsc{GTM}})/(p^{*}(\gamma-1)^{2})\right)
.

Proof.

Apply
Theorem

23
with
\varepsilon_{C}=\varepsilon
,
\beta=\beta_{\textsc{GTM}}
, and
s_{t}=+1
for all
t
. The hypothesis on
\varepsilon_{t}
gives
\Lambda_{t}=(4/\beta_{\textsc{GTM}})^{\varepsilon_{t}/\varepsilon_{C}}\cdot e^{2/C_{H}}\leq(4/\beta_{\textsc{GTM}})^{1/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta_{\textsc{GTM}})))}\cdot e^{2/C_{H}}\leq e^{1/C_{1}+2/C_{H}}
.
It follows that
\bar{p}_{t}=p^{*}/(\gamma\Lambda_{t})\geq p^{*}/(\gamma\Lambda)
so it follows that
\bar{p}:=p^{*}/(\gamma\Lambda)
suffices for the rejection threshold.
Items 1 and 3 follow directly from
Theorem

23
; the sample complexity uses
\Lambda_{t}\leq\Lambda=O(1)
.
The
\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
perturbation in Item 2 follows from
25
.
∎

7.3.
Algorithm description

We can now introduce
BatchToCO
(
Algorithm

3
) that takes as input black-box access to a mechanism
A
that solves a maximization problem
f:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in the batch DP setting, and constructs a mechanism that solves the same problem in the CO setting. We will show in
42
that
BatchToCO
fulfills the same sort privacy guarantee as
A
, i.e. pure or approximate. Further, it fulfills an error bound for the dataset
X_{t}
that scales with the error incurred by
A
on
X_{t}
when initialized with privacy parameters
\varepsilon_{t}=\Omega(\varepsilon/\ln^{2}(t/\beta))
,
\delta_{t}=\tilde{\Omega}(\delta/\ln^{2}t)
, plus some slow scaling polylogarithmic error terms. Additionally, with probability
1-\beta
, for all
t\geq 1
, the per-round sample complexity, i.e. evaluations of
A
, is at most
\tilde{O}(\ln(t/\beta))
.

Inputs and Outputs.
A run of
BatchToCO
proceeds as follows. It is given as input black-box access to a batch DP mechanism
A:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
to which it can feed the input dataset
X_{t}
at any point
t
in the input data stream, and privacy parameters
\varepsilon_{t}
and
\delta_{t}
. Every time it evaluates
A
, it is guaranteed that the evaluation is
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP. It will output a sequence of values
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 1}\in\mathcal{Y}^{*}
.

Checkpoints.

Algorithm

3
defines a sequence of increasing time-steps called
checkpoints
, denoted
(t(\ell))_{\ell\geq 1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
over the course of the stream. Checkpoints are time-steps at which
Algorithm

3
will update its output solution; at time-steps which are not checkpoints, the solution output will be the solution output at the previous checkpoint.
t(1):=1
, i.e. the first time-step is always the first checkpoint, and at the very beginning of the stream, with the dataset
X_{1}
, we make a call to the batch algorithm
A
to generate the very first output
Y_{1}
. All subsequent outputs will be determined as explained below.

Calls to
GTM
.
After having identified the
(\ell-1)
-th checkpoint,
Algorithm

3
identifies the
\ell
-th checkpoint via an invocation of
GTM
(
Algorithm

1
), with the parameter setting indicated by
35
. We refer to this instance as
\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}
. It defines a
threshold

h_{\ell}=(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
