, and conducts the threshold test:

1(f(X_{t},\hat{Y})+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})-h_{\ell}\geq 0),\qquad\hat{Y}\leftarrow A(X_{t},\varepsilon_{t}/2,\delta_{t}).

In other words, it checks whether the utility obtained from a value
\hat{Y}
drawn from the batch algorithm
A
for the dataset
X_{t}
beats the
\ell
-th threshold
h_{\ell}
. Since
A
is randomized, this is a probabilistic check, and by adding a draw from the Laplace distribution
\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})
, we will have the promise that
f(X_{t},\hat{Y})+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})
is
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP. It follows that we can define a single evaluation of this probabilistic test as the
t
-th input
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
to
\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}
. Since neighboring data streams
(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and
(X^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
fulfill the promise that there is at most one time-step
t
such that the new elements
x_{t}
and
x^{\prime}_{t}
inserted at that time-step are unequal, it follows that
X_{t}
and
X^{\prime}_{t}
are neighboring at all time-steps.

It follows from the privacy guarantee of
\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}
that this invocation fulfills a pure privacy guarantee. We set the output privacy parameter to be a value
\varepsilon_{\ell}
such that
\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\varepsilon_{\ell}<\varepsilon
, and the acceptance threshold
p^{*}=\Theta(1)
, i.e. some constant to be determined later. It follows from
35
, that by setting
\varepsilon_{t}=\Theta(\varepsilon_{\ell(t)}/\ln(t/\beta))
, we are guaranteed that all invocations
(\textsc{GTM}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 2}
are
\sum_{\ell\geq 2}\varepsilon(\ell)
-DP, and it will suffice to set
\varepsilon(\ell)=\Theta(\varepsilon/\ell\ln^{2}\ell)
. This holds even for approximate DP
A
because of the internal purification by
GTM
(see
35
); the additive privacy loss parameters affect only the accuracy of the threshold tests.

With high probability,
\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}
will halt if the test passes with probability at least
p^{*}+\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
, and it will not halt if
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}=p^{*}/\gamma\Lambda-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
, for some user-defined constants
\gamma
and
\Lambda
. Here
p_{t}
is the probability of the
t
-th threshold test passing, i.e.

p_{t}\,:=\,\Pr[f(X_{t},A(X_{t},\varepsilon_{t}/2,\delta_{t}))+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})\geq(1+\kappa)^{\ell}].

To absorb the
\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
terms, we introduce a constraint on
\delta_{t}
to be at most
\varepsilon_{t}\cdot\bar{p}/2
.

Modulo the additive error introduced by the Laplace noise, this implies that if
\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}
outputs
a_{t}=-1
, then
f(X_{t},\hat{Y})+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})
cannot be greater than
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
with probability greater than
p^{*}+\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
. Similarly, if
a_{t}=+1
, then
f(X_{t},\hat{Y})+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})
cannot be smaller than
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
with probability greater than
1-\bar{p}+\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
. Since the
\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
terms are negligible, this guarantee promises that if
\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}
indicates continuation, then it is safe to repeat the previous checkpoint’s solution whilst ensuring accuracy competitive with what the batch algorithm
A
would achieve on the dataset
X_{t}
with privacy parameter
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
. Further, since
a_{t}=+1
only when
p_{t}\geq\bar{p}-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
, and since the thresholds
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
increase exponentially with
\ell
whereas
f(X_{t},\cdot)
can only increase at most linearly with
t
(due to
1
-sensitivity), it will follow that with high probability, only some
O(\ln(t/\beta))
-many checkpoints are defined by time-step
t
. Since checkpoints are declared at a low rate, the privacy parameters passed to the active instance of
\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}
scale slowly, and the invocations of the batch algorithm also get slowly-decaying privacy-loss parameters
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
as
t
increases. Putting everything together, we are guaranteed high accuracy in the threshold tests.

Calls to
ThresholdSelect
.
When a new checkpoint is declared, we know that the previous checkpoint’s solution is no longer competitive with
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
, and a new solution must be generated. If we make a single invocation to
A
and set
Y_{t}
to equal its output, we are only guaranteed that it beats
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
with probability
\bar{p}-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
. To amplify the success probability, we make a call to the generalized private selection algorithm
ThresholdSelect
(
Algorithm

2
) to generate a new solution for release, with threshold set equal to
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
. We will refer to this invocation of
ThresholdSelect
as
\textsc{ThresholdSelect}_{\ell}
. It will follow that as long as
\textsc{ThresholdSelect}_{\ell}
makes at least
\approx 1/\bar{p}
many evaluations, at least one draw will exceed the updated threshold
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
, and this solution is selected and output by
Algorithm

2
. Again, there is some error introduced by Laplace noise added to privatize the score of each draw, but this again turns out to give us a lower order error term.

BatchToCO
guarantees.
To sum up, the privacy guarantee of
BatchToCO
follows by basic composition over the very first call to the batch algorithm
A
, and the subsequent invocations of
(\textsc{GTM}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 2}
and
(\textsc{ThresholdSelect}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 2}
. The accuracy guarantee of
BatchToCO
follows from the accuracy of the tests conducted by the invocations of
GTM
, and the quality of the solutions generated by the invocations of
ThresholdSelect
. The total sample complexity of
BatchToCO
turns out to be essentially
O(\ln(t/\beta))
, due to the sample complexity of the ex-post parameter setting with which GTM is used, and the relatively mild stopping probability
\xi=\tilde{\Theta}(\bar{p})
with which
ThresholdSelect
is invoked; since
\bar{p}=\Omega(1)
, this implies a relatively small number of calls until the threshold check passes and a solution may be output.

The outline of the rest of this section is as follows. In
36
and
37
we define the mechanisms constructed from
A
that we pass to the instances of
GTM
and
ThresholdSelect
, and prove accuracy and privacy guarantees for them.
38
proves that
BatchToCO
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP.
39
shows that when
\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}
outputs
a_{t}=-1
, then the quality of values generated by
A
cannot be much more than
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
, and that if
a_{t}=+1
, then
\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
cannot be much less than
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
.
40
uses the latter guarantee to show that the number of checkpoints scales logarithmically in the length of the stream.
41
shows that when a checkpoint is reached, and a value is generated via
\textsc{ThresholdSelect}_{\ell}
, then its quality is not too much less than
(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
. Finally,
42
puts everything together to formally prove the privacy, accuracy, and sample complexity guarantees of
BatchToCO
, and
43
and
44
show how to derive concrete accuracy bounds when the batch algorithm
A
is known to satisfy an accuracy guarantee with bounded error probability
\beta_{A}
, or just an in-expectation accuracy guarantee, respectively.

We collect the parameters and derived quantities used
in
BatchToCO
and its analysis.
The pseudocode appears in
Algorithm

3
;
the quantities below are referenced throughout
Sections
7.4
and
7.5
.

Input parameters.

•

\varepsilon<1
,
\delta\in[0,1)
: global privacy budget.

•

A
: batch DP algorithm,
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP for any given
(\varepsilon,\delta)
.

•

p^{*}\in(0,1/2]
: target halting threshold for the GTM.
In the high-probability setting,
p^{*}=(1-\beta_{A})/2

where
\beta_{A}
is the failure probability of
A
;
in the in-expectation setting with slack
c
,
p^{*}=c\alpha/2
.

•

\kappa>0
: parameterizes the scaling of the geometric sequence of thresholds
h_{i}=(1+\kappa)^{i}
.

•

\gamma\in(1,2]
: parameter in calls to
GTM
that controls the trade-off between the rejection threshold and per-step sample complexity.

•

\beta\in(0,1/e)
: global failure probability.

Weight sequence.

We fix a sequence
(\omega_{i})_{i\geq 1}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\omega_{i}\leq 1
.
We use
\omega_{k}=C_{\omega}/(k\ln^{2}(k+1))
, for an appropriate normalization constant
C_{\omega}
.

Constants.

The following constants are set to satisfy the privacy
and accuracy constraints established in

38
and
41
:

•

C_{1}>0,C_{H}>0
: control the ex-post privacy decay rate in
35
.The per-step privacy parameter within GTM instance
\ell
is
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon(\ell)/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta)))
.

•

C_{\varepsilon}=C_{1}/(C_{1}+C_{H}+3)<1
: scaling parameter for the per-checkpoint privacy budget, so that
\varepsilon(\ell)=C_{\varepsilon}\cdot\omega_{\ell}\cdot\varepsilon
(see per-checkpoint quantities below). Chosen so that the combined privacy cost of the
\ell
-th GTM instance and the corresponding
ThresholdSelect
call is at most
\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon
(see
38
).

•

C_{\beta}\leq 1/3
: scaling parameter for the per-instance failure probability, so that
\beta_{\ell}=C_{\beta}\cdot\omega_{\ell}\cdot\beta
(see per-checkpoint quantities below). The constraint
C_{\beta}\leq 1/3
ensures that
ThresholdSelect
succeeds with sufficiently high probability at each checkpoint (see
41
).

•

C_{\delta}=1+3e^{3}/C_{\beta}
: scaling parameter for the per-instance
\delta
budget, ensuring that the total additive privacy loss across all checkpoints is at most
\delta
(see
38
).

Derived quantities from the GTM.

From
35
:

•

\Lambda=e^{1/C_{1}+2/C_{H}}
. Depends only on
C_{1}
and
C_{H}
; independent of
t
,
\beta
, and the data.

•

\bar{p}=p^{*}/(\gamma\Lambda)
: the rejection threshold. The GTM continues past step
t
whenever
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
. Since
p^{*}\leq 1/2
and
\gamma\Lambda>1
, we have
\bar{p}<1/2
.

Per-checkpoint quantities.

For each GTM instance
\ell\geq 2
:

•

\varepsilon(\ell)=C_{\varepsilon}\cdot\omega_{\ell}\cdot\varepsilon
: the privacy budget allocated to instance
\ell
.

•

\beta_{\ell}=C_{\beta}\cdot\omega_{\ell}\cdot\beta
: the failure probability allocated to instance
\ell
. By definition of
\omega_{\ell}
,
\sum_{\ell\geq 2}\beta_{\ell}\leq C_{\beta}\,\beta
.

•

\xi_{\ell}=\bar{p}\cdot\beta_{\ell}
: the stopping probability parameter for
ThresholdSelect
at checkpoint
\ell
.

Per-step quantities.

For GTM instance
\ell
at time
t
:

•

\varepsilon(\ell,t)=\varepsilon(\ell)/(C_{1}\cdot\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta)))
: the privacy parameter for evaluations of the test and scored mechanisms at time
t
. The decay in
t
is required by
35
to keep
\Lambda_{t}\leq\Lambda
.

•

\delta(\ell,t)=\min(\bar{p}\,\omega_{\ell}^{2}\,\beta\,\delta/C_{\delta},\;(\bar{p}/2)\cdot\varepsilon(\ell,t))
: the approximate-DP parameter at time
t
. The first term in the min-expression ensures the total additive privacy loss across checkpoints is at most
\delta
; the second ensures
\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}\leq\bar{p}/2
, which absorbs the
\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}
perturbation in the GTM accuracy guarantee.

Batch algorithm parameters at time
t
.

Suppose there are
\ell-1
checkpoints before time-step
t
. The batch algorithm
A
is called with privacy parameters

\varepsilon_{t}/2=\varepsilon(\ell,t)/2
and
\delta_{t}=\delta(\ell,t)
.
Substituting, the effective batch privacy parameter is

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{batch}}(t)=\frac{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon}{2C_{1}\,\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta))},

and
\delta_{\mathrm{batch}}(t)=\delta(\ell,t)\leq\bar{p}\,\omega_{\ell}^{2}\,\beta\,\delta/C_{\delta}=\Theta(\omega_{\ell}^{2}\,\beta\,\delta)
.
We write
E_{A}(t)
as shorthand for

E_{A}(X_{t},\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{batch}}(t),\,\delta_{\mathrm{batch}}(t))
.

Input:

Output privacy parameters
\varepsilon<1
,
\delta\in[0,1)
; batch-DP algorithm
A
;
target threshold
p^{*}\in(0,1/2]
;
parameters
\kappa>0
,
\gamma\in(1,2]
;
failure probability
\beta\in(0,1/e)
.

Output:

At each time
t
, a solution
Y_{t}\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Define for all
i,t\geq 1
:;

h_{i}:=(1+\kappa)^{i}
;

\varepsilon(i):=C_{\varepsilon}\cdot\omega_{i}\cdot\varepsilon
;

\beta_{i}:=C_{\beta}\cdot\omega_{i}\cdot\beta
;

\xi_{i}:=\bar{p}\cdot\beta_{i}
;

\varepsilon(i,t):=\varepsilon(i)\,/\,\bigl(C_{1}\cdot\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta))\bigr)
;

\delta(i,t):=\min(\bar{p}\cdot\omega_{i}^{2}\cdot\beta\cdot\delta/C_{\delta},\,(\bar{p}/2)\cdot\varepsilon(i,t))
;

\varepsilon_{1}\leftarrow\varepsilon(1,1)
;

\delta_{1}\leftarrow\delta(1,1)
;

Y_{\mathrm{cur}}\leftarrow A(X_{1},\,\varepsilon_{1},\,\delta_{1})
;

Y_{1}\leftarrow Y_{\mathrm{cur}}
;

\ell\leftarrow 2
;

\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}.\mathrm{Init}(\varepsilon(\ell),\,p^{*},\,\beta_{\ell})
;

for

t=2,3,\ldots

do

\varepsilon_{t}\leftarrow\varepsilon(\ell,t)
;

\delta_{t}\leftarrow\delta(\ell,t)
;

a_{t}\leftarrow\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}.\mathrm{Step}(\mathcal{M}_{t}^{h_{\ell}},\,X_{t},\,\varepsilon_{t},\,\delta_{t})
;

if

a_{t}=-1

then

Y_{t}\leftarrow Y_{\mathrm{cur}}
;

else

\hat{Y}\leftarrow\textsc{ThresholdSelect}(\mathcal{Q}_{t},\;h_{\ell},\;\xi_{\ell},\;\min(\varepsilon_{t},1))
;

if

\hat{Y}\neq-1

then

Y_{\mathrm{cur}}\leftarrow\hat{Y}
;

Y_{t}\leftarrow Y_{\mathrm{cur}}
;

\ell\leftarrow\ell+1
;

\textsc{GTM}_{\ell}.\mathrm{Init}(\varepsilon(\ell),\,p^{*},\,\beta_{\ell})
;

end if

end for

Algorithm 3

Batch-to-Continual-Observation Meta-Algorithm

Definition 36

(Test and scored mechanisms)
.

Given a batch DP algorithm
A
, privacy parameters
\varepsilon_{t}>0
,
\delta_{t}\in[0,1)
, and a threshold
h>0
, define:

(1)

The
test mechanism

\mathcal{M}_{t}^{h}:\mathcal{X}\to\{-1,+1\}
: on input
X_{t}
, draw
\hat{Y}\leftarrow A(X_{t},\,\varepsilon_{t}/2,\,\delta_{t})

and return

\mathcal{M}_{t}^{h}(X_{t})\;:=\;\sign\!\bigl(f(X_{t},\hat{Y})+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})-h\bigr).

(2)

The
scored mechanism

\mathcal{Q}_{t}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}
:
on input
X_{t}
, draw
\hat{Y}\leftarrow A(X_{t},\,\varepsilon_{t}/2,\,\delta_{t})

and return

\mathcal{Q}_{t}(X_{t})\;:=\;\bigl(\hat{Y},\;\;f(X_{t},\hat{Y})+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})\bigr).

Lemma 37

(Properties of the test and scored mechanisms)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{t}^{h}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{t}
be as in
36
. The following statements hold:

(1)

Privacy.

Both
\mathcal{M}_{t}^{h}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{t}
are
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-differentially private.

(2)

Test success probability.

Let
p_{t}:=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}^{h}(X_{t})=+1]
.

(a)

For any
v\geq h
and
\beta_{A}\in(0,1)
such that
\Pr[f(X_{t},\hat{Y})\geq v]\geq 1-\beta_{A}
for
\hat{Y}\leftarrow A(X_{t},\varepsilon_{t}/2,\delta_{t})
,

p_{t}\;\geq\;(1-\beta_{A})\!\left(1-\tfrac{1}{2}\exp\!\left(-\tfrac{\varepsilon_{t}}{2}(v-h)\right)\right).

(b)

If
h\geq\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
, then

p_{t}\;\leq\;\tfrac{1}{2}\exp\!\left(-\tfrac{\varepsilon_{t}}{2}(h-\mathrm{OPT}_{t})\right).

(3)

Score accuracy.

Let
(\hat{Y},v)\sim\mathcal{Q}_{t}(X_{t})
. Then for any
\beta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
,

\Pr\!\left[\bigl|v-f(X_{t},\hat{Y})\bigr|>\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{1}{\beta^{\prime}}\right]\;=\;\beta^{\prime}.

Proof.

Item 1.

Each mechanism composes two operations on
X_{t}
: (i)
A(X_{t},\varepsilon_{t}/2,\delta_{t})
, which is
(\varepsilon_{t}/2,\delta_{t})
-DP; and (ii)
f(X_{t},\hat{Y})+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})
, which for fixed
\hat{Y}
is
\varepsilon_{t}/2
-DP since
X_{t}\mapsto f(X_{t},\hat{Y})
has sensitivity
1
.
By basic composition, both mechanisms are
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP.

Item 2.

Write
V:=f(X_{t},\hat{Y})
and
L\sim\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})
independent of
\hat{Y}
.
The test returns
+1
if and only if
V+L\geq h
.

For part (a): with probability at least
1-\beta_{A}
,
V\geq v\geq h
.
Conditioned on this event, fixing a draw of
V
,

\Pr[V+L\geq h|V]=1-\tfrac{1}{2}\exp({-\varepsilon_{t}(V-h)/2})\geq 1-\tfrac{1}{2}\exp({-\varepsilon_{t}(v-h)/2}).

Taking expectation over
V
, and dropping the conditioning on the event of probability
\geq 1-\beta_{A}
, the stated bound follows.

For part (b): since
f(X_{t},\hat{Y})\leq\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
unconditionally,
V\leq\mathrm{OPT}_{t}\leq h
.
For any draw of
V
,

\Pr[V+L\geq h|V]=\tfrac{1}{2}\exp({-\varepsilon_{t}(h-V)/2})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\exp({-\varepsilon_{t}(h-\mathrm{OPT}_{t})/2}).

Taking expectation over
V
gives the stated bound.

Item 3.

The score is
v=f(X_{t},\hat{Y})+L

where
L\sim\mathrm{Lap}(2/\varepsilon_{t})
is independent of
\hat{Y}
.
The claim follows from the Laplace tail bound:

\Pr[|L|>s]=\exp(-\varepsilon_{t}s/2)
for all
s\geq 0
.
∎

7.4.
Privacy

Lemma 38
.

If
\beta<1/e
,
\varepsilon<1
,
C_{\varepsilon}\leq C_{1}/(C_{1}+C_{H}+3)
, and
C_{\delta}\geq 1+3e^{3}/C_{\beta}
, then
Algorithm

3
satisfies
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy.
When
\delta=0
, the algorithm is
\varepsilon
-DP.

Proof.

The observable output is determined by:
(a) the initialization call to
A
at time-step
t=1
;
(b) the GTM transcript within each instance;
(c) the
ThresholdSelect
output at each checkpoint; and
(d) continuation outputs
Y_{t}=Y_{\mathrm{cur}}
,
which are post-processing of (a) and (c).
By basic composition, the total privacy cost is
the sum of the costs of (a)–(c).

Privacy loss per GTM Instance.

By
35
, the GTM transcript for instance
\ell
is
(1+C_{H}/C_{1})\varepsilon(\ell)
-DP with
\delta=0
, since purification is handled internally.
Substituting
\varepsilon(\ell)=C_{\varepsilon}\omega_{\ell}\varepsilon
, we get that each invocation is pure DP with privacy parameter

(19)

(1+C_{H}/C_{1})\,C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon.

Privacy loss per
ThresholdSelect
instance.

At checkpoint
\ell
, for
t=t(\ell)
,
ThresholdSelect
(
Algorithm

2
) draws from
\mathcal{Q}_{t}
, which is
(\varepsilon_{t},\delta_{t})
-DP by
37
.
By
34
(1) with
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{t}
,
\varepsilon_{0}=\min(\varepsilon_{t},1)
,
\delta_{1}=\delta_{t}
, and coin-flip probability
\xi_{\ell}
, the output is

\bigl(2\varepsilon_{t}+\varepsilon_{0},\;\;3e^{2\varepsilon_{t}+\varepsilon_{0}}\cdot\delta_{t}/\xi_{\ell}\bigr)\text{-DP}.

Since
\varepsilon_{t}\leq 1
, we have
2\varepsilon_{t}+\varepsilon_{0}\leq 3\varepsilon_{t}
and
3e^{2\varepsilon_{t}+\varepsilon_{0}}\leq 3e^{3}
.

For the
\varepsilon
-term, we note the checkpoint occurs at the halting time
t=t(\ell)
, where
\varepsilon_{t(\ell)}=\varepsilon(\ell,t)=\varepsilon(\ell)/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t(\ell)}\beta)))
.
Since
\ln(4/(\omega_{t(\ell)}\beta))\geq\ln(1/\beta)\geq 1
(assuming
\beta\leq 1/e
), we get that the
\varepsilon
-term is at most

(20)

\frac{3\varepsilon(\ell)}{C_{1}}\;=\;\frac{3\,C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon}{C_{1}}.

For the
\delta
-term, we have that by definition
\delta_{t}\leq\bar{p}\,\omega_{\ell}^{2}\,\beta\,\delta/C_{\delta}
, and
\xi_{\ell}=\bar{p}\,C_{\beta}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\beta
. It follows that the
\delta
-term is at most

(21)

3e^{3}\cdot\frac{\delta_{t}}{\xi_{\ell}}\;=\;3e^{3}\cdot\frac{\bar{p}\,\omega_{\ell}^{2}\,\beta\,\delta/C_{\delta}}{\bar{p}\,C_{\beta}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\beta}\;=\;\frac{3e^{3}}{C_{\beta}\,C_{\delta}}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\delta.

Privacy loss for first checkpoint.

The call
A(X_{1},\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{1})

costs
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{1})
, where
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon(1,1)
is at most

(22)

C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{1}\,\varepsilon/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{1}\beta)))\leq C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{1}\,\varepsilon/C_{1}.

Similarly
\delta_{1}=\delta(1,1)
which is at most

(23)

\bar{p}\,\omega_{1}^{2}\,\beta\,\delta/C_{\delta}\leq\omega_{1}\,\delta/C_{\delta},

using
\bar{p}\,\omega_{1}\,\beta\leq 1
. If
A
is pure DP and
\delta
has been set equal to
0
, then this equals
0
.

Total multiplicative privacy loss parameter.

Summing (
19
), (
20
) over
\ell\geq 2
,
and adding (
22
), the net multiplicative privacy loss parameter is at most

\displaystyle\frac{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{1}\,\varepsilon}{C_{1}}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon\!\left(1+\frac{C_{H}}{C_{1}}+\frac{3}{C_{1}}\right)\;\leq\;C_{\varepsilon}\,\varepsilon\!\left(1+\frac{C_{H}}{C_{1}}+\frac{3}{C_{1}}\right).

This is at most
\varepsilon
when
C_{\varepsilon}\leq C_{1}/(C_{1}+C_{H}+3)
.

Total additive privacy loss parameter.

GTM instances are pure DP, so by summing (
21
) over
\ell\geq 2

and adding (
23
), we have that the additive privacy loss parameter is at most

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{1}\,\delta}{C_{\delta}}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\frac{3e^{3}}{C_{\beta}\,C_{\delta}}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\delta\;\leq\;\frac{\delta}{C_{\delta}}\!\left(1+\frac{3e^{3}}{C_{\beta}}\right).

This is at most
\delta
when
C_{\delta}\geq 1+3e^{3}/C_{\beta}
, and this is true even when
\delta=0
, as mentioned above.
∎

7.5.
Accuracy

We establish the accuracy of
Algorithm

3
.
The following lemma translates the outcome of the GTM at each step into bounds on
\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
relative to the threshold
h
.

Lemma 39

(Threshold test accuracy)
.

Let
p^{*}\in(0,1/2]

and let
\bar{p}:=p^{*}/(\gamma\Lambda)

as in
35
.
With probability at least
1-\beta_{\ell}
over the randomness
of the
\ell
-th invocation of
Algorithm

1
:

(1)

If
a_{t}=-1
(continuation),
then
for any
v
satisfying

\Pr[f(X_{t},\hat{Y})\geq v]\geq 1-\beta_{A}
for some
\beta_{A}\leq 1-2p^{*}
,

v\;<\;h+\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}.

(2)

If
a_{t}=+1
(halting),
then

\mathrm{OPT}_{t}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\;>\;h.

Proof.

By
35
, with
\beta_{\textsc{GTM}}=\beta_{\ell}
,
with probability at least
1-\beta_{\ell}

the halting and continuation guarantees hold simultaneously
for all
t
.
We condition on this event.
By the definition of
\delta(i,t)
in
Algorithm

3
,

\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}\leq\bar{p}/2
.

Item 1.

If
a_{t}=-1
, then by
35
(2a)
and
\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}\leq\bar{p}/2
,
we have
p_{t}<p^{*}+\bar{p}/2
.
On the other hand, if
v\geq h
,

37
(2a) gives

p_{t}\;\geq\;(1-\beta_{A})\!\left(1-\tfrac{1}{2}\exp\!\left(-\tfrac{\varepsilon_{t}}{2}(v-h)\right)\right).

For both bounds to hold simultaneously,
since
(1-\beta_{A})\geq 2p^{*}
and
1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-x}
is increasing in
x
,
setting
x^{\prime}:=v-h
it must be the case that

\displaystyle 2p^{*}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\varepsilon_{t}x^{\prime}/2}\right)

\displaystyle<p^{*}+\bar{p}/2

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow 1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\varepsilon_{t}x^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\bar{p}}{4p^{*}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow e^{-\varepsilon_{t}x^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle>1-\frac{\bar{p}}{2p^{*}}\quad\geq\bar{p},

where in the above we use that
\bar{p}\leq p^{*}\leq 1/2
. Taking logarithms,
x^{\prime}<(2/\varepsilon_{t})\ln(1/\bar{p})
.
If
v<h
, the bound holds trivially.

Item 2.

By the contrapositive of
35
(2b):
if
a_{t}=+1
, then
p_{t}>\bar{p}-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}\geq\bar{p}/2
.
If
h<\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
, the conclusion holds trivially since
(2/\varepsilon_{t})\ln(1/\bar{p})>0
. If
h\geq\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
, then by
37
(2b),

p_{t}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\exp(-\tfrac{\varepsilon_{t}}{2}(h-\mathrm{OPT}_{t}))
.
Combining with
p_{t}>\bar{p}/2

and taking logarithms:

h-\mathrm{OPT}_{t}\;<\;\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}.\qed

Lemma 40

(Checkpoint count)
.

Under the hypotheses of
39
,
with probability at least
1-\sum_{\ell}\beta_{\ell}\geq 1-\beta

(by a union bound over the accuracy guarantees
of
35
across all instances),
the number of checkpoints
\ell(t)
declared by time
t
satisfies

\ell(t)\;=\;O\!\left(\frac{1}{\ln(1+\kappa)}\left(\ln\frac{t}{\varepsilon}+\ln\ln\frac{1}{\beta}+\ln\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\right)\right).

Proof.

Suppose instance
\ell
halts at step
t(\ell)
. By
39
Item 2,

h_{\ell}\;<\;\mathrm{OPT}_{t(\ell)}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t(\ell)}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}.

By bounded sensitivity,
\mathrm{OPT}_{t(\ell)}\leq t(\ell)
. Substituting
\varepsilon_{t(\ell)}=C_{\varepsilon}\omega_{\ell}\varepsilon/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t(\ell)}\beta)))
,

\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t(\ell)}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\;=\;\frac{2C_{1}}{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon}\cdot\ln\frac{4}{\omega_{t(\ell)}\beta}\cdot\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}.

We can write
\omega_{\ell}=C_{\omega}/(\ell\ln^{2}(\ell+1))
,

\ell\ln^{2}(\ell+1)\leq\ell^{3}
for
\ell\geq 1
,
and
t(\ell)\leq t
. It follows that

(1+\kappa)^{\ell}\;=\;h_{\ell}\;<\;t+C_{3}\cdot\frac{\ell^{3}\ln(t/\beta)}{\varepsilon}\cdot\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}

for an absolute constant
C_{3}>0
.
Taking logarithms:

\ell\ln(1+\kappa)\;<\;\ln\!\left(t+C_{3}\cdot\frac{\ell^{3}\ln(t/\beta)}{\varepsilon}\cdot\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\right).

Since
3\ln\ell
is eventually dominated by the linear growth of
\ell\ln(1+\kappa)
,

\ell\;=\;O\!\left(\frac{1}{\ln(1+\kappa)}\left(\ln\frac{t}{\varepsilon}+\ln\ln\frac{1}{\beta}+\ln\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\right)\right).\qed

Lemma 41

(Checkpoint quality)
.

Under the hypotheses of
39
, suppose instance
\ell
halts at step
t(\ell)
and
ThresholdSelect
(
Algorithm

2
) is run with the scored mechanism
\mathcal{Q}_{t(\ell)}
(
36
), stopping probability parameter
\xi_{\ell}
, and threshold
h_{\ell}
.
Then with probability at least
1-3\omega_{\ell}\beta
,

f(X_{t(\ell)},\,Y_{\mathrm{cur}})\;\geq\;h_{\ell}-\frac{4C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t(\ell)}\beta))}{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell}\beta}-\frac{2C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t(\ell)}\beta))}{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon}\ln\frac{4}{\bar{p}}.

Proof.

Write
t=t(\ell)
throughout.
We show that three events each hold
with probability at least
1-\omega_{\ell}\beta
.

Bound on
K

Since instance
\ell
halted, by
35
(2b) and
\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}\leq\bar{p}/2
, the success probability of each draw satisfies
p_{t}>\bar{p}-\delta_{t}/\varepsilon_{t}\geq\bar{p}/2
.
By
34
(3), the number of draws
K
is stochastically dominated by
\mathrm{Geo}((\bar{p}/2)(1-\xi_{\ell})+\xi_{\ell})
. Since
(\bar{p}/2)(1-\xi_{\ell})+\xi_{\ell}\geq\bar{p}/2
, it follows that it is also stochastically dominated by
\mathrm{Geo}(\bar{p}/2)
.
Setting
k_{\max}=\lceil(2/\bar{p})\ln(1/(\omega_{\ell}\beta))\rceil
:

\Pr[K>k_{\max}]\;\leq\;(1-\bar{p}/2)^{k_{\max}}\;\leq\;e^{-(\bar{p}/2)k_{\max}}\;\leq\;\omega_{\ell}\beta.

Success of
ThresholdSelect
.

By
34
(2)
with coin-flip probability
\xi_{\ell}=\bar{p}\,C_{\beta}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\beta
,
using
1/p_{t}\leq 2/\bar{p}
,
(1-p_{t})\leq 1
,
and
(1+\varepsilon_{0}/2)\leq 3/2
:

\Pr[\text{output}=-1]\;\leq\;\frac{3\xi_{\ell}}{\bar{p}}\;=\;3C_{\beta}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\beta\;\leq\;\omega_{\ell}\beta,

where the last inequality holds for
C_{\beta}\leq 1/3
.
When
ThresholdSelect
succeeds,
it returns
(\hat{Y},v)
with
v\geq h_{\ell}
.

Uniform bound on Laplace noise terms.

By
37
(3) applied to each of the first

k_{\max}
draws with
\beta^{\prime}=\omega_{\ell}\beta/k_{\max}
,
a union bound gives:

\Pr\!\left[\exists\,j\in[k_{\max}]:|v_{j}-f(X_{t},\hat{Y}_{j})|>\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{k_{\max}}{\omega_{\ell}\beta}\right]\;\leq\;\omega_{\ell}\beta.

Combining.

By a union bound, all three events hold simultaneously
with probability at least
1-3\omega_{\ell}\beta
.
On this event:

K\leq k_{\max}
(Step 1),
so the returned draw has index
j\leq k_{\max}
;
its score satisfies
v\geq h_{\ell}
(Step 2);
and its Laplace noise satisfies

|v-f(X_{t},\hat{Y})|\leq(2/\varepsilon_{t})\ln(k_{\max}/(\omega_{\ell}\beta))
(Step 3).
Therefore:

f(X_{t},Y_{\mathrm{cur}})\;\geq\;v-|v-f(X_{t},\hat{Y})|\;\geq\;h_{\ell}-\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{k_{\max}}{\omega_{\ell}\beta}.

We simplify the additive loss.
Since
k_{\max}\leq 1+(2/\bar{p})\ln(1/(\omega_{\ell}\beta))\leq(4/\bar{p})\ln(1/(\omega_{\ell}\beta))

for
\omega_{\ell}\beta\leq 1/e
:

\ln\frac{k_{\max}}{\omega_{\ell}\beta}\;\leq\;\ln\frac{4}{\bar{p}}+\ln\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell}\beta}+\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell}\beta}\;\leq\;2\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell}\beta}+\ln\frac{4}{\bar{p}}.

Substituting
\varepsilon_{t}=C_{\varepsilon}\omega_{\ell}\varepsilon/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta)))
:

f(X_{t},Y_{\mathrm{cur}})\;\geq\;h_{\ell}-\frac{4C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta))}{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell}\beta}-\frac{2C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta))}{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell}\,\varepsilon}\ln\frac{4}{\bar{p}}.\qed

Theorem 42

(Batch-to-Continual-Observation reduction)
.

Let
f
be a bounded-sensitivity, data-monotone maximization problem,
and let
A
be a batch
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP algorithm.
Fix geometric spacing
\kappa>0
, gap parameter
\gamma\in(1,2]
,
threshold
p^{*}\in(0,1/2]
, failure probability
\beta\in(0,1/e)
, and
\varepsilon<1
.
Run
Algorithm

3
.
For each time
t
, define

V_{A}(X_{t})\;:=\;\sup\!\left\{v:\Pr\!\left[f(X_{t},\,A(X_{t},\,\varepsilon_{t}/2,\,\delta_{t}))\geq v\right]\geq 2p^{*}\right\},

where
\varepsilon_{t}
and
\delta_{t}
are the privacy parameters in effect at time
t
.
Then:

(1)

Privacy.

The output sequence
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP.
When
\delta=0
, the output is
\varepsilon
-DP.

(2)

Accuracy.

With probability at least
1-O(\beta)
,
simultaneously for all
t\geq 1
:

f(X_{t},Y_{t})\;\geq\;\frac{V_{A}(X_{t})}{1+\kappa}-O\!\left(\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\beta}+\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\right)\right),

where
\bar{p}=p^{*}/(\gamma\Lambda)

and
\ell(t)=O((\ln(t/\varepsilon)+\ln\ln(1/\beta)+\ln\ln(1/\bar{p}))/\ln(1+\kappa))
.

(3)

Sample complexity.

At each step
t
, the GTM makes

O(\ln(t/\beta)/(p^{*}(\gamma-1)^{2}))
calls to
A
.
At each checkpoint
\ell
,
ThresholdSelect
makes
at most
O(\ln(1/(\omega_{\ell}\beta))/\bar{p})
calls
with probability at least
1-\omega_{\ell}\beta
.

Proof.

Item 1 is
38
.

Probability accounting (Item 2).

We condition on the intersection of the following events:

•

The accuracy guarantee of
35

holds for every instance.
By a union bound: probability

\geq 1-\sum_{i}\beta_{i}\geq 1-C_{\beta}\,\beta
.

•

The checkpoint quality guarantee of
41

holds at every checkpoint.
By a union bound: probability

\geq 1-3\sum_{i}\omega_{i}\,\beta\geq 1-3\beta
.

Total failure
\leq(C_{\beta}+3)\beta=O(\beta)
.

Between-checkpoint argument.

Fix
t\geq 2
.
Let
\ell(t)
denote the index of the most recent checkpoint at or before time
t
,
with
\ell(t)=1
if no checkpoint has been declared
(corresponding to the initialization at
t=1
).
The currently active GTM instance is
\ell(t)+1
.

Case
\ell(t)=1
(no checkpoint declared).

Instance
2
has not halted at step
t
.
By
39
Item 1
with
v=V_{A}(X_{t})
and threshold
h_{2}=(1+\kappa)^{2}
:

V_{A}(X_{t})\;<\;(1+\kappa)^{2}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}.

Since
f(X_{t},Y_{t})\geq 0
by non-negativity:

f(X_{t},Y_{t})\;\geq\;\frac{V_{A}(X_{t})}{1+\kappa}-(1+\kappa)-\frac{2}{(1+\kappa)\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}.

Substituting
\varepsilon_{t}=C_{\varepsilon}\omega_{2}\varepsilon/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta)))
,
the additive loss is

O(\ln(t/\beta)\ln(1/\bar{p})/(\omega_{2}\varepsilon)+1)
,
which is dominated by the general bound.

Case
\ell(t)\geq 2
.

Step 1: lower bound on
h_{\ell(t)}
.

Instance
\ell(t)+1
has not halted at step
t
.
By
39
Item 1
with
v=V_{A}(X_{t})

and threshold
h_{\ell(t)+1}=(1+\kappa)h_{\ell(t)}
:

V_{A}(X_{t})\;<\;(1+\kappa)\,h_{\ell(t)}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}},

so

(24)

h_{\ell(t)}\;>\;\frac{V_{A}(X_{t})}{1+\kappa}-\frac{2}{(1+\kappa)\varepsilon_{t}}\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}.

Step 2: checkpoint quality.

By
41
at checkpoint
\ell(t)
:

f(X_{t(\ell(t))},Y_{\mathrm{cur}})\;\geq\;h_{\ell(t)}-\frac{4C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t(\ell(t))}\beta))}{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\varepsilon}\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\beta}-\frac{2C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t(\ell(t))}\beta))}{C_{\varepsilon}\,\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\varepsilon}\ln\frac{4}{\bar{p}}.

Step 3: monotonicity.

Data monotonicity gives

f(X_{t},Y_{t})\geq f(X_{t(\ell(t))},Y_{\mathrm{cur}})
.
Since
t(\ell(t))\leq t
,

\ln(4/(\omega_{t(\ell(t))}\beta))=O(\ln(t/\beta))
.

Combining.

Substituting (
24
) into Step 2
and applying Step 3.
The continuation correction

(2/((1+\kappa)\varepsilon_{t}))\ln(1/\bar{p})

uses
\varepsilon_{t}=C_{\varepsilon}\omega_{\ell(t)+1}\varepsilon/(C_{1}\ln(4/(\omega_{t}\beta)))
,
giving
O(\ln(t/\beta)\ln(1/\bar{p})/(\omega_{\ell(t)+1}\varepsilon))
,
which is absorbed since
\omega_{\ell(t)+1}=\Theta(\omega_{\ell(t)})
.
The total additive loss is

O\!\left(\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\beta}+\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\right)\right).

Checkpoint count.

By
40
,

\ell(t)=O((\ln(t/\varepsilon)+\ln\ln(1/\beta)+\ln\ln(1/\bar{p}))/\ln(1+\kappa))
.

Sample complexity (Item 3).

The per-step GTM cost is
35
(3):

O(\ln(t/\beta)/(p^{*}(\gamma-1)^{2}))
calls per step.
At each checkpoint, by
41
,

ThresholdSelect
makes at most

k_{\max}=O(\ln(1/(\omega_{\ell}\beta))/\bar{p})
calls
with probability at least
1-\omega_{\ell}\beta
.
∎

Corollary 43

(High-probability batch guarantee)
.

Under the hypotheses of
42
,
suppose additionally that
A
satisfies

f(X,A(X))\geq\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E_{A}

with probability at least
1-\beta_{A}
for some
\beta_{A}<1
.
Set
p^{*}=(1-\beta_{A})/2
.
Then with probability at least
1-O(\beta)
,
simultaneously for all
t\geq 1
:

f(X_{t},Y_{t})\;\geq\;\frac{\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-E_{A}(t)}{1+\kappa}-O\!\left(\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\beta}+\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\right)\right),

where
E_{A}(t)
is evaluated at

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{batch}}=\Theta(\omega_{\ell(t)}\varepsilon/\ln(t/\beta))

and
\delta_{\mathrm{batch}}=\Theta(\omega_{\ell(t)}^{2}\beta\delta)
, and
\bar{p}=(1-\beta_{A})/(2\gamma\Lambda)
.

Proof.

Since
\Pr[f(X_{t},A(X_{t}))\geq\alpha\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-E_{A}(t)]\geq 1-\beta_{A}=2p^{*}
,
we have
V_{A}(X_{t})\geq\alpha\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-E_{A}(t)
.
The result follows from
42
(2).
∎

Corollary 44

(In-expectation batch guarantee)
.

Under the hypotheses of
42
,
suppose additionally that

\mathbb{E}[f(X,A(X))]\geq\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}(X)-E_{A}
.
Fix a slack
c\in(0,1)

and set
p^{*}=c\alpha/2
.
Then with probability at least
1-O(\beta)
,
simultaneously for all
t\geq 1
:

f(X_{t},Y_{t})\;\geq\;\frac{(1-c)\alpha\cdot\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-E_{A}(t)}{1+\kappa}-O\!\left(\frac{\ln(t/\beta)}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\varepsilon}\cdot\left(\ln\frac{1}{\omega_{\ell(t)}\,\beta}+\ln\frac{1}{\bar{p}}\right)\right),

where
\bar{p}=c\alpha/(2\gamma\Lambda)

and
E_{A}(t)
is evaluated at

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{batch}}=\Theta(\omega_{\ell(t)}\varepsilon/\ln(t/\beta))

and
\delta_{\mathrm{batch}}=\Theta(\omega_{\ell(t)}^{2}\beta\delta)
.

Proof.

By
33
with slack
c
,

\Pr[f(X_{t},A(X_{t}))\geq(1-c)\alpha\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-E_{A}(t)]\geq c\alpha=2p^{*}
.
Thus
V_{A}(X_{t})\geq(1-c)\alpha\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-E_{A}(t)
.
The result follows from
42
(2).
∎

8.
Applications

Our batch-to-continual-observation reduction (
42
) has many applications. In this section, we cover some of them in detail.

8.1.
Submodular maximization

Table 3.

42
to batch DP submodular maximization algorithms.
\mathrm{OPT}
: batch optimum;
\mathrm{OPT}_{t}
: CO optimum at time
t
;

: cardinality or matroid rank constraint;
\kappa,\eta>0
: user-defined parameters;
\beta
: failure probability. Monotone/non-monotone refers to argument-monotonicity; our framework additionally requires data-monotonicity. For in-expectation batch guarantees,
44
is applied with small constant
c>0
.

(1{-}\tfrac{1}{e}{-}\eta)\mathrm{OPT}-O\bigl(\tfrac{k\ln m/\beta}{\varepsilon}\bigr)

\tfrac{1-1/e-\eta}{1+\kappa}\,\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-\tilde{O}\bigl(\tfrac{k}{\kappa\varepsilon}\ln^{3}\tfrac{t}{\beta}\ln\tfrac{m}{\beta}\bigr)

Decomp. monotone
\varepsilon
-DP,
k
-Matroid,
\eta{\in}(0,1)

(1{-}\tfrac{1}{e}{-}\eta)\mathrm{OPT}-O\bigl(\tfrac{k\ln m/\eta\beta}{\varepsilon}\bigr)

\tfrac{1-1/e-\eta}{1+\kappa}\,\mathrm{OPT}_{t}-\tilde{O}\bigl(\tfrac{k}{\kappa\varepsilon}\ln^{3}\tfrac{t}{\beta}\ln\tfrac{m}{\eta\beta}\bigr)

For a natural number
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\mathcal{U}=\{1,2,\cdots,m\}
be the universe. A set function
f:2^{\mathcal{U}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is
submodular
if

\forall S\subseteq T\subseteq\mathcal{U}\quad\text{and}\quad v\in\mathcal{U}\backslash T,\quad f(S\cup\{v\})-f(S)\geq f(T\cup\{v\})-f(T).

In the problem of
submodular maximization
, we are given query access to a submodular set function
f:2^{\mathcal{U}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. The goal is to find a subset
T\subseteq\mathcal{U}
that maximizes
f(T)
under certain constraints, such as:

•

Cardinality constraints. The goal is to find
T
that maximizes
f(T)
under the constraint that
|T|\leq c
.

•

Matroid constraints. Given a rank-
c
matroid
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{I})
, we want
T
to be an independent set of the matroid (i.e.,
T\in\mathcal{I}
)

•

Knapsack constraints. There is a cost
c_{u}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
associated with each element
u\in\mathcal{U}
, and one is only allowed to pick sets
S
such that
\sum_{u\in S}c_{u}\leq 1
.

•

p
-extendible system constraints. If
\mathcal{I}
is a collection of subsets of
\mathcal{U}
, the pair
(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{I})
is a
p
-extendible system if for all
S\subset T\in\mathcal{I}
, and
u\in\mathcal{U}
such that
S\cup\{u\}\in\mathcal{I}
, there is some set
V\subset T\backslash S
of at most
p
elements such that
(T\backslash V)\cup\{u\}\in\mathcal{I}
.

Submodular maximization is also studied under various restrictions on the objective function itself, such as:

•

Monotone, or argument-monotone. A submodular function
f
is monotone if whenever
S\subseteq T
, then
f(S)\leq f(T)
. Note that this is distinct from the data monotonicity used in this paper to construct our black-box reduction, which is why we call this condition
argument monotonicity
to avoid any ambiguity.

•

Decomposable. Also known as the combinatorial public projects problem, here the function
f
can be written as

\displaystyle f(\cdot)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(\cdot)

for some
f_{i}:\mathcal{U}\to[0,1]
. Note that here since the co-domain is non-negative, decomposability unconditionally implies data-monotonicity.

•

Bounded curvature. For a monotone submodular function
f
, its total curvature
\kappa_{f}
is given by the expression

\displaystyle\kappa_{f}

\displaystyle=1-\min_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\frac{f(\mathcal{U})-f(\mathcal{U}\backslash\{u\})}{f(u)-f(\emptyset)}.

For objective functions with known curvature, we can typically give a better approximation guarantee than in the general case.

Private submodular maximization in the batch setting.

Motivated by the problem of
private feature selection
,
24
were the first to initiate a systematic study of the optimization of submodular functions under the constraint of differential privacy, introducing privacy-preserving algorithms for both monotone and non-monotone objectives, under cardinality, matroid, and
p
-extendible systems constraints. Previously the work
15
had considered a special instance of private submodular maximization under
k
-cardinality constraints for
decomposable
objectives, achieving a
(1-1/e)
-approximation and an additive error which is
O((k/\varepsilon)\log m)
under approximate DP. This approximation factor is known to be optimal in the non-private setting under standard hardness assumptions and
15
showed that a
\Omega(k/\varepsilon)
factor is indeed necessary under the constraints of pure-DP. A similar linear dependence of the additive error on
k
was shown also to hold in the approximate DP setting by
2
. All of these works require a bounded sensitivity of the objective function in terms of the input data set. Formally, the private submodular maximization problem is stated as follows:

Definition 45

(Private submodular maximization)
.

We are given a correspondence
X\to f_{X}(\cdot)
between datasets
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathcal{X}}
and submodular functions
f_{X}
, with the property of
bounded sensitivity
; i.e. there is some public value
\Delta f
such that given the set of admissible inputs
\mathcal{C}\subset 2^{\mathcal{Y}}
, it is the case that

\max_{X\sim X^{\prime},Y\in\mathcal{C}}|f_{X}(Y)-f_{X^{\prime}}(Y)|\leq\Delta f.

The private submodular maximization problem asks for a solution to the submodular maximization problem defined by the objective
f_{X}
for a given data set
X
, and the constraint set
\mathcal{C}
. The mapping from the data set
X
to the output solution
Y^{*}
must be differentially private for the given choice of privacy parameters.

There has since been a long line of work in the DP algorithms literature studying variants of private submodular maximization. We see that since the bounded sensitivity constraint is inherent to this problem, our result
42
is applicable to each work where the data-monotone condition is also fulfilled. In some cases, like non-negative decomposable objectives in both monotone and non-monotone cases, the data-monotone condition is always fulfilled — note that even if a submodular function
f_{X}
is non-monotone, the data monotone condition requires only that for all
X\subset X^{\prime}
,
f_{X}\leq f_{X^{\prime}}
. In the decomposable setting we have
f_{X}=\sum_{x\in X}f_{\{x\}}
, and as long as
f_{\{x\}}\geq 0
, data-monotonicity is immediate. In other cases where we only have
\Delta f
-sensitivity, data monotonicity is an extrinsic condition not necessarily true of all objectives.

To the best of our knowledge, there are no prior works for private submodular maximization in the continual observation setting. With our framework, we are able to apply each work in the batch setting to give the first guarantee in the same setting in the continual observation setting, with the additional potentially extrinsic constraint of the objective being data-monotone.

We construct
Table

3
by applying
42
to each prior work. The batch approximation factor
\alpha
is preserved up to the geometric spacing factor
(1+\kappa)
. For algorithms with in-expectation guarantees,
44
converts the in-expectation guarantee to a high-probability one via
33
, incurring only a
(1-c)
factor in the multiplicative guarantee for an arbitrarily small user-chosen
c>0
, at the cost of an
O(\ln(1/c))
additive term. In the table, we absorb this loss into the existing slack parameter
\eta
or suppress it as a lower-order term.

8.2.
Densest subgraph

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
and a node weight function,
c:V\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
, a subset
S\subseteq V
, let

\rho(S)=\frac{|E(S)|}{\sum_{v\in S}c(v)},

where
E(S)
is the set of edges induced by
S\subseteq V
, i.e., with both end points in
S
. The goal of the densest subgraph problem is to find

S^{*}:=\argmax_{S\subseteq V}\rho(S).

The above definition is the general form of densest subgraph problem (known as
weighted densest subgraph
). The unweighted densest subgraph has all node weights
O(1)
, in which case,
\sum_{v\in S}c(v)=O(|S|)
and the objective becomes

S^{*}:=\argmax_{S\subseteq V}\frac{|E(S)|}{|S|}.

It is easy to see that in both cases, under edge-insertions the objective is monotonically increasing for every candidate solution
S
, and so
42
applies as is. Further, in the weighted setting, the only works considered assume that
c(v)\geq 1
, so we also immediately have that the objective of interest is
1
-sensitive.

For graph problems, differential privacy is usually formulated in terms of node-neighboring and edge-neighboring graphs, which is to say that neighboring input graphs
G=(V,E)
and
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
can vary in the presence or absence of at most
1
node in the node-neighboring setting, and in the presence or absence of
1
edge in the edge-neighboring setting. The latter is a weaker notion of privacy but is in practice much easier to work with — we will consider only this notion of privacy. In the CO setting, we consider two streams to consist of edges of the graphs. Two streams are considered to be neighboring if they differ in one time epoch, where an edge is present or absent.

Prior work in the batch setting.

The work
5
solves the unweighted densest subgraph problem for any given constant
\eta>0
with multiplicative approximation
1/(1+\eta)
and additive error
O(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\log^{4}n)
in the batch setting in an
\varepsilon
-DP manner. This algorithm succeeds with high probability, which they specify to mean with probability
1-1/n^{c}
for any given constant
c
.

The work
6
solves the weighted densest subgraph problem in the approximate-DP setting with no multiplicative loss at all (
\alpha=1
) and additive error
O\left(\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\left(\log\left(\tfrac{n}{\delta}\right)\log\left(n\right)\right)}\right)
. This bound holds with high probability, which tracking the proof of the relevant result can be set to be
1-1/n^{c}
for any constant
c>0
. They assume that
c(v)\geq 1
, a standard assumption from the non-privacy literature.

Implications for CO

In the unweighted setting, we apply
42
Item 2 to the high-probability guarantee of
5
. Since the batch algorithm achieves a
1/(1+\eta)
-approximation, our reduction gives a
1/((1+\eta)(1+\kappa))
-approximation under CO. For any desired constant multiplicative loss, we can set
\eta
and
\kappa
to be small constants. The additive error is

\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon}\log^{2}t\cdot\log^{4}t\right)+\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon}\log^{3}t\right)=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\kappa\varepsilon}\log^{6+a}t\right).

In the above, the
\tilde{O}
notation hides
\log 1/\varepsilon
terms and
a
is an arbitrarily small constant.

In this unweighted setting, the state of the art result is not the one achieved by our framework, but rather by
12
, who give an
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm that achieves a
\frac{1}{1+\kappa}
multiplicative approximation with additive error
O(\tfrac{1}{\kappa^{4}\varepsilon}\log^{5}n)
with high probability for all time-steps. The gap between our
\log^{6+a}t
and their
\log^{5}n
reflects the cost of the black-box approach.

In the weighted setting, to the best of our knowledge, there is no prior work in the CO setting. Here, on applying
42
Item 2 to the batch DP result of
6
(which has
\alpha=1
), we get that there is an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP algorithm that with high probability achieves a
\tfrac{1}{1+\kappa}
-approximation and additive error

\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon\kappa}\log^{2}t\sqrt{\log\tfrac{t}{\delta}\log t}\right)+\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon\kappa}\log^{3}t\right)=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon\kappa}\log^{3}t\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\delta}}\right).

8.3.
Densest
k
-subgraph

We also note the recent work of Khayatian, Vullikanti and Konar
18
who give the first
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-edge DP algorithms for D
k
S.

Given an unweighted undirected graph
G=(V,E)
with
|V|=n
and
|E|=m
, and a target size
k\in[n]
, the Densest-
k
-Subgraph (D
k
S) problem seeks a subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq V
of exactly
k
vertices maximizing the induced edge density
d(\mathcal{S}):=|\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{S}}|/\binom{k}{2}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{S}}=\{(u,v)\in E:u,v\in\mathcal{S}\}
. We write
d_{k}^{*}:=\max_{|\mathcal{S}|=k}d(\mathcal{S})
for the optimum. As in the densest subgraph setting, the induced edge count has sensitivity
1
under edge-DP and satisfies data monotonicity under edge insertions, so
42
applies.

D
k
S is computationally intractable: achieving an
O(n^{1/(\log\log n)^{c}})
-approximation is hard under standard complexity-theoretic assumptions
22
. As a result, no worst-case multiplicative approximation ratio
\alpha
is available, and
43
and
44
do not apply. However,
42
and its data-dependent guarantee via
V_{A}(X_{t})
imply that the performance of the CO construction based on their algorithm ought to inherit a performance similar to the performance of their algorithm executed at time step
t
with privacy parameter
\varepsilon/\ln^{2}(t/\beta)
, and some additional additive polylogarithmic overhead scaled by
1/\kappa
.

As the performance of their algorithm is measured entirely empirically, there is no corresponding theoretical result that we can state. We conclude this section by observing that our reduction applies in this problem setting, that our analysis indicates non-trivial performance guarantees, but leave it as an open question for future work to test whether this actually pans out in practice.

8.4.
Max-Cut

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
and an edge weight function
w:E\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the MAX-CUT problem asks for a subset
S\subseteq V
that maximizes the cut value:

\max_{S\subseteq V}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e=(u,v)\in E\\
u\in S,\,v\in V\backslash S\end{subarray}}}w(e).

It is easy to see that for every candidate solution
S
, the objective is monotonically increasing for insertion-only edge streams. The sensitivity of this problem is
\max_{e\in E}w(e)
, so when a bound on this expression is known the objective function can be rescaled and
42
can be applied.

Chandra et al.
1
gave an
\varepsilon
-differentially private algorithm
A
that
\left(1,\beta,E_{A}\right)
-solves MAX-CUT, where

E_{A}(X_{t},\varepsilon,0,\beta):=O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(n\log n+\log\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)\right)\right).

Note that the algorithm of Chandra et al.
1
is not efficient: the time and space consumption depend on the complexity of finding the exact max-cut on a noisy graph with non-negative edge weights. However, since our framework applies to this problem, our reduction implies that if there exists an efficient algorithm for DP MAX-CUT in the batch setting, then we would immediately have an efficient algorithm in the CO setting as well, with an accuracy guarantee derived from
42
.

9.
Accuracy guarantees in prior work

In this section we recall the formal accuracy guarantees in prior work, and compare them at a technical level with the guarantees we derive for the Generalized Thresholding Mechanism.

9.1.
Prior work

We first give a brief review of the above threshold mechanism in the standard setting, and the techniques of two works most related to our works, i.e.,
21
and
4
.

Review of problem description.
Recall that in
standard private testing
there is a sequence of private real numbered values
(v_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and a threshold
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. A tester processes the stream in an online fashion and outputs
a_{t}=-1
until it receives
v_{t}\geq\tau
; at this point it outputs
a_{t}=+1
and halts. However, the output of the tester should be
\varepsilon
-DP where
v
and
v^{\prime}
are considered neighboring streams if
\forall t,\,|v_{t}-v^{\prime}_{t}|\leq 1
. The
Above Threshold
mechanism
11
solves this problem with the
accuracy guarantee
that with probability
1-\beta
for all
t\geq 1
,
a_{t}=+1\Rightarrow v_{t}\geq\tau-O((1/\varepsilon)(\log(t/\beta)))
, and
a_{t}=-1\Rightarrow v_{t}\leq\tau+O((1/\varepsilon)(\log(t/\beta)))
.

For the ease of the readers, we recall the problem of
generalized private testing
. In the generalized private testing, the values
(v_{t})_{t\geq 1}
are replaced with a sequence of mechanisms, datasets and privacy parameters
\mathcal{M}_{t}:\mathcal{X}\to\{+1,-1\}
,
X_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
, and
\varepsilon_{t}\geq 0
, and the threshold
\tau
is replaced by a target success probability
p^{*}\in(0,1)
. For simplicity, we assume here
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon_{1}
for all
t
. We define
p_{t}=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})=+1]
and
p^{\prime}_{t}=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X^{\prime}_{t})=+1]
, where
X^{\prime}_{t}
is a dataset neighboring
X_{t}
. Two streams of mechanism and dataset pairs
(\mathcal{M}_{t},X_{t})
and
(\mathcal{M}_{t},X^{\prime}_{t})
are considered neighboring if
p_{t}\in(e^{-\varepsilon_{t}}p^{\prime}_{t},e^{\varepsilon_{t}}p^{\prime}_{t})
and
(1-p_{t})\in(e^{-\varepsilon_{t}}(1-p^{\prime}_{t}),e^{\varepsilon_{t}}(1-p^{\prime}_{t})
. We call this the
stability guarantee of
p_{t}
. The analyst should process the stream and stop at the first
(\mathcal{M}_{t},X_{t})
for which
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
. Just as in the standard setting, the goal is to suffer a small loss in accuracy of these threshold tests, which is to say that if the test fails, then
p_{t}
should not be more than
p^{*}
, and if it passes, then
p_{t}
should not be much less than
p^{*}
.

Technical overview of
21
.
Liu and Talwar approached this problem by initially assuming access to an oracle which gives the analyst the exact value of
p_{t}
. Then, they observed that the
log-odds ratio
of the input probability, i.e.
\Phi(p_{t})=\log(p_{t}/(1-p_{t}))
is
2\varepsilon
-Lipschitz. In this simplified setting, they can reduce the generalized private testing problem to the standard setting, and they show essentially that with this reduction, with probability
1-\beta
for
t\geq 1
, if
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}
then
a_{t}=-1
, where
\bar{p}_{t}=(\beta^{2}/t)^{12\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}p^{*}
; for
p_{t}\in(\bar{p}_{t},p^{*})
, there is no guarantee. We see that the logarithmic additive error in accuracy in the standard setting gets amplified to a multiplicative polynomial gap between
p^{*}
and
\bar{p}
in the generalized setting.

However, in generalized private testing, the analyst does not have access to
p_{t}
. To circumvent this issue, the generalized Above Threshold mechanism of
21
draws
N_{t}
many samples from
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
, and use a coupling argument to show that the estimates
\widehat{p}_{t}
of
p_{t}
fulfill a weak Lipschitz property. As discussed in the introduction, the downsides of this approach are that (a) we can only guarantee approximate DP outputs while requiring the inputs to be pure DP, (b) the exponent of
\beta^{2}/T
is
12
and (c) the sample complexity has a large
(T/\beta)^{12\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon}
factor.

Technical overview of
4

Cohen, Lyu, Nelson, Sarlós and Stemmer revisited this problem, and gave a unified framework for generalized private selection and generalized private testing. Their Test procedure for generalized private testing draws a value
p^{\dagger}
, with cumulative density function,
\Pr[p^{\dagger}\leq x]\leq x^{\theta_{C}}
for all
x\in[0,1]
and some fixed
{\theta_{C}}\geq 0
chosen by the analyst. Then, for every mechanism and dataset
\mathcal{M}_{t}
and
X_{t}
, they draw a value
b_{t}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p^{\dagger})
. If
b_{t}=-1
, then they output
-1
without evaluating
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
. If
b_{1}=+1
, then they output whatever they get from the evaluation of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
. They showed that, if for all
t\geq 1
,
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{t})
-DP, then their mechanism achieves
((2+{\theta_{C}})\varepsilon_{1},\sum_{i}\delta_{i})
-DP. In particular, for a target output probability of
\varepsilon>2\varepsilon_{1}
, this implies that picking
{\theta_{C}}=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1}-2
suffices for
(\varepsilon,\sum_{t}\delta_{t})
-DP.

4
introduce two new key technical insights which make this possible: (1) a coupling argument along the lines of the sparse vector technique that only requires an injective mapping, not a bijective one, and (2) the observation that one can lean on the inherent privacy guarantee of each mechanism evaluation to avoid adding more noise for each test.

The significance of the first insight is that a one-sided perturbation that only systematically reduces the probability of a test passing suffices for proving the uniform privacy loss bound; this means that less noise is needed for achieving privacy, and this improves the accuracy of tests. Along with the second insight, this implies much less additional is being added to achieve a similar privacy loss guarantee, compared to
21
. At a high level, the
{\theta_{C}}\varepsilon_{1}
term in the privacy loss is incurred by the coupling argument, and the
2\varepsilon_{1}
term is incurred by the last mechanism evaluation that leads to a halt.

4
do not give accuracy guarantee along the line of
21
. In this section, we analyse the accuracy of their mechanism along the lines of
21
.
We also derive a lower bound demonstrating that with this algorithm, one cannot achieve a rejection threshold greater than
p^{*}\beta/T
.
The highlight is that the coefficient of
\varepsilon_{1}/\varepsilon
is significantly better, but an additional
\beta/T
factor is incurred in the rejection threshold.

9.2.
The accuracy guarantee of
21

We recall the algorithm and formal guarantee for the reader’s reference. We start with main steps in the ExtendedAboveThreshold mechanism presented in
20
(the arxiv version of
21
). The mechanism gets target threshold
p^{*}\in(0,1)
, a failure probability
\beta\in(0,1)
, a privacy parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, an auxiliary parameter
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
, a stream length
T>1
, and input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{1}>0
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
denote the output privacy parameter (
\varepsilon_{3}
in the notation of
21
).
The ExtendedAboveThreshold algorithm (
(
21
, Algorithm 4)
) proceeds as follows.

(1)

At initialization, it draws shared noise
\nu\sim\mathrm{Lap}(4(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{0})/\varepsilon)
.

(2)

Let
\sigma_{\mathrm{LT}}:=12(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{0})/\varepsilon
for the exponent that governs the accuracy–privacy tradeoff. Define

(25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}C&:=2(e^{\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon_{1}}+1+e^{\varepsilon_{0}/2})<21,\qquad\Delta:=\frac{C\ln(8T/\delta)}{\varepsilon_{0}(e^{\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon_{1}}-1)}=O\!\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}\ln\frac{T}{\delta}\right),\\
N&:=\frac{e^{\varepsilon_{0}}\,\Delta}{\min\{p^{*},1-p^{*}\}}\cdot\left(\frac{T+1}{\beta}\right)^{\!\sigma_{\mathrm{LT}}},\qquad\Phi_{\Delta}(x):=\frac{Nx+\Delta}{N(1-x)+\Delta}.\end{split}

(3)

At each step
t=1,\ldots,T
,

(a)

draws
N
i.i.d. evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
;

(b)

computes the empirical success probability
\widehat{p}_{t}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}\{y_{t,j}=+1\}
;

(c)

draws per-step noise
\xi_{t}\sim\mathrm{Lap}(8(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{0})/\varepsilon)

(d)

if

e^{\xi_{t}}\cdot\Phi_{\Delta}(\widehat{p}_{t})\;\geq\;e^{\nu}\cdot\Phi_{\Delta}(p^{*}).

then output
a_{t}=+1
and halt.
Otherwise output
a_{t}=-1
and continues.

We now restate their result in our notation:

Theorem 46

(Theorem 4.4 in
21
restated in our notation)
.

Fix a target threshold
p^{*}\in(0,1)
, a failure probability
\beta\in(0,1)
, a privacy parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, an auxiliary parameter
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
, a stream length
T>1
, and input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{1}>0
. Let
\varepsilon>0
denote the output privacy parameter (
\varepsilon_{3}
in the notation of
21
). Let
\sigma_{LT},C,\Delta,N
, and
\Phi_{\Delta}(x)
be as defined in
eq.

25
.

Suppose that each
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP. Then the following hold:

(1)

Privacy.

The mechanism is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP.

(2)

Not stopping too early.
Conditional on the algorithm halting at step
R
, with probability at least
1-\beta-\delta/2
:

p_{R}\;\geq\;\frac{1}{2}\,e^{-\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{\beta}{R+1}\right)^{\!\sigma_{\mathrm{LT}}}p^{*}.

(3)

Not stopping too late.
Conditional on the algorithm halting at step
R
, with probability at least
1-\beta-\delta/2
: no earlier step
i<R
satisfies

\Phi_{\Delta}(p_{i})\;\geq\;e^{\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{R+1}{\beta}\right)^{\!\sigma_{\mathrm{LT}}}\Phi_{\Delta}(p^{*}).

Further, (
(
21
, Theorem 4.2(c), “moreover”)
), with probability
1-\beta-\delta/2
,
p_{R}
is bounded from below:

p_{R}\;\geq\;1-\left(1+\frac{e^{\varepsilon_{0}}}{2(1-p^{*})}\left(\frac{R+1}{\beta}\right)^{\!\sigma_{\mathrm{LT}}}\right)^{\!-1}.

(4)

Halting.
If at some step
t
(conditional on not having halted earlier) the success probability satisfies

\Phi_{\Delta}(p_{t})\;\geq\;e^{\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)^{\!\sigma_{\mathrm{LT}}}\Phi_{\Delta}(p^{*}),

then the algorithm halts at step
t
with probability at least
1-\beta-\delta/(4T)
.

Typographical note:
The statement of
(
21
, Theorem 4.2(d))
writes
\Pr[a_{t}=+1|\ldots]\leq\beta+\delta/(4T)
, but the proof establishes the complementary bound
\Pr[a_{t}\neq+1|\ldots]\leq\beta+\delta/(4T)
, as stated above.

(5)

Sample complexity.

The number of evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at each step is

N\;=\;O\!\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\,\min\{p^{*},1-p^{*}\}}\cdot\left(\frac{T}{\beta}\right)^{\!\sigma_{\mathrm{LT}}}\cdot\ln\frac{T}{\delta}\right).

Note that the halting guarantee is
not
a guarantee that the algorithm halts whenever
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
; the condition requires
p_{t}
to be substantially above
p^{*}
. For large
N
,
\Phi_{\Delta}(x)\to x/(1-x)
, so the condition is approximately

\frac{p_{t}}{1-p_{t}}\;\geq\;e^{\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)^{\!\sigma_{\mathrm{LT}}}\cdot\frac{p^{*}}{1-p^{*}}.

9.3.
Accuracy guarantee for the Test procedure of Cohen et al.
4

Input:

Stream of
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP mechanisms
(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
with
p_{t}:=\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})=+1]
; target
p^{*}\in(0,1)
; failure probability
\beta\in(0,1)
; parameter
{\theta_{C}}=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1}-2>0
.

Draw
p^{\dagger}
from
[0,1]
with
\Pr[p^{\dagger}\leq x]=x^{\theta_{C}}
;

Function

\textsc{Test}(\mathcal{M}_{t})
:

Draw
r\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p^{\dagger})
;

if

r=0

then

return

-1
;

return

\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
;

for

t\geq 1

do

N_{t}\leftarrow\lceil\ln(2/\beta)/((\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}\,p^{*})\rceil
;

\mathit{halted}\leftarrow\mathit{false}
;

for

j=1,\ldots,N_{t}

do

if

\textsc{Test}(\mathcal{M}_{t})=+1

then

Output

a_{t}=+1
;

\mathit{halted}\leftarrow\mathit{true}
;

break
;

end if

end for

if

\mathit{halted}

then

halt
;

else

output

a_{t}=-1
and continue to step
t+1
;

end for

Algorithm 4

Generalized Private Testing via
4

4
introduce an elegant and lightweight procedure for achieving an SVT-style privacy loss for generalized private testing. They are able to apply this framework to improve the standard SVT, as well as error bounds for the private multiplicative weight update method
16
and bounds for adaptive data analysis
7
. They introduce the Test subroutine in
Algorithm

4
, and show the following result:

Theorem 47
.

(
4
, Theorem 2)
.
Let there be a stream of
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{t})
-DP mechanisms
(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
, and parameter
{\theta_{C}}=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1}-2>0
.
The
Test
procedure of
(
4
, Algorithm 1)
(reproduced here as the
Test
procedure in
Algorithm

4
) takes as input the given stream and halts when it has output
+1

c\geq 1
times . The collection of outputs satisfies
((2c+{\theta_{C}})\varepsilon_{1},\sum_{i=1}^{T}\delta_{t})
-DP for all
\delta\in(0,2^{-\sqrt{c}})
.

This theorem provides an adaptive privacy guarantee: it applies to any adaptive sequence of calls to
Test
, where the choice of mechanism at each call may depend on prior outputs, as long as the sequence terminates after receiving
c
positive responses.

In their work, Cohen et al.
4
do not give an accuracy guarantee as introduced by
21
for the generalized private testing problem. We derive an accuracy guarantee for their Test procedure along the lines of the notion of accuracy introduced by
21
.

At initialization, the mechanism draws a shared pass probability
p^{\dagger}
from
[0,1]
with
\Pr[p^{\dagger}\leq x]=x^{\theta_{C}}
for all
x\in[0,1]
. At each step
t
, the mechanism makes up to
N_{t}
independent calls to
\textsc{Test}(\mathcal{M}_{t})
, where each call draws
r\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p^{\dagger})
and returns
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
if
r=1
and
-1
if
r=0
. The mechanism halts at step
t
if any call returns
+1
, and continues to the next step if all
N_{t}
calls return
-1
.

We see that with probability
p^{\dagger}
, the
Test
procedure does not evaluate the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at all. To avoid the possibility of a missed halt, we test every mechanism some
N_{t}
-many times to ensure that if
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
, then we would encounter a
+1
with probability
1-\beta
. We formalize this guarantee in
Algorithm

4
and the following result.

Proposition 48

(Accuracy of the mechanism of
4
for generalized private testing)
.

Fix a target threshold
p^{*}\in(0,1)
, a failure probability
\beta\in(0,1)
, an input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, and a private dataset
X
. Suppose
\varepsilon>2\varepsilon_{1}
, and define
{\theta_{C}}:=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1}-2>0
. When
Algorithm

4
is run on an input sequence of mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X)
, the following properties hold.

(1)

Privacy.

The mechanism is
\varepsilon
-differentially private.

(2)

Accuracy.

With probability at least
1-2\beta
, simultaneously for all
t\geq 1
:

(a)

Halting:
If
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
, then
a_{t}=+1
.

(b)

Continuation:
If
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}
, then
a_{t}=-1
, where

\bar{p}_{t}\;=\;\frac{p^{*}\beta_{t}}{2\ln(2/\beta)}\left(\frac{\beta}{2}-\beta\right)^{1/\theta_{C}}.

(3)

Sample complexity.

The number of evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at each step is at most
N_{t}=O\!\left(\frac{\ln(2/\beta)}{p^{*}(\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}}\right)
.

Proof.

Each call to
\textsc{Test}(\mathcal{M}_{t})
returns
+1
with probability
p^{\dagger}\cdot p_{t}
, where
p^{\dagger}\in[0,1]
with
\Pr[p^{\dagger}\leq x]=x^{\theta_{C}}
: the Bernoulli trial passes with probability
p^{\dagger}
, and conditioned on passing,
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X_{t})
returns
+1
with probability
p_{t}
. At step
t
, the mechanism makes calls sequentially and halts globally on the first
+1
response, or outputs
a_{t}=-1
after
N_{t}
consecutive
-1
responses. Since the calls are independent, the probability of observing at least one
+1
among
N_{t}
calls is
1-(1-p^{\dagger}\,p_{t})^{N_{t}}
, regardless of whether we stop at the first
+1
or run all
N_{t}
calls.

Privacy.

Algorithm

4
produces an adaptive sequence of
Test
calls (the choice of
\mathcal{M}_{t}
at each step may depend on prior outputs
a_{1},\ldots,a_{t-1}
) that terminates after at most
c=1
positive response. Therefore, by
47
, the full transcript is
\varepsilon
-DP for
\varepsilon=(2+{\theta_{C}})\varepsilon_{1}
.

Noise control.
Define the event
\mathcal{G}:=\{p^{\dagger}\in((\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}},(1-\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}})\}
. Since
\Pr[p^{\dagger}\leq x]=x^{\theta_{C}}
, we have
\Pr[\mathcal{G}^{c}]=\beta
.

Halting.

Conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
, suppose
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
at some step
t
. Each of the
N_{t}
independent calls returns
+1
with probability
p^{\dagger}\cdot p_{t}\geq(\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}p^{*}
. The probability that all
N_{t}
calls return
-1
is

(1-p^{\dagger}\cdot p_{t})^{N_{t}}\;\leq\;(1-(\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}p^{*})^{N_{t}}\;\leq\;\exp(-N_{t}(\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}p^{*})\;\leq\;\frac{\beta}{2},

where the last inequality uses
N_{t}\geq\ln(2/\beta)/((\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}p^{*})
. Since we only need to detect the first instance of
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
, the probability of a Type II error at any step is at most
\beta/2
, conditioned on
\mathcal{G}
.

Continuation.

Suppose
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}_{t}
at step
t
. Since
x\mapsto 1-(1-x)^{N_{t}}
is non-decreasing on
[0,1]
and
p^{\dagger}\leq(1-\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}
, the probability that at least one of the
N_{t}
calls returns
+1
satisfies,

1-(1-p^{\dagger}\cdot p_{t})^{N_{t}}\;\leq\;p^{\dagger}\,N_{t}\,p_{t}\;\leq\;(1-\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}\,N_{t}\,\bar{p}_{t},

where the first inequality uses
1-(1-x)^{N}\leq Nx
for
x\in[0,1]
. Summing over all
t\geq 1
, we require

\sum_{t\geq 1}\left(1-{\frac{\beta}{2}}\right)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}\,N_{t}\,\bar{p}_{t}\;\leq\;{\frac{\beta}{2}}.

Defining
\bar{p}_{t}=\tfrac{\beta_{t}}{2N_{t}(1-\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}}
, since
\sum_{t\geq 1}\beta_{t}/2=\beta/2
, this bound holds. Setting the value of
N_{t}
,

\bar{p}_{t}\;=\;\frac{\beta_{t}}{2\left\lceil\frac{\ln(2/\beta)}{(p^{*}(\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}})}\right\rceil\left(1-\frac{\beta}{2}\right)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}}\;\leq\;\frac{p^{*}\beta_{t}(\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}}{2\ln(2/\beta)(1-\beta/2)^{1/{\theta_{C}}}}.

Failure probability.

The total failure probability is at most

\underbrace{\Pr[\mathcal{G}^{c}]}_{\leq\,\beta}\;+\;\underbrace{\Pr[\text{Type\penalty\ II error}\mid\mathcal{G}]}_{\leq\,\beta/2}\;+\;\underbrace{\Pr[\text{Type\penalty\ I error}\mid\mathcal{G}]}_{\leq\,\beta/2}\;\leq\;2\beta.

This completes the proof of
48
.
∎

Remark 49

(Approximate DP inputs)
.

48
assumes pure
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP input mechanisms. When the input mechanisms are
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta)
-DP,
(
4
, Theorem 2)
gives
((2+{\theta_{C}})\varepsilon_{1},\sum_{i}\delta_{i})
-DP, where the sum is over all calls to
Test
. Since
Algorithm

4
makes as many as
\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}
calls over
T
inputs, this yields a total
\delta
-cost of up to
\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}\delta_{t}
, which scales linearly in both the number of trials per step and the stream length.
4
also provide a Rényi DP analysis
(
4
, Theorem 2)
to obtain tighter approximate DP guarantees via conversion, at the cost of a more involved privacy accounting.

Both the linear
\delta
-accumulation and the need for Rényi DP analysis can be avoided by applying the purification technique of
24
. Given
(\varepsilon_{1},\delta)
-DP mechanisms, each evaluation is passed through a binary symmetric channel with crossover probability
\phi=\delta/(e^{\varepsilon_{1}}-1+2\delta)\leq\delta/\varepsilon_{1}
, producing a pure
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP mechanism. The acceptance and rejection thresholds are then perturbed by at most
\delta/\varepsilon_{1}
. This approach applies to the mechanism of Cohen et al.
4
as well as to our
Algorithm

1
, and yields pure DP guarantees for the output in both cases, with no
\delta
-accumulation across calls.

Lemma 50

(False positive accumulation under shared randomness)
.

Let
M\geq 1
,
q_{0}\in(0,1]
, and
q>0
. Let
\Omega
be a probability space and
E\subseteq\Omega
an event with
\Pr[E]\geq q_{0}
. Suppose that for every outcome
\omega\in E
, conditioned on
\omega
, at least
M
conditionally independent trials are observed, each succeeding with conditional probability at least
q
. Then the probability that at least one trial succeeds is at least

q_{0}\left(1-e^{-Mq}\right).

In particular, if this probability is at most
\beta
for some
\beta<q_{0}
, then

q\;\leq\;\frac{1}{M}\ln\frac{1}{1-\beta/q_{0}}.

For
\beta\leq q_{0}/2
, this gives
q\leq 2\beta/(Mq_{0})
.

Proof.

For every
\omega\in E
, the probability that all
M
trials fail is at most
(1-q)^{M}\leq e^{-Mq}
. Averaging over
E
,
\Pr[\text{all fail}\mid E]\leq e^{-Mq}
, so
\Pr[\text{at least one succeeds}\mid E]\geq 1-e^{-Mq}
. Marginalizing, the unconditional probability of at least one success is at least
q_{0}(1-e^{-Mq})
. Setting this at most
\beta
and rearranging,

1-e^{-Mq}\leq\frac{\beta}{q_{0}}\;\Rightarrow\;Mq\leq\log\frac{1}{1-\beta/q_{0}}.

Dividing both sides by M in the latter inequality yields the stated bound. For
\beta\leq q_{0}/2
, we have
\beta/q_{0}\leq 1/2
, and the standard inequality
\ln(1/(1-x))\leq 2x
for
x\in[0,1/2]
gives
q\leq 2\beta/(Mq_{0})
.
∎

Corollary 51

(Error lower bound of
algorithm

4
)
.

Fix
\beta\in(0,1/4)
,
T\geq 1
,
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, and
\varepsilon>2\varepsilon_{1}
. Write
\theta_{C}=\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{1}-2>0
for the parameter of the mechanism of
4
. Consider their testing mechanism applied to
T
mechanisms, each
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP with range
\{-1,+1\}
, success probabilities
p_{t}
, and evaluated
N_{t}
times at step
t
.

If there exists
\bar{p}>0
such that for every instance with
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}
for all
t\in[T]
, the probability that the algorithm halts is at most
\beta
, then

\bar{p}\;\leq\;\frac{4\beta\cdot 2^{1/\theta_{C}}}{\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}}.

In particular, for any fixed
\theta_{C}>0
,
\bar{p}=O\!\left(\beta/\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}\right)
.

Proof.

The mechanism draws
p^{\dagger}\in[0,1]
with
\Pr[p^{\dagger}\leq x]=x^{\theta_{C}}
. Define
E=\{p^{\dagger}\geq(1/2)^{1/\theta_{C}}\}
, which has probability
q_{0}=1-((1/2)^{1/\theta_{C}})^{\theta_{C}}=1/2
. At each step
t
, each of the
N_{t}
calls to
Test
independently draws
r\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p^{\dagger})
and evaluates
\mathcal{M}_{t}(X)
if
r=1
. For any fixed
p^{\dagger}\in E
, the
Test
calls are conditionally independent of each other, each producing
+1
with conditional probability
p^{\dagger}\cdot p_{t}\geq(1/2)^{1/\theta_{C}}\cdot\bar{p}
. The algorithm halts on any
+1
. Apply
50
with
M=\sum_{t=1}^{T}N_{t}
,
q=(1/2)^{1/\theta_{C}}\,\bar{p}
, and
q_{0}=1/2
. Since
\beta<1/4=q_{0}/2
,

(1/2)^{1/\theta_{C}}\,\bar{p}\;\leq\;\frac{4\beta}{M},

giving the claimed bound on
\bar{p}
.
∎

9.4.
Technical Overview of
13

Ghazi, Kamath, Knop, Kumar, Manurangsi, and Zhang
13
introduce two mechanisms which are relevant to our setting. They work in the generalized private selection setting and consider a stream of mechanisms generating outputs with scores in some arbitrary domain, and their objective is to find an output which achieves a user-defined threshold.

Their first mechanism is their generalized AboveThreshold mechanism with random dropping
(
13
, Algorithm 2)
. It is similar to the
Test
procedure of
4
, with a dropping probability
p^{\dagger}_{G}=e^{-\varepsilon_{i}\cdot k}
, where
k\sim\mathrm{Geo}(e^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
. For every mechanism in the input stream they first draw a value
y\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p^{\dagger}_{G})
, and only query the mechanism if this check passes. Then, if the score of the item output by the mechanism beats the given threshold, they output this item, along with the index of the mechanism that generated this output, in the clear. They show that this mechanism fulfills an
(2\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon^{\prime})
-ex post privacy guarantee.

Their guarantee is more general than that of
4
by allowing for varying input privacy parameter
\varepsilon_{i}
, and they also show that one can output the element that achieves the score which passes the threshold test. However, the core idea is similar - each access to
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is mediated by a dropping probability, and in fact the distribution from which this probability is drawn is very similar (we explain this next). Further, the privacy loss guarantee is similar; for
\varepsilon_{t}=\varepsilon_{1}
, their guarantee is exactly the
2\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon
guarantee achieved by
4
.

To reason about the dropping probability distribution, we see that for
k\sim\mathrm{Geo}(e^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
and any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Pr[k\geq j]=e^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}j}
. It follows that the
1-\beta
-probability upper bound on
k
is
\approx\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\log 1/\beta
. It follows that the
(1-x)
probability lower bound on the dropping probability
p^{\dagger}_{G}
equals
\exp(-\varepsilon_{i}\cdot\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\log 1/x)\approx\beta^{\varepsilon_{i}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}
. In other words,
\Pr[p^{\dagger}<x]\approx x^{\varepsilon_{i}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}
. This is essentially the same distribution from which the dropping probability
p^{\dagger}
of
4
is drawn, and it follows that similar upper and lower bounds in terms of accuracy for generalized private testing follow.

13
introduce another algorithm called the Hyperparameter Tuning Mechanism with Random Dropping (Algorithm 3 in their paper); we recall it here for reference in our notation for ease of comprehension. The algorithm incurs output privacy loss
\varepsilon=2\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon^{\prime}
; we write
\theta_{G}=\varepsilon-2\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
to match notation with our discussion.

Input:

Mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{t}:\mathcal{X}\to\{-1,+1\}
, each
\varepsilon_{1}
-DP, for
t\in[T]
; dataset
X
.

Output:

(o,t)\in\{-1,+1\}\times[T]
or
\perp
.

S\leftarrow\{\perp\}
;

Draw
k\sim\mathrm{Geo}(e^{-\theta_{G}})
;

for

t=1,\ldots,T

do

Draw
y_{t}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(e^{-\varepsilon_{1}\cdot k})
;

//
random drop

if

y_{t}=1

then

o\leftarrow\mathcal{M}_{t}(X)
;

S\leftarrow S\cup\{(o,t)\}
;

end if

end for

return
element of
S
selected by a data-independent rule;

Algorithm 5

Maximum Selection with Random Dropping
13

They prove that their algorithm achieves the following accuracy guarantee:

Theorem 52

(
13
, restated)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, and let
\mathcal{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{M}_{d}
be mechanisms where
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is
\varepsilon_{i}
-DP with range
\mathcal{O}
. Define

T_{i}\;:=\;\left\lceil\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\frac{2}{\beta}\right)^{\!\varepsilon_{i}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}\ln\!\left(\frac{2}{\beta}\right)\right\rceil.

Run
Algorithm

5
with
\mathcal{E}=\mathrm{Geo}(e^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}})
, repeating each
\mathcal{M}_{i}
for
T_{i}
times in the input sequence. Then:

(1)

Privacy.
The mechanism is ex-post
\tilde{\varepsilon}
-DP with
\tilde{\varepsilon}(o,i)=2\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}(\perp)=0
.

(2)

Accuracy.
If there exists
o^{*}\in\mathcal{O}
and
i^{*}\in[d]
such that
\Pr[\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}(D)\geq o^{*}]\geq\alpha
, then the algorithm outputs an element at least as large as
(o^{*},0)
with probability at least
1-\beta
.

(3)

Sample complexity.
The number of evaluations of
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is at most
T_{i}=O\!\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\frac{2}{\beta}\right)^{\!\varepsilon_{i}/\varepsilon^{\prime}}\ln\!\left(\frac{2}{\beta}\right)\right)
.

This guarantee is very compelling, because unlike our upper and lower bounds for generalized private testing, it does not incur poor scaling with the length of the stream. However, we find that when we analyse their mechanism in terms of
testing
, as opposed to for
selection
, it incurs a similar accuracy loss of
\beta/T
. We formalize this via a toy example. Consider a setting where there are some
T
mechanisms;
G
of them are
good
, and have a probability
p^{*}
of generating good
+1
outputs, and the remaining
T-G
of them are
bad
, and only generate good
+1
outputs with probability
\bar{p}_{t}
. If we want a
1-\beta
probability guarantee that the mechanism whose index is output by the selection mechanism is not bad, then for any fixed choice of
\theta_{G}
, we will require
\bar{p}\approx\Theta(\beta/T)
if
G=o(T)
.

Corollary 53

(Error lower bound for
Algorithm

5
when used for testing)
.

Fix
\beta\in(0,1/2)
,
T\geq 2
,
p^{*}\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, and
\varepsilon>2\varepsilon_{1}
. Write
\theta_{G}=\varepsilon-2\varepsilon_{1}
and suppose
\beta\leq(1-e^{-\theta_{G}})/2
. Consider
Algorithm

5
applied to
T
mechanisms with range
\{-1,+1\}
, of which
G\in[T-2]
have success probability
p_{t}\geq p^{*}
and the remaining
T-G
have success probability
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}
. Suppose the selection rule is uniform random among all
(+1,t)
in
S
.

If the probability that the algorithm returns
(+1,t)
with
p_{t}\leq\bar{p}
is at most
\beta
, then

\bar{p}\;\leq\;\frac{2\beta(1+Gp^{*})}{(T-G)(1-e^{-\theta_{G}})}.

In particular, for any fixed
\varepsilon>2\varepsilon_{1}
,
\bar{p}=O(\beta(1+Gp^{*})/(T-G))
.

Proof.

The algorithm draws
k\sim\mathrm{Geo}(e^{-\theta_{G}})
. Conditioned on
k=0
, which has probability
1-e^{-\theta_{G}}
, every Bernoulli gate passes, so all
T
mechanisms are evaluated independently. Fix a bad configuration
t_{0}
with
p_{t_{0}}\leq\bar{p}
. Under uniform random selection among all
(+1,t)
in
S
,

\Pr[t_{0}\text{ selected}\mid k=0]\;=\;\bar{p}\cdot\Ex\!\left[\frac{1}{1+N_{-t_{0}}}\right],

where
N_{-t_{0}}
is the number of
+1
’s from the remaining
T-1
configurations, independent of
t_{0}
’s output. Since
x\mapsto 1/(1+x)
is convex, Jensen’s inequality gives

\Ex\!\left[\frac{1}{1+N_{-t_{0}}}\right]\;\geq\;\frac{1}{1+\Ex[N_{-t_{0}}]}\;=\;\frac{1}{1+(T-G-1)\bar{p}+Gp^{*}}.

The events
\{t_{0}\text{ selected}\}
are disjoint over distinct bad configurations
t_{0}
, so summing over all
T-G
bad configurations,

\Pr[\text{bad selected}\mid k=0]\;\geq\;\frac{(T-G)\bar{p}}{1+(T-G-1)\bar{p}+Gp^{*}}.

Incorporating
\Pr[k=0]=1-e^{-\theta_{G}}
and setting the result at most
\beta
:

(1-e^{-\theta_{G}})\cdot\frac{(T-G)\bar{p}}{1+(T-G-1)\bar{p}+Gp^{*}}\;\leq\;\beta.

Rearranging:
(1-e^{-\theta_{G}})(T-G)\bar{p}\leq\beta(1+(T-G-1)\bar{p}+Gp^{*})=\beta+\beta(T-G)\bar{p}-\beta\bar{p}+\beta Gp^{*}
. Moving the
\beta(T-G)\bar{p}
term to the left:

(1-e^{-\theta_{G}}-\beta)(T-G)\bar{p}\;\leq\;\beta(1+Gp^{*}-\bar{p})\;\leq\;\beta(1+Gp^{*}).

Since
\beta\leq(1-e^{-\theta_{G}})/2
, the factor
1-e^{-\theta_{G}}-\beta\geq(1-e^{-\theta_{G}})/2>0
, giving

\bar{p}\;\leq\;\frac{2\beta(1+Gp^{*})}{(T-G)(1-e^{-\theta_{G}})}.

Since
1-e^{-\theta_{G}}
is a positive constant depending only on the privacy parameters,
\bar{p}=O(\beta(1+Gp^{*})/(T-G))
for any fixed
\varepsilon>2\varepsilon_{1}
.
∎
