Appendix A
Concentration Inequalities

Lemma 54

(Poisson Chernoff bounds)
.

Let
M\sim\Po(\lambda)
with
\lambda>0
. For any
u\geq 0
, the following tail bounds hold:

(1)

Upper tail:

\Pr[M\geq\lambda+u]\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2(\lambda+u)}\right)

(2)

Lower tail:

\Pr[M\leq\lambda-u]\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2\lambda}\right)

Proof.

Let
M\sim\Po(\lambda)
. We first derive the moment generating function (MGF). Using the probability mass function
\Pr[M=k]=e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}
, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
:

\Ex[e^{tM}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{tk}e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}=e^{-\lambda}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\lambda e^{t})^{k}}{k!}=e^{-\lambda}e^{\lambda e^{t}}=\exp\!\big(\lambda(e^{t}-1)\big).

Upper tail.

For any
t>0
, we apply Markov’s inequality to the monotonically increasing function
e^{tM}
:

\Pr[M\geq\lambda+u]=\Pr[e^{tM}\geq e^{t(\lambda+u)}]\leq\frac{\Ex[e^{tM}]}{e^{t(\lambda+u)}}=\exp\!\big(\lambda(e^{t}-1)-t(\lambda+u)\big).

To minimize the right-hand side, we differentiate the exponent with respect to
t
and set it to zero, which yields
t=\ln(1+u/\lambda)
. Note that
t>0
. Substituting this optimal
t
back into the exponent gives:

\Pr[M\geq\lambda+u]\leq\exp\!\left(\lambda\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}-\left(1+\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\ln\!\left(1+\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\right)\right).

Let
x=u/\lambda\geq 0
, and define
h(x)=(1+x)\ln(1+x)-x
. We claim that
h(x)\geq\frac{x^{2}}{2(1+x)}
.
Let
f(x)=h(x)-\frac{x^{2}}{2(1+x)}
. We have
f(0)=0
. The first derivative is
f^{\prime}(x)=\ln(1+x)-\frac{x^{2}+2x}{2(1+x)^{2}}
, with
f^{\prime}(0)=0
. The second derivative simplifies to
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{1+x}-\frac{1}{(1+x)^{3}}=\frac{(1+x)^{2}-1}{(1+x)^{3}}=\frac{x^{2}+2x}{(1+x)^{3}}
.
Since
f^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\geq 0
,
f^{\prime}(x)
is non-decreasing, implying
f^{\prime}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\geq 0
, which in turn implies
f(x)\geq 0
for all
x\geq 0
.
Thus, the inequality
h(x)\geq\frac{x^{2}}{2(1+x)}
holds for all
x\geq 0
. Substituting
x=u/\lambda
into this bound yields:

\frac{u}{\lambda}-\left(1+\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\ln\!\left(1+\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\leq-\frac{(u/\lambda)^{2}}{2(1+u/\lambda)}=-\frac{u^{2}}{2\lambda(\lambda+u)}.

Multiplying by
\lambda
and exponentiating gives the desired upper bound:

\Pr[M\geq\lambda+u]\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2(\lambda+u)}\right).

Lower tail.

For the lower tail, let
0\leq u\leq\lambda
. (For
u>\lambda
, the probability is exactly
0
and the bound trivially holds). For any
t<0
, we apply Markov’s inequality, which yields:

\Pr[M\leq\lambda-u]=\Pr[e^{tM}\geq e^{t(\lambda-u)}]\leq\frac{\Ex[e^{tM}]}{e^{t(\lambda-u)}}=\exp\!\big(\lambda(e^{t}-1)-t(\lambda-u)\big).

Minimizing this over
t<0
yields the optimal choice
t=\ln(1-u/\lambda)
. Substituting this into the exponent gives:

\Pr[M\leq\lambda-u]\leq\exp\!\left(\lambda\left(-\frac{u}{\lambda}-\left(1-\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\ln\!\left(1-\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\right)\right).

Let
y=u/\lambda\in[0,1)
, and define
g(y)=-y-(1-y)\ln(1-y)
. Using the Taylor series expansion for
\ln(1-y)=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{y^{k}}{k}
, we have:

g(y)=-y+(1-y)\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{y^{k}}{k}=-y+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{y^{k}}{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{y^{k+1}}{k}=-y+y+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}y^{k}\left(\frac{1}{k}-\frac{1}{k-1}\right)=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{-y^{k}}{k(k-1)}.

Since every term in the series is strictly negative, we can bound the sum by taking just the first term (
k=2
), yielding
g(y)\leq-\frac{y^{2}}{2}
.
Substituting
y=u/\lambda
gives:

-\frac{u}{\lambda}-\left(1-\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\ln\!\left(1-\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\leq-\frac{u^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}}.

Multiplying by
\lambda
and exponentiating gives the desired lower bound:

\Pr[M\leq\lambda-u]\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2\lambda}\right).

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 55

(Poisson Chernoff bounds – Multiplicative Form)
.

Let
M\sim\Po(\lambda)
with
\lambda>0
. For any
c>0
, the following tail bounds hold:

(1)

Upper tail:
If
c>\lambda
,
\Pr[M\geq c]\leq\frac{e^{-\lambda}(e\lambda)^{c}}{c^{c}}
.

(2)

Lower tail:
If
c<\lambda
,
\Pr[M\leq c]\leq\frac{e^{-\lambda}(e\lambda)^{c}}{c^{c}}
.

Proof.

We use the moment generating function of the Poisson distribution, which is
\mathbb{E}[e^{tM}]=\exp\!\big(\lambda(e^{t}-1)\big)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Upper tail.

Assume
c>\lambda
. By Markov’s inequality, for any
t>0
, we have:

\Pr[M\geq c]=\Pr[e^{tM}\geq e^{tc}]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[e^{tM}]}{e^{tc}}=\exp\!\big(\lambda(e^{t}-1)-tc\big).

We minimize the right-hand side by differentiating the exponent with respect to
t
and setting it to zero:

\lambda e^{t}-c=0\implies e^{t}=\frac{c}{\lambda}.

Since we assumed
c>\lambda
, the optimal parameter is
t=\ln(c/\lambda)>0
, making it valid for the right-tail Markov application. Substituting
e^{t}=c/\lambda
and
t=\ln(c/\lambda)
into the bound yields:

\Pr[M\geq c]\leq\exp\!\left(\lambda\left(\frac{c}{\lambda}-1\right)-c\ln\left(\frac{c}{\lambda}\right)\right)=\exp\!\left(c-\lambda-c\ln\left(\frac{c}{\lambda}\right)\right).

Using the properties of exponents, we can rewrite this exactly as:

\Pr[M\geq c]\leq e^{-\lambda}e^{c}\left(\frac{\lambda}{c}\right)^{c}=\frac{e^{-\lambda}(e\lambda)^{c}}{c^{c}}.

Lower tail.

Assume
c<\lambda
. For any
t<0
, Markov’s inequality yields:

\Pr[M\leq c]=\Pr[e^{tM}\geq e^{tc}]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[e^{tM}]}{e^{tc}}=\exp\!\big(\lambda(e^{t}-1)-tc\big).

Minimizing this over
t<0
yields
t=\ln(c/\lambda)
and optimal value
e^{t}=c/\lambda
. Because
c<\lambda
, the parameter
t=\ln(c/\lambda)<0
, which is valid for the left-tail application. Substituting this
t
into the bound yields the exact same algebraic sequence:

\Pr[M\leq c]\leq\exp\!\left(c-\lambda-c\ln\left(\frac{c}{\lambda}\right)\right)=\frac{e^{-\lambda}(e\lambda)^{c}}{c^{c}}.

This completes the proof.
∎
